LORD WOLMER'S STRONG LANGUAGE. SHEER FORCE, BLOOD-BOILING INJUSTICE AND HOLLOW MOCKERY. Lord Wolmer,M.P., addressed a political meeting at Go'borne, Newton-le-Willows, on Saturday evening. He said the Government's method of getting the Welsh Disestablishruent and Home Rule bills passed into law was not by means of reasoned argument, of attentive debate, or of compromise and persuasion, but by sheer force and by the most unscrupulous use of the gag A more monstrous, a more contemptible, a more wretched bill than the Welsh Disestablishment Bill WBS never introduced into the House of Commons. It made his blood boil, because he was certain that, if the people were consulted, the vast majority—Churchmen and Non- conformists alike—would say that on no account would they have any such measure. On the previous day the Prime Minister introduced a resolution that the com- mittee stage of the bill should be got through in four teen days. That meant ninety-one hours' discussion to put an end to an establishment and endowment that had existed for more than 800 years. Ft was a hollow mockery and a farce. The idea of a free House of Commons and a great deliberative assembly was as much of a myth and an old fable as tke stories of King Arthur and the Round Table. The Home Rule Bill was being forced into law under the rules of the same gag.

THE WHITE SCOURGE.

THE INSURANCE ACT CAMPAIGN AGAINST CONSUMPTION.

Interesting Local Conference.

A feature of the National Insurance Act which social workers have united in acclaiming is the great national campaign agaiast consumption which it makes possible. In order that the working of the sanatorium provisions of the measure might be fully understood locally, the Citizens' Guild of Help convened a conference of members of the Insurance Committee and health authorities, in conjunction with the Guild workers,<Yud this interesting meeting took place on Tuesday even- ing n the girls' dining room at the works of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sous Ltd. Mrs Haslam, of Bolton, opened the discussion with an instructive address' outlining the powers which the Act confers upon Insurance Committees and giving information on difficult points. Mr B S Townroe presided over a representative attendance. Amongst those who sent apologies for non-attendance were the Mayor and all the members of the Health Committee, who were detained at a meeting. Mr. Harold Smith, M.P , wrote regretting that Parliamentary duties prevented his attendance. He continued : 1 am very pleased that you are bringing before the people of Warrington the subject of tuberculosis and the methods of treating it. I imagine that few people realise how heavy a toll of human life is taken by this disease. lam informed that nearly 1,200 people in Liverpool alone die from it. This is appalling, and must command the sympathy and atten- tion of all classes. I believe that housing reform is the first thing to tackle, altd I learn from those who have studied the question in Liverpool, and particularly from the Chairman of the Housing Committee, that tilts awful death-roll wodld be double were it not for the splendid housing work of the Liverpool C oporation. The CHAIRMAN sold they were met its people who recognised that in the Insurance Act there WaS a genuine effort to grapple with the problem of tuberculosis, and that the latitude allowed to local committees mide an appeal to all citizens to help the Act on. the elasticity which was provided in the tuberculosis clauses gave them great hope that the Act would assist them in putting down the great white scourge. Mrs HAsuot observed that owing to several causes their horizon under the Insurance Act in relation to consumption was by no means as cloudless as they had expected it to be by that time. After explaining what legislation bad hitherto done for tuberculosis, she said that Town Councils could have done much to combat the disease with the powers they possessed before the passing of the Insurance Act. Poor Law cases were always more hopeless than others, because destitution was the primary cause as wages being low for unskilled workmen, there was nothing left for the emergencies of illness. A serious defect of the Insurance Act seemed to be that the poorest would get no benefit ; those whose stages were below Is 61 per day did not insure, arid therefore tor the large class of sweated labour the Act brought no relief. Having explained the more important provisions of the Act affecting tuberculosis, Mrs Haalam said a scheme for dealing with tuberculosis, introduced by Dr Philip, if Edin- burgh, and which which had attracted much notice on the Continent, and latterly in Lohdon, and elsewhere in England, mist of the London boroughs having adopted it, had been working with encouraging results. The dis- pensary signified the ctmtre point or pivot rOlirld which were ranged home advice and treatioent for the cases known as " contacts," sanatoria tor early cases, son other institutions for hopeless ones, farm colonies with graduated labour, open air schools, Sce. So lan as was known the machtnery which was ready for July 15th when the Insurance Act came into force, and the sana- torium benefit. was to begin immediately,. hut ordinary medical benefit not until January next. 'When MR. LLOYD GEORGE MADE HIS AMAZING AND MAGNIFICENT SPEECH

on introducing the Insurance Act, he carried with him the sympathy or all social workers who had seen the hold this compleint had osi our ant-king population She called the speech amazing because it was the first time the welfare of our people was rased to such importance, and she called it magnificent because no one who read carefully through the newspaper reports but saw opening out, a vista of great possibilities which the administration of the Act would render possible. When she looked back on the general feeling of rejoic- ing into which party feeling had not then entered it was with sadness that they perceived how they stood to-day, for the details of the Act, so far as they were understood, made it difficult to see the goal at which she trusted they would eventually arrive. The UNFORTUNATE DIFFICULTIES WITH THE DOCTORS seemed as if they would mar what might have been, with necessary amendments and improvements, a great boon to the working claeses. She did not touch upon any political asrect of the question—the issues were too great for the stnallnesses of party to have permanent weight—butfrom a long Poor Law experience she considered Oat contract practice was not the best conceivable at any time, and it was a matter Of regret that the principle was to be extended to the whole of the insured population. The right of each person to choose his own doctor ought to be within the reach of poor as well as rich, and she had urged that surely it was not beyond the limits of possibility that a scale of charges could be drawn up between the local doctors and the Board of Guardians allowing that freedom to Poor Law patients under the limits of safe expenditure. She ventured to hope that the difficulty with the doctors would be soon bridged over, because until that was done the benefit portion of the Act remained in a state of chaos. Many and varied duties fell upon the local Health Committees under the Act, and they would have to he dealt with in a large and generous spirit if success was to be ultimately obtained. Referring to the constitution of the committees, Mrs Haslam said it seemed a pity that so large a proportion of representa- tives was given to the friendly societies or, in other words, insured persons. It would be fairer to ' allow some representatives of the employers of labour who paid large contributions through the great firms, and also there ought to be room to admit en element that would not be personally interested. The committees were not wide enough in their choice and no seat was lr.ld by any man with the larger knowledge and wisdom that could come of well-spent leisure. It had been announced that consumption cases could be dealt with immediately, but for those localities which had not already working schemes in order that was difficult, and the hiring of beds at a distant sanatorium could only be regarded as a stop-gap or temporary expedient. The administering of sanatorium benefits was in confusion, and because ot the vacillating policy of the last few months it was difficult to adjust matters. The constantly changing policy of the Commissioners made things difficult. It was also laid down in the Act that is. 3d. was to be given by the Treasury for each insured person (the money set aside by the Finance Act) for the provision of sanatorium benefit. A month ago that was reduced to fid., fid, being taken to add to the doctors' fee. Therefore, an authority which reckoned 40,000 insured persons, instead of having £2,500 for sanatorium benefits, would only have £1,500, and those who knew anything about the expense of sanitoria, even the cheapest, could see that the sum would not be adequate along with other expenses, to say nothing of dispensary treatment or operations for tubercle elsewhere than in the lung. It seemed to many people also that the out- cry about sanitoria, or institutional treatment, was not very sound. Only last September Sir J Crighton Browne drew attention to the fact that during the last half century without sanitoria, the phthisical mortality had fallen neerly GO per cent, and that fall was due to better sanitation and if it could be maintained, phthisis and the need for sanatorium treatmentlwould disappear Not by diagnosing and legislating for one illness alone but by imrnovisia HYGIENIC CONDITIONS ALL ROUND

did they raise the level of health of our town and make infectious illness, VI h e n it did come our way.impossible of long continuance. Stress was laid on the advisa- bility of snaking schemes which might be correlated with the activities of various authorities and persons concerned, so as to ensure that adequate provision be made on a scientific basis for the prevention and treat- ment of disease. It was their duty to gather up all the threads of public hctivity in their respective districts until they formed a rope strong enough to bridge over all petty jealousies, and to bold them together until nubile opinion should force them forward towards the future of all-round better health conditions for the whole of the population. (Applause). In the course of the discussion special emphasis was laid on the necessity of after-case of patients who had been "patched up ' in sanstoria and often found it difficult to obtain employment. In this connection Mr C hi PARKER said it was desirable that suitable ve work should be found for those who were undergoing fsoantmatoarniiiimoaptoretts,tnmt pat o treatment. It twnoeuloclu,tehebboetli,:oudld, not only save the patient from the demoralization which continued inactivity was apt to produce. One valuable side of sanatorium treatment was that those who underwent it might afterwards act as missionaries in their own locality and spread the knowledke of the principles of health and sanitation. Mr F A FROST said the scheme under the Act was to treat the disease after it had been acquired. Could raising the stamina of youth by open-air not something be done to prevent the disease becoming apparent, by thiesyn werealli lines. He Hider wa and treatment, thus fitting them either to shake off the disease or resist it altogether.lf theofGoevoeurnsametan:eeenstyptopwwesioo:numilliddye itwasels'o raised the question. not watetnetottioonmtnontyhaetxpaespnesicyteosfatnhaetoluine.stion Mr NV J RowLallo (secretary of the Guild of Help) said the great factors in prevention emtmtepnetr afewedaginegq. I of how the wages were spent, and the 1. y anew what a question, buttt e r ueTathieonl would do some avasmotnagmtotteustpooof trwwiistehextpeognadtditutroe tfheeerdei 'lg. Sanatoria tweontlsilyd enfloetotwivoerkstmotikra,cloens educational convinced, however, that they would never get effective curative work through sanatoria, until they had some

Mr J S HILL, a member of the local Health Com- mittee under the Insurance Act, in seconding, said that. only 050 was allowed to Warrington under the sana- torium benefit scheme, and before the benefits came in they ciere already committed to an expenditure of £lbo. A sum of £350 was like a mere drop in a bucket Urgent cases were waiting, but could not be taken, because there was no room. They were paying 31s per patient per week, and that was the cost in Warrington of sanatorium treatment. As the hospital got larger the cost of administration would work out at less per capita. Other towns weredoing it for less. They had been able to agree with the doctors for the present so far as the administration of the sanatorium benefits was concerned. The terms arrived at were not cow raot terms. Mr Lloyd George had,however stated that in his new scheme for the payment of doctors sanatorium work would be inclusive. At present it was not.

, The resolution was unanimously agreed to.

- Mr C E PARICHR, in proposing a vote of thanks to Mrs Haslain, expressed some satisfaction that the death rate from tuberculosis had been steadily reduced. So much light was now being thrown on the subject that we could with some certainty look forward to the prospect that in twenty or thirty years the disease would have shrunk to the small proportions of small pox and the plague. Mr FROST seconded and the vote was heartily passed.

systematic efforts to deal with after-care. In this con- nection he pleaded for a sympathetic attitude on the part of employers.

Mr. J. MOORE MURRAY (director of education)! emphasised the necessity for care of a family whilst the father was in a sanatorium, Bo that they should not suffer by the sudden stoppage of income. If the standard of living were lowered, it created conditions favourable for the development of the disease in the children. They could go a long way towards prevent- ing consumption if they could start with all their schools and homes new. It was a tremendous problem, but the last five years had seen more progress in school planning, with a view to maintaining the health of the children, than in any period since 1870. They were not very rapid in Warrington in these matters, but they hoped soon to have a school where the fresh air facilities would be everything that could he desired. Parents had, however, complained that they would not send their children to school because the teachers would persist in keeping the windows open. The Rev A Al MITCHELL said it seemed to him that the sanatoria were not in any wise an absolute necessity. He did not for one moment wish to depreciate what the Chancellor had done or proposed to do, bat he thoueht that other methods might have been resorted to, and that the money which was proposed to be applied to sanatoria might be more fitly used in other directions. The whole secret of the solution of the problem day in the fact that prevention was better than cure. They had to set themselves, not to the erection of sanatoria for cases which were far advanced—and under the Poor Law they only got cases which were very far gone and seemed to be hopeless as regarded benefit and ultimate recovery—but to see to the housink conditions. He was very much stru.k with the fact that Warrington was still more or less

===== 0002601_19121116_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
NOTORIOUS FOR ITS SLUMS

and the town was aka, be supposed, greatly afflicted with tuberculosis. 1 hey must not be satisfied until they had swept the slums away. The rev gentleman also advocated a more rigid inspecticn of the meat which was offered for sale and which often,he believed, gave rise to the disease. Alluding to the diet question he said those who indulged in alcohol were the persons who were the most productive of tuberculosis and he believed that those who were engaged in the drink trade in public-houses were very largely victims of the disease. Tuberculosis was chiefly prevalent under conditions of poverty and they must therefore go to the root of the mischief. There was very little tie iu suggesting sanatorium treatment if the patients were afterwards to he sent back to the homes which had been productive of the disease. As far as Peor Law statisiics were concerned, they went to show that in almost all the cases which had ceen treated the patients had revived for n little time and then the last state had been worse than the first ; there had been au i utter collapse and finally death. He would like to have seen the menthe's of the Board of Guardians attending that conference in a body. He was only a junior member of the Board and the wiseacres who knew 53 much about the PJor LAW were conspicuous by their absence.

In reply to Mrs Simpson, Mrs lIAK.A.Iat stated that pstients could not be forced to go into hospital against their will. The next alternative was to try mud induce them to haven little shelter erected in their backyards. f t people chose to take ordinary precautions, it was not absolutely necessary to send them away. Mr J A RANSOM moved a resolutinn expressing the hope that a Care Committee would bo set up ii. War- rington for the bent& a tuberculosis patients and those dependent on them.

NEW GERMAN AMBASSADOR AND HIS WIFE.

Prince Liehnowsky, the new German Am- bassador to the English Court, arrived this week. The Prince is one of the Kaiser's most intimate friends. He is a very great and wealthy landowner, and belongs to one (il the most ancien and powerful of German s.ristocratie ',louses. His diplomatic ,experi-

ence is Glight; he was at one time in London as Attaché at the Embassy, and afterwards went to Constantinople and Bucharest as Secretary. Of middle age, the Prince has only been married eight years, and has a tfamily of two boys and a girl.

SAID HE WAS SINGLE.

WARRINGTON EVIDENCE IN A BIGAMY CHARGE. '

At Middlesbrough on Tuesday, a charge of bigamy was preferred against Ti 1011139 Edward Phillips (29), wire-drawer, of no fixed abode. Mr A fauriston appeared to prosecute, while Mr Albert E Forbes defended. . _

The facts as given by Mr Lauriston in his opening statement, were Ora the prisoner went through a form of marriage on February 10th, 1906, with a girl (Lily Hart), at St Barnabas' Church, Warrington. Subse- quently they lived together in Middlesbrough, and were together as lat 3 as May, 1910. Towards the end of that month be answered an advertisement and went to lodge at a house in Willow-street. There, there was a girl named Ada Bell, with whom he kept company, saying he was a single man, lie seduced her, and later promised to marry ber on learning that she would have to go into the Workhouse if he did not. He suggested That the marriage should take place at the Registrar's Office in Leeds, and she went there to give notice in August, 1911. The prisoner joined her, and on August 21st a form of marriage was gone through, the prisoner describing himself as a bachelor. Frederick Charles Wilkinson of Winwick-road, Warrington, gave evidence of the marriage with Hart at Warrington ; while Inspector Stewart, of the Warrington Police, deposed that two maintenance orders t-r desertion had been obt4ined against him by the woman Hart. The second of these was granted in March of the present year, and on each occasion the prisoner was present. tie did not then suggest that the woman was not his wife. A copy of the certificate of marriage between Thomas Edward Phillips and Lily Hart was produced, showing that the ceremony took place on February 10th, 190 b. Both witnesses identified Lily Hart, who was present in court. Ada Bell bore out the opening statement as far as her relations with Phillip' were concerned, and said that they lived together in Middlesbrough until March, liil2. Their child was born in October, 1911. Early in the present year in consequence of something she heard she asked him if he bad been married before, and he replied that she was his only wife, although be had formerly lived with a woman. She, however asked him to go away until all was made clear, and he did so. Later he sent her a note saying that the detectives were alter him because he was a married man, and they went to Warrington, witness staying with prisoner's mother. Prisoner was thereupon committed for trial at York Assizes.

He sometimes thought Scotchwen would be an inch nearer the Great White Throne than the Englishmen because of their insistence. the Scotchman had been educat- ing his people insisting on politicans that they should have a Licensing Bill and now by a majority of 157 they had had the third reading. The Scotchruen were to have the privi- lege—if the House of Lords did not interfere and one might devoutly hope that for once they would sleep—of saying whether theywould have public houses or not—they would have the privilege of local option. (1-lear,lisar.) It was an experience that was monotonous in its universality that wherever one travelled there was always a congested area of public houses. One had also had the opportunity to note that the congestion of the public houses wab just in the area where the poorest were the thickest and where the need of fresh air and freedom were so imperative. They demanded for England like tbe Scotchinan had for Scotland, the right to say where and when the liquour should be sold. Referring to the resolution the rev gentleman said he maintained that evil did strike down to the very roots of our national life because it struck down to the very root of the pelple's hope and life when it struck at the vitals of childhood and there was no more potent force. Those words were corroborated by men on the labour benches. There was no force so potent in the destruction of our child life as the influence of strong drink. (Hear, bear.) They should give the child of every parent the opportunity and privilege which they needed to become the citizens of the future. They were told that the streets of Liverpool were filled with Bulgarians singing their patriotic authemsond there was some chance that if the Englishmen were coming back from war they would be singing " Rule Brittania " with a hiccough If they Walked into Cheetham Hill, in Manchester they might see signs of squalor but they would not see in the cupboard of the Jewish mother the gin which blasted and ruined her offspring. Some- how, in English life there had been woven that craving for strong drink. Generation after generation had weakened the will of each succeeding one. Our com- mercialism, with its intense grip, had also helped to weaken, until now in every spot and place where degradation reigned supreme it seemed hopeless for the child. Even in regard to Warrington one was aware how INTER-RELAT,D WERE THE QUESTIoNS OF POVERTY AND DRINK.

He maintained that if we could close the doors of every public house in the town and keep them oloted for 10 years we could solve the slum problem of the town. (Hear, hear.) The child life was ruined by that trade. It was round the door of the gin palace that the child found its playground and the parents reeled drunk from its doors. It was a shame to us as a cation that we allowed the children to play round the doors of the publio house. There was another phase of the " trade's" work he would mention. Without a blush they told us their trade was their politics. They gave us to understand that they I did not mind using democratic institutions to advanca the interests of their own "trade." One marvelled at the wonderful work which had been done by the U.K.A. and kindred institutions against that mass of interests. The "trade" did not always turn from suborning the police. One was always ready and glad to pay a tribute to the police officers who saw to the welfare of the town, and patrolled the streets. He himself had so high a regard for them that he was one of those who urged that in the White Slave Traffic Bill the police should have the power to arrest on suspicion. Sheffield had,however,revealed to them how far the ramifications of the " trade" extended; what they were prepared to do and how much to encompass. The police should always be assured of the moral support of the best citizens of the town and also that they would have the backing of the best oortion of the town when they brought their cases before the magistrates who had not always a sympathetic ear. Think of the temptations in the way of 'a constable, troubled as some of them were with an unquenchable thirst. He brought his case before the magistrates, with scant support, and if he failed, be received a lecture from the chief constable, who did not always speak in honeyed phrases. He thought there were times when temperance wrrkers had watched the battle instead of being in it. He had seen times when they had had to watch every public- house during a general election. (Hear, hear.) Every public-house had been a committee room for that party which was pledged to support the drink interests. Surely it became temperance workers and the churches to fight with the best weapons they possessed. He had been in other Lancashire towns as well as Warrington, in greater towns, and in one which had been nick-named "rough." He had paraded the streets at Sunday noon

TEMPERANCE SUNDAY. IS WARRINGTON RULED BY WILDERSPOOL P

"I have beard it whispered that Wilderspool sits over this town" was the significant statement made by the Rev H Ogden in addressing a large number of Temperance workers on Sunday night in the Parr Hall. The meeting had been promoted by the Warrington Band of Hone Union in celebration of Temperance Sun- pay and, judging by the enthusiasm displayed; much good should ammo. Despite the strong forces arrayed against them, Temperance advocates have succeeded in influencing public opiuion to such an extent that their cause is no lonoer regarded with contemptuous derision but is daily commanding an increasing measure of support from thin ing men and women. The pre- sident of the Band of Hope Union, Mrs Jas Dodds, pre- sided over the meeting on Sunday and she was supported by a number of local clergy and Band of Hope workers. Absentees who bad sent apologies were Miss Bessie Dutton, the Revs John Yonge and 11 Middlewood. Mrs Dodds briefly opened the proceedings and moved the following resolution " That this meeting declares that delay in dealing effectively with the drink evil which strikes down to the very roots of our national life, is frought with danger and din ster to the State, and in full reliance upon the Prime Minister's repeated pledges, calls upon the Government to intro- duce and pass the promised Licensing Bill, including the power of local veto, in the Parliament Act of 1913." In a stirring and instructive address the Rev H OGDEN seconded the resolution. He said they were apt to lose sight of the very real menace and danger that the "trade " was to the prosperity, purity and holiness of the country. He thought sometimes that Englishmen had become so habituated to the sight of the drink evil that they were apt to lose sight of the peril that it was to the nation. They should not lose sight,of it, but keep it ever before them as the great enemy they had to tight. In regard to the great white slave question, the evil had been at our doors and the foul river had been fl 'wing on at our feet and its devastating influence had been felt throughout the nation. Yet it was only when the question was recalled vividly to our initds by the work of Mr W T Stead that we shook ourselves from the habit of seeing it and determined to st.fle it. He thought it was the same with the drink evil—that men and women saw it so

Tile team to go to St. Helens on Thursday has been provisionally selected as follows: Jolley, full back; Brooks, Tranter, A N Other, and Mclntyre, three- quarters ; Nicholas, Rushton or Douglas, half-backs ; Thomas, R Thomas, Cox, Skelhorne, Fearnley, Cheaters (or Arnold), forwards.

The vacancy at centre-threequarter may be filled by Hud.ou. The young Wiganer played a brilliant game at Swinton on Saturday and scored two dashing tries. If he acquits himself well to-day he will secure promotion.

Renwick is still an absentee, but hopes to be fit and ready in a fortnight. He has been to a specialist this week and got the injury to his shoulder rectified, and

all that it „ requires now is massage and rest. Brad- slut ,'s shoulder was found to be much worse, and he will probably have to undergo an operation this week- -end to set the displaced muscles right.

I learn that Waywell, who declined to play with the "A " team, has been placed on transfer. At one time this young threequarter looked like training on into a first-class man, but he has never fulfilled his early promise.

And now for the strenuous and sensational game of last Saturday with Swinton. The Blues are acknowledged to be a much-improved team and after the shock they administered to the locals at Waders- pool no one in this neighbourhood will despute the fact. They started oil as if they looked upon the Wire- pullers as very "small fry," and by cool, clever, and confident football they ran up a score of 12 poitts to nil at half-time. Fancy Warrington 12 points down at half lime on their own ground and against Swinton ! Shades of Jim Valentine ! The Blues seemed to have recovered their long-lost powers and .glory.

Warrington were certainly " full value" for their big deficit up to the interval. The whole team seemed to have got an attack of nerves simultaneously. The 'forwards packed badly and lost possession. But the backs ! Well! the ball might have been red-hot the way most of them attempted to handle it. The trouble began with Rushton who mulled everything Nicholas served up, and this spread to Dickinson and Brooks. Attempts at combination were such frightful failures that the spectators began to jeer. Even the defence Was wobbly, and. the tackling left a lot to be desired when Z• 3 wi n ton scored a couple of tries. Byder's three .goals were beauties, and he hit the post with a fourth shot. There is no doubt about it, ViTarriagton were a well-beaten side at half-time, and deservedly so. But they won the metal Yes; they wiped out those twelve formidable.points and scored 2 goals, 3 -tries (13 points)in the next thirty-five minutes. It was a great performance, capped by a wonderful 'finish that made the welkin ring. This is probably the

best performance the Wirepulle—rs have pui up for any years, and shows that there are possibilities in the team. The whole side showed a wonderful revival of form, dashing forward work being supplemented by .capital combination and strong finishing powers. But Warrriugton were lucky to win, and there was another reason apart from the improved display of the Wire- Pullers in the second half.

Swinton made the fatal mistake of easing up and adopting defensive tactics when they had got what 'certainly appeared to be a winning lead. Only a week or two ago I had to lecture Warrington for the same load policy, though in their ca,e there was even less Justification for such tactics. Anyhow, it had the same Fesult, and it always will have tidal result if persisted in for any length of time. The folly of it lies not in the futility of defence, but in the fact that such tactics encourage the other side. The losers have everything to gain, they see the once strong opponents sitting tight "at home," and they play with redoubled confi- dence and energy. Stvinton's change of front gave 1 , .Warrington exactly the tonic they required. The men found their feet, got an early score, and then carried , all before them. If Swinton had gone on with the same aggressive, skilful football they showed them- Selves capable of in theifirst half I do not see how they ' could have lost. In 'fact they would have had as good a chance of scoring again as Warrington had of wiping ' off any of the deficit. The Blues have lost two matches in succession by the odd point in the last five minutes. The cause should be painfully obvious by now!

1

Just another line for the concluding score by Tranter and Mclntyre. It waa a great effort by the former which enabled him to rob the full back at "centre, and a wonderful run by " Mac " which took him over under the posts. Mclntyre is a funny Player. nalf the game be will give you the impression that he is indifferent or asleep, and then he comes out with terrific effort which puts everything else in the Ithade. It was so on Satnrday, and also against ,Wigan. There is no doubt "Mao " has the speed in 'lira What lie requires is that eagerness which keeps Ulan "on his toes" from start to finish. He ought to be a much better player than he is !

-- I was favourably impressed with both the young Niles and the old Blues. Del Davies is still a great %Crum half and a good leader, while in Frodsham (full back), Ryder, Dawson. and Moores, the team has '.ol.nag players who have ideas and the ability to carry Mena out. A solid forward pack completes a team which is far in advance of any side Swinton have 'turned out for many years. 1 wish them well in their attempt to recover the lost prowess of the "

Warrington were weakest at ha' f back—the position 1 lvhich has caused endless trouble .0 the club manage- -ot, e bra long time past. Nicholas began well, but ne absolute failure of Rushton as a connecting link l'iith the threequarters seemed to have a bad effect ..I1 round. The ex-Halifax man was hopeless on "niturday, and his defence was no betier than his 4ttack. I would far soonerhave the rejected Creevey. e, at least, had some cleverness with the ball and ' nevi where the line lay. There will have to be a I'llange at half back, or the murmurs of the crowd will 11,nrrt to something worse. HERE'S TO HARRY ASHTON ! I learn, that the popular hon. secretary, Mr Harry Ashton, was married last week to Miss Hallwood, of Wilderspool, and has since been honeymooning in Paris. Every member of the Club, and supporter of the Rugby game in Warrington, will wish him the best of luck, for it would be impassible to exaggerate the value of his services both as player and official over a lengthy period. As he is the soul of geniality in addition, I am sure that many members will be anxious to join with the Committee in offering him some tangible token of their esteem and goodwill.

AUSTRALIAN TOUR DECLINED.

The General Comniiitee of the No-rdie—rn Rugby Union on Tuesday considered the invitations received from Australia and New Zealand to send out a team at the end of the present football season. After lengthy consideration it was resolved to decline the invitation on the ground that the terms of 50 per cent. offered by the New South Wales League are totally inadequate, and matters of agreement and detail which can only be arranged by correspondence are necessary to be settled before a further tour can be arranged.

The terms offered by the New Zealand League were quite satisfactory, but the tour, whenever made, must comprise both Dominions. The offer of New Zealand cannot ho dealt with until arrangements with New South Wales are also ratisfactory.

The Union have made it clear in the wording of their resolution—" Matters of agreement and detail, which can only be arranged by correspondence, are necessary to be settled before a further tour can be arranged '—that they do not altogether see eye to eye with the New South Wales League. This undoubtedly concerns the transfer law, which was so knowingly and wilfully broken by the colonial body in the case of Frawley. Then, too, 50 per cent, of the taltings is not tempting when it is conmdered that the previous touring side took 05 per cent, and the Australian tourists over here got 70 per cent.

A... 0 0 6 Widnes A 0 1 3 Wigan A Pemberton Wigan llighfleld... 0 021 Broughton R. A 2 0 4 A Remarkable Finish at Warrington. The much-improved Swinton team were the visitors to Warrington on Saturday and there was a crowd of fully 5,000 to welcome the Blues under the captaincy of Dai Davies. Both teams were well represented,and with the weather fine and dry, a good game was assured. The teams were : Warrington—Jolley, back ; Mclntyre, Tranter, Dickenson and Brookes, threequarter backs ' • Rushton and Nicholas, half-backs; G Thomas, R Thomas, Skelhorne, Cheaters, Cox and Fearnley, forwards. Swinton—Frodsham, back ; Brooks, Ryder, Dawson, and Moores, threequarter backs ; Dai Davies and Fair- hurst, half-hacks; Blears, Dan Davies, Smith, McVeigh, Price and Preston, forwards. Referee, Mr D Longbottom, Halifax ; touch-judges, Messrs E J Hannaforil , (St. Helens) and E Williams (Widnes). Swinton opened in aggressive fashion, and with the home team slow in settling down play was mostly in

===== 0002601_19121116_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
A CROSS-COUNTRY RUN.

the home quarter. Attempts at combination were a failure, chiefly due to the faulty handling of Rushton and Dickenson, and Swinton had no trouble in main- taining the upper hand. The game was only about five minutes old when a weak punt by R Thomas enabled Fairhurst to mark in good position, and Ryder kicked a goal. Brooks made a praiseworthy attempt to lead his men to the other end, but fumbling was the order of the day, and Swinton were soon back. A clever move by Davies and Dawson tricked the Warrington backs and Moores raced over at the corner with a clever try, from which Ryder hit the upright. Warrington now endeavoured to get a grip of the game, and the forwards rushed the ball to the other end, Frodshans making a brilliant response. , Nicholas cleared the Swinton halves nicely, but his pass to Rushton got no further than Dickenson, who was again at fault with an easy transfer and a good opening. The Swinton forwards next raided Jolley's charge, but the young full back met the attack confl- fidently. Swinton would not he denied, and Davies making a mark, Ryder kicked another fine goal from long range. Warrington seemed completely out of the hunt, but they rallied gamely, and Nicholas had a fruit- less shot at goal from the 25 line. At lest the backs got moving in s-mething like proper order,only for the effort to fail at Brooks,who made his first mistake when the line was insight. The epidemic of fumbling seemed to be going through the side, and it was no surprise to see Swinton race up again with smart play and Moores go over at the corner with another capital try. Ryder failed at goal, but the Blues turned round wits the com- mending lead r f 3 goals 2 tries (12 points) to nil. There was an earnest confat between the home players and officials during the interval, and when the teams turned out Nicholas and Rushton changed places. The home forwards began again with fiery zea),and Frodsham needed- all his cleverness to keep the Swinton line Intact. The Blues seemed to have made up a defensive plan of campaign with the result that Warrington were continually on the attack. Rushton and Nicholas got the ball away beautifully at centre,and Trainer charged through the Swinton backs grandly before passing to Mclntyre. The latter made a strong run down touch before throwing inside to Cox, who completed a beautiful bit of combination with a try in good position. Thomas added a goal amid hearty cheering. Following on this success Warrington apreared to fall away and the game fluctuated consider- ably and the visitors came near scoring when Moores was hauled up inches short of the line. Swinton marked on every oonceivable occasion and play dragged considerably. In fact there was scarcely anything of interest until the last ten minutes, and then came a brilliant run by Jolley, which had an inspiring effect on the" Wirepullets," for they made the raid complete on the Swinton line, and the ball coming out to Tranter the three-quarter rushed over for a try in a good position. Jolley made a mess of the goal kick. The score was now 8-12, and Swinton once more made a move to the other end, Dai Davies being largely instrumental in keeping Warrington in check. But with five minutes to go there came a sensational change over the scene. Dickenson punted out loftily from under the home posts and Tranter followed up at a tremendous pace. He reached Frodsham as soon as the ball and snapping it from the full back's grasp he threw to Mclntyre who out-paced the whole of the Swinton backs and snored under the bar after a movement the length of the field. One point now divided the teams and when Thomas carefully placed the winning goal there was tumultuous cheering all round the ground. Swinton made &despairing rally and Davies broke away, but Mclntyre saved in his own quarter and when the whistle sounded Warrington had won a remarkable game by 2 goals, 3 tries (13 points) to 3 goals 2 tries (12 points). ROCHDALE WELL BEATEN AT WIDNES.

At Widnes, before 2,000 spectators. Rochdale lost Dearden in the first quarter of an hour. and West was injured in the last quarter, and had to retire. The home side was superior both in the forward and back divisions. Some passing initiated by Redmond ended in a forward dribble, and Redmond scored, Jones easily improving. J O'Garra made a clever opening, and then gave to 'Reid, who crossed the line. Jones failed at the place. A third try was registered by Higgins, and at the interval Widnes led by 11 points to nil. In the second half Widnes had many chances, but they only managed to score one try, and this was to the credit of Daintith. The same player kicked a penalty goal, and Jones did likewise. Padden placed a goal for Rochdale and in the last minute the same player scored a try. Result :—Widnes 3 goals 4 tries (IS points), Rochdale Hornets 1 goal I try (5 points). Teams and officials : Widnes—Jones, back ; Daintitb, Redmond Lally and Law, three-quarter backs ' • Noon and J o;Gara, half- backs ; G Aspey, Cosgrove, Higgins, Reid, H 0 Gara and Lloyd, forwards. Rochdale Hornets—Baxter, back ; Paddon, Robinson, West and Hopwood, three-quarter backs; Taylor and Jones, half backs ; Morrisey, Roman, Turner Fitzsimmons, Deardon and Carter, forwards. Referee, C Platt, Swinton. Touch judges, J Entwistle, Wigan and A Jones, Warrington. RUNCORN OUTCLASSED AT OLDHAM. The Runcorn team were quite outclassed at Oldham, where the home team gained their first victory on that ground this season. Four minutes from the start a clever passing run sent Avery benind the posts, and Ferguson added goal. Oldham backs got going again and quickly added th/ tries, Lomas, Williams, and

Farnsworth scoring, Ferguson kicking a goal in each case. RUDCOM'S only points in the first half were from a penalty kick by Richardson. In the second half the Runcorn defence was pierced by Holbrook. Williams, Ferguson, and B D Dtvies, and Ferguson placed two goals. The good work of the Runcorn forwards was again of value. Richardson and Thornton scoring, and Richardson adding a goal. Other tries were scored for Oldham by Wharton, Holbrook, and Williams, and Ferguson added a goal. Resnit :—Oldham, 7 goals, ii tries (47 points) ; Runcorn, two goals 2 tries 10 points). THE COMBINATION.

WARRINGTON "A " BEAT SWINTON " A."—Warring- ton " A" team excelled themselves on Saturday when they beat the Blues second team at Swinton. Daintith took the place of J Brown, and the early stages of the game were in favour of the home side. Whitehead followed up a long kick smartly and scored at the corner, Worsley failing at goal. Shortly afterwards a mull by Evans gave the visitors a chance, and Hudson picking up in the loose scored near the posts. Critchley adding extra points. Midfield play followed, and A Brown, breaking away from the scrimmage, gave to Clare, the latter being pushed into touch when danger- ous. Good passing by the home teem was neatly stopped by Clare. The visiting forwards broke sway in determined fashion, and Daintith being placed ip possession forced his way over. Critchley failed at goal. Half-time :—Warrington "A " one goal two tries (eight points) ; Swinton "A," one try (three points).

On restarting both teams indulged in vigorous tactics and from a free kick against the home team Critchley landed a fine goal. Weak defensive play by the home team let in Eiudson, Critchley again adding the extra points. Shortly afterwards Hackett picking up scored a flue try for Swinton, Worsley goading. Result : Warrington " A," three goals three tries (15 points) ; Swinton " A," one goal two tries (eight points).

LANCASHIRE JUNIOR CUP. RXLANDS' RECS AND RUNCORN WHITE STAR

PLAY AGAIN.

- - Owing to the disturbance on the Rylands' ground at the close of the match the week before, the two con- testing teams were ordered to play again at Gorsey- lane last Saturday. On this occasion a league referee, Mr B Ennion, took charge, and the game was conducted in a proper spirit. The Runcorn men hardly showed the same determination as on the previous occasion and the home team were somewhat easy winners. Rylands' were the first to 'Attack and Leah,following on good work by the forwards, had a shot at goal whieh just failed. Jolley placed his side in a good position and had a try for a goal from a penalty, hut be failed. The Warringtonians showed superior form behind the pack and Hennessey had hard luck to be floored a yard or so from the line. Rylands' still continued bard aggressors and after another round of passing Griffiths made no mistake with the final trausfer,going over for a fine try. Once they had broken through the defence there was no holding the " Wiremen," and Leah further increased the lead with a dropped goal, whilst just before the interval a concerted movement gave Hennessey a chance of scoring at the corner. The goal kick failed. At the interval the home side led by one goal two tries (eight points) to nil. Resuming, the Runcorn men showed signs of weakness on the defence and play was mainly confined to their quarters. Jolley, the custodian, however, showed rare nerve at, the most critical situations. Tries were scored by Hennessey, Bramhall (full back) and Floyd, and Leah dropped a goal. Result :—Rylands' Recrea- tion, 23 points ; Runcorn White Star, nil.

TEAM FOR TO-DAY. THIRD ROUND LANCASHIRE JUNIOR CUP. RYLANDS V SHERDLEY ATHLETIC.—At Sherdley. Bramhall, full back ; Griffiths, Leah, Horrocks, Hennesey, three-quarters; Floyd, P Morris, half backs; Mason, Stretford, H Morris, Broadhurst, Langley and Slater, forwards.

'WEAVER REFINING CO. V. STOCKTON _HEATH.

_ At Acton Bridge, Vaughan was early called upon to clear from Smith, whilst at the other end Waller with a huge kick cleared from Evans and Beam. Returning again, the home team, through Horton, opened their account before the game was five minutes old. Straight from the re-start Stockton Heath applied pressure, and were rewarded, Lockhirt equalizing. Even play followed for a time, although each defence was tested only one more point came before the interval, Evans adding the second goal for Weaver. The second half was more in favour of the home team, Evans adding number three, whilst fourteen minutes before the close Sharpes further increased their lead. The game ended: —Weaver Relining Co., four; Stockton Heath, one.

In the second portion Csdishead Wesleyans had neatly all the game, and although Holmes, the Brigade custodian, brought off some smart clearances he could not save his side from a heavy defeat. Played on the ground of the former. The visitors won the toss and Mill-lane kicked off against the wind. Challinor soon got possession, hut Berry relieved. Give and take play followed, full time being reached with a draw.

the home team for they obtained a beautiful goal just before the whistle sounded for half time. On resuming Warrington showt•cl better football although they did not score again. There is one point which the Excelsiors ought to note, that is, when In trout of goal, shoot.

MOORE AND DARESBURY V. CROSFIELDS.

Moore and Daresbury were again victorious at home. The ground was in good condition and consequently the game was fairly fast. The visitors were outclassed from start to finish. In the first half goals were scored for the home team by T Wilkinson with M Cart- wright (2) and le Anti obus. After the interval the visitors had the wind in their favour but still failed to, find the home goalkeeper any work. In quick succes- sion goals were added for the parish team by A Hough- ton and Hde Prez (2). The whistle blew with the score. Moore and Daresbury 7 goals, Crossflelds nil . Moor and Daresbury (2nd team) gained a well earned victory over Walton on their own ground the score being 3 goals to nil.

HOCKEY.

LATCH FORD.

The First Eleven lost 4-1 against Kersal at Prest- wich. They had distinctly hard lines in dropping 4 goals (although Kemal were the best team) as three of the shots were the softest things imaginable. The visiting goalkeeper had en unfortunate afternoon, but goalkeepers are long-suffering individuals. Latchford had an equal share of the play in the first half but were behind 2-0 at half time. Afterwards Latchford kept up a strong attack and Pearson scored for them but Kersal scored two more. Southern, the Luticashire County back, played a fine game for tiers'sl and continually broke up the visiting forwards near the circle. Hull land Walker were also good at forward. For Latchford the halves were the best and they and the full-backs worked hard against some clever forwards and were in no way to blame for the defeat. The visiting forwards could make little impression on the Kersal defence. The Second Eleven had to visit Ashley First who won 10-1 at Latchford in September. The result of Saturday's game was 3-1 for Ashley, who are strong this season, having the assistsnce of some of the disbanded Altrincham Club players. The score was 1-1 until quite near time and Ashley rushed the defence twize and won unexpectedly. The re-appearance or Dixon made a big difference in Latchford's defence. Gibbs played soundly at centre half and Laver and Hohin made a capital right wing. Colton scored for the visitors by taking a centre from,tho right and giving the Ashley goalkeeper no chance. _

The First Eleven v Bootle at home to-day is Wright, goal ; W Fletcher, C Billington, backs ; F W Isherwood, J B k'lrilkinson. A Knight, halves; R A Dale, E Pearson, D Travis. E Brinks, A Starkey, forwards. Referee, Mr Cobb. CROSFIELD'S CLUB.

Crosfields met Urruston at Urnaston on Satnrday. The visitors were unfortunate in having three of their regular players absent and were further handicapped by the bad state of the ground. Urmstou were the first to score, which was the result of a good passing move- ment by the forward line ; this was followed up by two more goals. Crosfields retaliated after a fine dribble, A C Bloomer scoring a goal. In the second ball the visitors bad rather more of the play, but were unable to add to their score. Urmston, however, managed to penetrate Crosfield's defence once more, the result being 4-1 in favour of Urrnston.

Dispassionate people THE HONOUR who do not take the OF THE HOUSE. same keen interest in the political situation as do party men may be pardoned if they con- demn the disorderly proceedings in the House of Commons as displaying a lamentable dis- regard of the high traditions of that assembly Time was when English people were glad to be able to point proudly to the dignity and , orderliness of their own Parliament in contrast to the scene of riot and disturbance which frequently mark the deliberations of other national assemblies. The possibility of that proud boast is gone. When more than a year ago the Prime Minister was prevented from speaking by the continued cry of "Divide " there were forthcoming pretty sure evidences that the solid opinion of the country did not endorse this particular method of political controversy. Something worse than that has now happened. Ministers have been prevented from speaking, the appeals of the Chair for order have been disregarded, the Speaker has been defied, and the Chamber itself has been disgraced by a personal assault upon a Minister. How will these incidents be regarded by the country as a whole? We cannot but believe that the majority of the sober-minded citizens of the country will be shocked and displeased bythe demonstration of systematised disorder in the council of the nation. There is in the English people an underlying reverence and respect for authority which is one of its most price- less assets. Occasionally a section of the people has got out of hand and has violated that sense; but invariably such a departure has brought a revulsion of feeling strong enough to prevent permanent disturbance of the national mind. But when the elected re- presentatives of the people, in whom has been placed the supreme trust of sitting in the national assembly, set out deliberately to defy the authority of the Chair and to bring public business to an end simply by organised disorder, a grave and even irreparable injury is done to the institution of Parliament itself. The domain of politics has recently witnessed an example of defiance of the law on the part of the militant advocates of woman's suffrage and the condemnation of every section of the general public Was the reward of this out- burst. If the British Parliament is subjected to the same treatment that was meted out to the windows of the West-end shopkeepers, then the authors of the disorder must expect I that the judgment passed upon them by the public will be no less severe. .NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Our Member, in his suddenly acquired 'faith in precedents, alludes to the snap division of Monday as one which should result in "driving the place-seekers out of office." "Place-seekers !" Well ! Well i * * Mr. John Burns tells us : "That since 1902 when the raging tearing propaganda of the Tariff Reformers began, of 12,000 occupations in the Census he could only find five trades that had declined. They were, publicans, undertakers, pawnbrokers,chitnney-sweepers and fly-paper makers." * * *

The doubtful blessing of motor 'buses is shortly to be conferred upon the inhabitants of some of the districts which are not served by the tramways. The Tramways Com- mittee has decided to invite tenders for three of these vehicles, but the routes upon which they will be placed have not yet been determined. In all probability, however, the Lovely-lane and Bewsey-road area will be one of the favoured (?) localities. *

Mr. W. S. Penley, the well-known actor, who was famous for his creation and impersonations of" Charley's Aunt," died at his residence at St. Leonard's on Monday. He was 61. The present generation owes a lot to the late Mr. Penley. Few people who have reached their majority can have failed on some occasion or another to enjoy the amaz- ing adventures of " Charley's Aunt." The dead actor, in his time, has contributed largely to the gaiety and merriment of the nation. He would desire no better epitaph. * * *

The police have been indulging in an organ- ised raid on motorists, greatly to the benefit of the poor-box. The magistrates wisely did not take the offences too seriously, and we should imagine that some of the motorists from other towns would have an agreeable surprise when they were dismissed on pay- ment of the costs and ss. to the police poor- box. There is no doubt that nany drivers have traversed Sankey-street and other narrow thoroughfares at too great a speed. Some of them are Warrington drivers, well acquainted with the danger. We hope the timely action of the police will have a good effect. * *

The Irish Parliamentary delegates who recently returned from a tour of Australia and New Zealand to collect funds for the furtherance of the Home Rule cause were entertained at a gathering in London on Saturday, at which Mr John Redmond and other Nationalist members were present. Mr. W. A. Redmond said that they were received everywhere with marvellous unanimity and with scarcely a word of opposition. Whatever political divisions might exist in Australia they were agreed on Home Rule for Ireland. Mr. T. P. O'Connor said that he honed that in a couple of years the work of the Irish Parliamentary party would he done. *

The Lymm Urban District Council was suddenly and unexpectedly recalled from high considerations of Parliamentary pro- cedure to parish pump politics on Wednes- day evening. With solemn formality the members had passed a resolution to promote a Bill for the purchase of the waterworks when a" voice" from the back of the Council Chamber put the question: "Has this any connection with the sewer you are putting across my field ? " The anti-climax was somewhat startling and the Chairman hastily assured the interruptor that his field would not be affected by the business in hand. The gentleman responsible for this little diversion was Mr R Oldfield, who, it is interesting to note, was one of' the first members to be elected to the old Local Board in 1863. * •

Polling took place at Taunton on Monday, to fill the Parliamentary vacancy caused by the succession of Viscount Peel to the peerage. The result was declared on Monday night as follows : Sir Oilhert Whiß (C) 1,882 Mr. J. E. Schunk (1.) 1,597 285

Conservative mainrity

Taunton has in variably returned a Con- servative since 1885. The majority of 233 at the election in December, 1910, was the smallest the Conservatives ever had. In January, 1910. it was 368 and in 1909 it was 891. The Liberal poll on Monday was the largest in the history of the constituency. The Government of "traitors " still has some credit in the country! * * *

i lhe high cost of living s arranged by Private understanding. There are two great things to do. One is to set up the rule of justice and of right in such matters as the tariff. The Tariff has become a system of favours, 'Which the phraseology of the schedule was often deliberately contrived to conceal. Tell me that high prices are not due to the 'tariff! The tariff gave these men (the Trust Makers) the chance to do the thing which has produced the high prices. If every time we bought an article we could pay the price it would cost without the tariff, and should then be compelled to march down to the Custom House and pay what the -tariff comes to, then we would quickly become aware of just what the tariff does for us, and we would find a way to change lt.-'-From President Wilson's Speeches. • •

" Whom the gods wish to destroy they 'first make mad." Mr. Bonar Llw and his henchmen in the House were surely the maddest of" Mad Hatters" on Wednesday. Like spoiled children who cannot have their own way they lost their little tempers, screamed and threw things about. It was an edifying exhibition, truly, by the "gentle- Manly party." Most of the morning papers '3ll both sides unite in condemning the scene, and deploring the effect it ray have on the bainds of the electorate. The Newcastle Daily Chronicle (Li), 'ays: Men who are in deadly earnest do not behave themselves like a crowd of corner-boys at a dog fight. The Daily Graphic (C.). says: It may be that there are in the byways and back streets of politics a certain number of coarse-minded partisans who appreciate vulgar abuse, cud even open violence ; but to the great mass of sober-minded Englishmen such scenes as tho=e enacted in the House of Commons on Wednesday are utterly repugnant. The outracte inflicted upon the dignity of Parliamentary life will certainly recoil upon the individuals and the party responsible for it. Never yet in England has a political party gained anything by adopting the methods of the pot-house brawler. Mr. Bonar Law, with that fine capacity 11. r dignified leadership which has already _ulstinguished his short reign, says he eepts responsibility for what took elace and is entirely unrepentant. Well, so Lie it. There is a day of reckoning for such ,Ten as Mr. Bonar Law, and it cannot be far `‘4lBtarit.

LOCAL PARS.

Burglary at Penketh. Early yesterday morning burglars broke into the residence of Mr Philip C Garnett, Brookside, Penketb, and appropriated about £8 in cash. An entrance was effected by means of a front window.

Appointment for Mr. T. P. Bradshaw Mr. T. P. Bradshaw has been appointed by the Commissioners of Inland Revenue, as collector of taxes for No. I Division of the Borough of Warrington Mr. Bradshaw will shortly commence business on his own account.

News was received in Liverpool yesterday that the steamer Zelia, from Garston to Dublin, with coal, had foundered near the Bailey Lightship, outside, Dublin, and seven of the crew had been lost. The vessel belonged to Messrs William A Savage, Ltd, Chapel- street Liverpool, formerly the Zillah Shipping Corn pany, in which several local gentlemen are interested.

Mr Harold Smith, M.P. MR. SMITH, M.P., rushed into print AND PRECEDENTS, immediately after the

" snap " division in Parliament to inform his constituents that.the Government had suffered a "set-back from which it cannot recover." "If precedent were followed he had no doubt that the, Government must resign or drop the Bill. It is a fortunate thing that there are a few thinking politicians in Warrington, and that the majority of Mr Smith's constituents do not look upon him as the fount ef all truth and wisdom. When he talks about " precedent" he should not take it for granted that everybody has forgotten Mr. Balfour's view of precedents. A defeat to be real must express the fact that the Government no longer command the con- fidence of the House. Such a defeat was that which Mr. Balfour ignored in 1905. No one, except jaundiced politicians like Mr. Harold Smith, pretends that Monday's defeat expressed anything of the sort. In his own mind Mr. Smith knows as well as the countrY that the Government can still command a majority of 100 for the Bill which he saYa they " must drop." When Home Rule next "drops," it will drop on the shores of Ireland like the blessed dew of Heaven.

The Citizens' Guild of WARRINGTON AND Help deserves the , CONSUMPTION, thanks of all public' spirited townspeoPle' for its efforts to focus attention uPnli, the administration of those clauses 0: the National Insurance Act which deal with the provision of sanatoria anu other means of combating the greP„ scourge of consumption. The conference hela under its auspices on Tuesday evening elicited many helpful suggestions and should do much to ensure the working of this most' important section of the national health scheme to the fullest advantage. There are, of course, formidable difficulties to encounter, but only by practical experience can the machine be perfected, and it is sufficient for all keen social workers to knovi', that the Act, conscientiously administered, provides an instrument for grappling with an appalling problem on national lines. Warrington occupies a favourable, position, as compared with the majoritY municipalities. Thanks to the foresight 01 the Health Committee, sanatorium accornl- modation was available immediately the -No* came into operation, but an extension of ~tb \''"' institution at Sankey will be inevitable. 6 are glad that the conferenceurged the fgrina—. tion of an after-care committee, in 01_13 interests of consumptives who have undergo sanatorium treatment and after-. wards resume the battle of life with what often proves a heavy handicap. Such a NM- mittee should be capable of invaluable -work in finding suitable employment for the charged patient and in safeguarding the wel" fare of his family at a time when the income of the home is certain to be diminished.

Sir Gilbert Greenall's Successes at Chrysan-

themum Show.

One of the finest exhibitions of chrysanthemums seen in Manchester for many years was opened in the Town Hall, Manchester, on Tuesday, by the Lord Mayor (Mr S W Royse). S. GARLAND 51, Bridge st., Warrington.—Advt. The Picturedrome.

The attractions advertised for next week at the Picturedrome are:. Monday and Tuesday, 'The masked sisterhood," a thrilling story of a secret society of which the members are all women; it is an exceptionally unique picture. For Wednesday and Thursday," Wanted a grandmother," a pretty comedy in which the ideal picture actor, Maurice Costello, fills the principal character. "The will of John Waldron," an exciting Western dram, is billed for Friday and Saturday.—The new manager, Mr Allan Cooper, will take charge on Monday, Mr Gallagher assuming control at Widnes Picturedrome under the same proprietary.

Technical institute Prize Distribution.

The attention of our readers is directed to the fact that the annual presentation of prizes arid certificates to students of the School of Art, the Technical Institute and the Commercial Institute is to take place at the Parr Hall on Wednesday evening. The Chairman of the Education Committee (Councillor Henry Roberts, J.P.), is to preside, and the prizes, &c., will be distri- buted by Mr J H Reynolds, M.Sc., the late Director of Education for Manchester and Principal of the Manchester Municipal School of Technology. There will be an organ recital by Mr W H Payton, Mus. Bac., from 7.30 to 8 o'clock, at which hour the meeting will begin. In the course of the evening, Madame Sara Silcock is to give some vocal items. These occasions are always interesting to the parents and friends of the recipients of prizes, but, in view of the exceptional experience of the principal speaker, who for so many years has been a leader in technical and commercial education, the present one promises to have a wider importance. Tickets can be obtained at the Education Office.

Lady Inspector's New Apppointment. At a meeting of the Health Committee, held at the Town Hall on Tuesday evening Miss E G Pakes resigned her position as borough inspector of mid- wives to take up a similar position in Worcestershire. She has proved herself a very conscientious and diligent official andler departure will be regretted by her many friends in the town. On Saturday at it gathering in the Winmaileigh Cafe, Miss Pates was presented by the midwives of Warrington with a silver mounted biscuit barrel The presentation was made by Mrs Chadwick and Mrs Knot', the recipient briefly returning thanks. To-day Miss Pekes is to receive a further gift in the form of a portrait group of the midwives of Warrington, handsomely framed and suitably inscribed.

Bishop of Liverpool's Accident. Owing to an accident, the Bishop of Liverpool has been compelled to cancel his engagements of the immediate future, but hopes to be able to resume duty by probably Tuesday of next week. Last Tuesday evening shortly after seven o'clock,his Lordship in com- pany with the Archbishop f York and Mrs Chavasse, had reached St Peter's Church, Liverpool, for the festival service in connection with the Central Board of Missions. Of the three the Bishop was the ]a-t to der,cend from the motor-car which conveyed the party to the door of the church. Havieg instructed : the motor chauffeur as to the time to return after the service, the bishop moved forward, and in doing so stumbled over a portmarrteau belonging t r the arch- bishop, which had been placed on the footrvalk, and fell heavily on his face on the pavement. Dr Chavasse was naturally stupefied by the shr,ck, but soon recovered sufficiently to be able to walk, with the assistance of two poli2e offirers, into St Peter's vestry. From there he was shortly af erw rrts driven to 3 medical surgery in Rodney-street, tl here he received every attention. Much sympathy is naturally felt it] all quarters for the bishop, the prospect of wh se early recovery isthe subject of deep-felt congratulation Relics of Old Election Days. Two documents which throw an inter, s ing light on the local Parliatnentary elections of bygone dye, have been offered to the Nluseuni by Messrs II It , White and S ars— One is the election address of Mr John Wilson-Patten, afterwards Lord Witunarlsiah, to'the electors of the South West Lancashire Division io 1830, and it is remarkable for the absence of any reference to the political questions of that day. The Ifollowing is an extract : " The recent melancholy event, which hastens the dissolarion of the present Parliament, compels um to intrude myself agarn upon your notice, and to commence a general Canvass. It is my anxious wish to have the honour of waiting personally upon each individual Freeholder, but the Elective b rdy of this populous county is so immense that I much fear that time will not allow me fully to accomplish so arduous an undertaking. I must, therefore, throw myself upon your indulgence, and beg that if, through accident or want of time. I should omit any individual or any district, the omission may be attributed to its proper calla° and not to any disrespect or negligence on my part." The other document contains a number of resolutions passed at a meeting of freehrlders held at the Lion Hotel in July, 1830, in which the freeholders declare that "John Wilson-Patten, Esq., is a highly eligible person to represent. this county in Parliament." Attached to this document are the signatures of many of the notable townstue:., of that day.

Minh Lads' Brigade. The first annual meeting of the Non-Commissioned Officers' Mess was held at the Mess Headquarters, Rose and Crown-street, on Monday last, this date being the 21st anniversary of tie Church Lads' Brigade. The Sergeant Major in opening the meeting reviewed the events of the past 12 months, and thanked all his brother officers who had so ably assisted him to make the Mess what it was at present. The under-mentioned officials were elected to serve on the various committees etc for the ensuing year : Presideat, Sergeant Major Bottomley ; vice-president. Sergeant Instructor Gibson ' • secretary, Sergeant Bugler Goodwin • treasurer, Staff Sergeant Leach, Committee : A Company, Ci lour-Sergeant Thornton, B Company, Sergeant Hinton, 0 Company, Corporal Astall, D Company, Quarter-Master Sergeant Roberts, E Cempany, Colour-Sergeant Bradshaw. Auditors : Colour-Sergeant Spencer, Colour-Sergeant Woodbridge, Corporal Howells. Football Committee : Colour-Sergeant Spencer, Sergent Bugler Goodwin, Lance-Sergeant Musson, Bugler Unsworth, Corporal Howells. Press repre- sentatives : Colour-Sergeant Woodbridge, Corporal Howells.—A smoking concert was held last evening, at which the Commanding Officer (Lieutenant-Colonel Appleton was present.—On behalf of the Mess, the Sergeant-Major presented to him as a token of regard for the work he (the Colonel) had done for the Warrington Battalion of the C.L 8., a framed photo- graph of the Mess. - In reply, the Colonel thanked the Sergeant-Major for his kind words, and said it was a great pleasure to him, and to the other officers, to know the good name this Battalion had gained not only in Warrington, but elsewhere. His thanks, were due to the non-commissioned officers for having so ably backed him up in everything he had done.—A very enjoyable programme was then gone through, many of the lads contributing songs or ,ecitations.

Miss Elizabeth Cooke has been appointed head mistress of the Infants' department of the Beamont Council school at a commencing salary at the rate of £llB per annum*

A letter was read from the Rector of Warrington asking that the syllabus for religious instruction in use in the public elementary Council schools in the Borough may be introduced into the Secondary School. ?IIILDIZEN AND AMUSE3IENTS.

The School Attendance Committee resolved that this committee support the Health Committee in the repre- sentations which they are making to the Watch Com- mittee with a view to steps being taken to, as far as possible, prevent, the attendance of children at places of entertainment in the Borough after 9 p.m. SCHOOL ATTENDANCE OFFICERS' SALARIES. An application was submitted from the School Attendance Officers for an increase in their rate of payment, and it was resolved that the maximum salary of the School Attendance Officers be increased from £lO4 to £llO per annum.

UNDERFED CHILDREN. Instructions were given for inquiries to be made through the head teachers as to any children there may be at present attending the elementary schools in the Borough insufficiently fed. THE CHILDREN'S TEETH. A suggestion that an evening lecture to parents of school children upon the subject of the care of the children's teeth should be given by the School Dental Officer in one of the schools was approved by the Medical Inspection Committee and authority was given for the necessary expense to be incurred in connection with the advertising of the lecture and its illustration by lantern slides. Consideration of a further sugges- tion that an effort should be made to induce and *mist parents to obtain tooth brushes for use by their children was deferred for further information upon the subject.

Inkinber for Weethoughton (Mr. O. F. Clarke) at-the previous sitting. Insurable persons are liwit,d to those in receipt of £lO4 per annum, and there is a slidinix scale of contributions, both sexes being treated alike ii regard to payments. The following amendmeet trout the Socialist beeches was read by the Clerk of the House. Amendment Clauee Ill.—Delete the whole, and substitute : (1) Free lull benefits to all employee' persons above the ages of 21 years, whose weekly income is less than 25 shillings. (II) Free full benefits to all employed persons whose total weekly income is less than 6 shillings per head of family. (11I) In case of all employed persons, not included in the foregoing, the scale of payments shall be :—Weekly income 25/. to 30/. Id. per week; weekly income 30/. to 40/. 2d. per wees. Air DALBY (Soc., East Bradford), in moving the amendment, said he felt greatly encouraged by the speech of the member of the Government. The atti- tude they were taking that night WAS not the attitude they would take if they were aitting on the benches at Westminster. They took upthe only attitude they thought possible in a seciety like that. They had been asked by some of the Literal members of the House why they did not make the sch,me non-contributory. He was not quite sure whether—going practically on a matter of ultimate principles—he would substitute a non-con- tributory measure in place of the one they had before them. They bad never beard of Insurance in the sense they had it that night for the more highly paid proles- sional workers of the country. The manual wage earner was a man who sold his commodity and as soon as he could not supply that commodity his wage ceased. The business man was treated as a personality aud his services were retained for a long period. His salary went on whilst he was ill. They wanted a just attempt to be made to remedy the absurd payment which was given to a man for the work he did. No one knew the horrors of family life on 25s per week. • Continu- ing, the speaker asked on what grounds they could support the present clause in the Bill in regard to the maternity benefit ? There were only two courses open for members on that matter, seeing that without a shadow of doubt the present commercial system was largely responsible for illegitimacy. (" Question.") The miserable pittance paid out offered a direct bribe and inducement to it. Th t being the case either they were to spread broadcast a means of prevention of child tattle or OD the other hand they were to help the mother. He did not see any other way out of the difficulty (" Moral way !") Tneir amendment did remedy two or three serious blots on the Bill before the House. (Hear, hear and applause).

Mr IsHERWOOD (President of the Board of Agriculture) said the Governthent refused to accept the amendment. (Cries of shame.) They could not eliminate the fact that it was going to help the propagation ,of immorality. 'The member for East Bradford seemed to think the man should go free. He also thought there was no sincerity in the amendment for the proposer said that if they were elsewhere they would not propose that amendment ; we had better give them the whole House of Commons and let them rule it all themselves. (Opposition cries : " They would not throw books at each other.") Continuing, the speaker said the majority of workingmen did not care about their wives and families. (Question.) Nine-tenths of the workingmen in Warrington had a beer bill to pay before they went home at week-end. But so long as the man had money to pay for drink he ought to have it if he wanted it and could afford it.

Mr. CHAMBERLAIN (SOC., COlOO Valley), said they believed that the contribution ought to be in accord- ance with what a man could afford. The previous speaker did not think drink was at all bad if the man could afford it. If he went on like that he would have to join them on the Socialist benches. What they bad been troubling about for years was that the working- man could not afford champagne. If the bon. member knew anything about social economy he must know that the workingmen paid everything. They were out to uplift the workingmen and by that emendment they hoped to do a little towards it. (Hear, hear and applause). Mr. IL OSBORNE (L , Wellington South), said it was imperative that if they were going to have a general scheme of insurance that they should absorb the friendly societies end make it a State scheme. Mr. H. E. HAWTHORNE (C., East Belfast), opposed the amendment but he only did so on principle. He thought there should be no benefit without more or less of a contrihui ion. The majority of members of the Government were in favour of a higher living wage- ("Question.") "Give us a chance."—(" You have had the chance.") The members on the benches opposite have had more chances than we have had.— (Mr WYNNE JONES : We have not had the House of Lords and if we have not done more you mist ask your House of Lords the reason.) Mr KILDUFF (Nat., Dublin Harbour) spoke of the maternity benefit clause and said the person who brought in a hill for National Insurance should be like the ideal man who brought it iu—Mr Lloyd George. (Hear, hear and applause). The Rev E C E CARLETON (C., Truro) compared the allowance for medical benefit in the Bill before the House and that provided for iu Mr Lloyd George's Act. A Nationalist Member asked were they in order in discussing Lloyd Go-age's Act when they had the Government's Bill before the house?

The SPEAKER: He is attempting to justify the present Bill by comparing the one of 1911. Mr. JARMAN (the leader of the Opposition) : What does this Bill do to provide medical benefits ? They are almost on the scale provided by Mr. Lloyd George. Rev E. C. E CARLETON : We are discussing the general question of the matter. The Bill instituted by Lloyd George would stop the voluntary contribu- tions to the Infirmary. (Cries of no, no.) The house surgeons bad also said they would want salaries.
