CONFERENCE OF CLERGY AND LAITY.

Religious Instruction in Council

Schools.

The clergy and lay representatives of the rural deanery of Winwick held a conference at the Church House, Bridge-street on Wednesday afternoon. Prior to the conference a chapter of the clergy was held in Holy Trinity Church when an hddress on " Enthusiasm regained" was given by the Rev T H P Hyatt, Vicar of St Mary's, Grassendale. The Rural Dean (Rev Canon Willis) presided at the Conference. A veto of sym,sathy was passed with the Bishop of Liverpool in the accident which has befallen him. The following appointments for the next twelve months were made :—Hon. secretary, the Rev 0 Vale Owen ; hon. treasurer, Mr J H Smethurst ; representa- tives of the rural deanery on the diocesan committees the late members re-elected en bloc. . .

Mr SMETHURST save a statement with regard to the flosnces of the deanery and it was resolved that each parish be requested to contribute to the expeuses. RELIGIOUS INSTRUCTION IN COUNCIL SCHOOLS.

Mr W H STANSFIELD gave an address on this subject. Having contended that to Churchmen the undenomina- tional instruction given in Council schools was without a soul, lifeless and cold," he said the religious instruction of a child of Church parents ought to be definite and dogmatic, and it was in return for the right and opportunity of giving that definite teaching to Church children in the Council schools that he would give to Nonconformist bodies the right to instruct their own children in Church schools in single-school areas. Considerably more than half of the children in the country were now being taught in Council schools, and they should insist on the right of the parent to have his child taught the principles of religion by a member of the religious denomination to which he belonged. Such a plan was in operation in Birmingham in 1906 (and might be still in operation), where the Church and other religious bodies took advantage of the oppor- tunity afforded them of giving definite religious instruction on Tuesdays and Fridays in each week. He believed that the fears of some people about introducing discord into the schools if such a plan were universally adopted were unfounded. Such a scheme had succeeded I in Australia, Germany and other countries, and why should it not succeed in this ? There was abundant evidence that undenominationalism had had its day,and people were beginning to realise that religious instruc- tion, such as that which was perforce given in Council I schools at present, was, apart from a religious organiza- tion, a failure. The Rev. Canon STEVENSON introduced the question of religious instruction in Secondary Schools. He pointed out that, in the Municipal Secondary Schools, the Education Authority was not compelled to furnish facilities for such instruction but might allow it at the request of the parents. If denominational Instruction was not possible, they should try to get Scriptural teaching introduced. It was a great grief to him to find children—the flower of their children— growing up without Bible knowledge. The request for religious teaching must not come from that conference or from any other organization, however much respected, but direct from the parents. The Rural Dean and other speakers approved the adoption of the policy mentioned by Canon Stevenson for the present., but the conference as a whole was strongly of the opinion that Church teaching for Church children was the only policy which could ultimately obtain.

DRAMAS OF THE CARD TABLE.

[COPYRIGHT.]

"Gaming," says old Cotton in his Compleat Gamester," "is an enchanting witchery, gotten between idleness and avarice;" and ever ~inc. 2ards were introduced into Europe, during ths :hirteenth and fourteenth centuries, card-games have been perhaps the favourite means of ;ratifying the well-nigh universal craving. IThere seems, in fact, to be inevitably in the mind of man some sort of superstitious respect for the verdict of these painted pasteboards. whose lice in one form or another is and has been general in all nations and all ages. They are the very signs and symbols of the Goddess ( Luck, and therefore seem to fascinate es- pecially those engaged in pursuits where chance ;,s bound to play a part, so that it vill atet be found matter of history that those who play desperate game. be it of love, intrigue, or war, are specially ready to tcst their fottunes on the turn of the cards. Such games may, at times, have had their effect on history. An over- wrought brain, a strained state of the nerves, may very readily be vividly impressed by the result of tineh consultations of the oracle; and he state of depression or of vigorous hope thus induced may easily decide the crisis one Wai Jr the other.

In Mr. Orchardson4 elebrated picture, "Hard Hit," we see a young ?4,,hteenth-century gallant, who has evidently lost heavily at cards, withdrawing from the tame in which he has nothing lEft to risk, witle lightness and bravado which barely hides the lespair beneath the mask. THE INTRODUCTION OF CARDS.

One of the earliest stories relating to cards is in connection with their first introduction rite France in the reign of Charles VI. The infortunate kin:: having lost his Teason ihrougls "coup do soleil," a pack of cards was devised. For his amusement, and some of these are be. lieved to be still in existence. A less glint early association is in connection with the be :rothal of James IV. of Scotland. to his Eng. fish bride, for we read of the King's coming, his Haroun-al-Raschid fashion, "privily to the castell, where he entered within the chain- Der with a small company, and found tht ;Ilene playing at the cardes." In early days, however, cards were not in very general use, at any rate in England, and it was not until the days of the Restoration that card. playing began to be very greatly in vogue. It is a curious phenomenon that games of cards are the most frequent solace of those in misfortune and suspense, and it seems likely enough that the Court of Charles 11. brought back with them fondness for high play from their impecunious exile in France.

===== 0002601_19121116_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
SOMEI DRAMATIC EPISODES.

In the Restoration period we read of some re. rnarkable incidents at the card tables, and Mr, l'epys records one or two such. "Among others Nfr. Creed and Captain Ferrers tell me the stories of my Lord Duke of Buckingham's and my lord's falling Out at Havre-de Grace at cards: :hey two and my Lord St. Albans playing. The Duke did, to my Lord's dishonour, often say that he did, in his conscience, know the contrary to what he then said about the difference at cards: and so did take up the money that he should have lost to my Lord, which, my Lord resenting. fli.d nothing then, but that he doubted not but there were ways enough to get his money of him. F.o they parted that night; and my Lord sent ir R. Stayner, the next morning, to the Duke to know whether he did remember what he said last night, and whether he would own it with his sword and a second, which he said lie would, and So both sides agreed. But my Lord St. Albans and the Queen and Ambassador Montagu did waylay them at their lodgings till the differ. ?nee was made up, to my Lord's honour, who bath got great reputation thereby." Further, he gives a graphic description of "the gaming at the Groome Porter's," the groom-porter being an official of the royal house hold, among whose duties were the providing of 3ards and dice, and settling disputes in gaming matters. Evelyn also has some mention of this, and says that on twelfth night " he came away when the Duke of Ormond had won about £l,OOO, and left them still at passage, cards, etc." At this time, however, dicing was still the gamester's favourite pastime, as being on the one hand the most ri.,ky and desperate of ven.•i tures, and on the other affording to the sharper many chances of turning the luck in his own favour.

===== 0002601_19121116_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
ANECDOTES OF TILE CARD TABLE.

Memoirs and letters of the early eighteenth century abound in anecdotes of the card-table, and one of the most remarkable features of that period was the prevalence of gambling among the gentler sex. Card-playing with some of these ladies as a terrible mania. It was the passion lof their byes, and Horace Walpole has an aneo. dote to the effect that "Lord Besborough and Lord Dillon, pla.ying at ryninze Vother night with Miss Pelham, and happening to laugh, she flew into a passion and said: 'lt was terrible to play with boys!' 'And our two ages together,' said Lord Dillon, 'make up above a hundred ' and forty.'" Towards the close of the eighteenth century this phase reached its height in the cele• brated Faro banks of certain ladies of fashion, one of whom, Lady Buckinghamshire, is said to have slept with a blunderbuss and a pair of pistols ready to hand, to protect her " bank "! Gillray has a well-known print, entitled "Modern Hospitality, or a Friendly Party in High Life." In this Lady Archer is represented with ronleaux of gold and bank notes before her, smiling, as she shows her cards, with the remark: " The knave wins all!" The Prince of Wales, sitting next her, has just lost his last piece, and with raised eyebrows expresses his wonder at the luck. Lady Buckinghamshire, who has staked double, looks annoyed at her losses, as also does Fox, whose last three pieces have just gone the way of the rest. We learn from a newspaper of 1802 that "in a pleasant village near the Metropolis, noted for its constant 'tea and turn-out' parties, the extortion of 'Card Money' had risen to such a pitch that it was no unusual thing for the lady of the house, npon the breaking up of a table, to immediately examine the sub-cargo 9f the candlestick, and. previous to the departure of her guests, proclaim aloud the lamentable do- falcation of a pitiful shilling, which they might, perchance, have forgot to contribute."

REGENCY MANNERS.

An amusing story is told of Charles James Fox, who owed a gambling debt to Sir John Lade. lie was prepared to discharge the claim, when Sir John, callhig, for pen and ink, began to calculate the interest. "I thought," said Fox, "this was a debt of honour. As you seem co consider it a trading debt, and as T make' it ah invariable rule to pay my Jew creditore test, you 'mist wait a little longer for your ..uoney !"

EMPLOYEES SYSTEMATIC THEFTS. IRON GOODS STOLEN FROM MESSRS. RICHMONDS WORKS. At the Warrington Police Court on Saturday before Mr J W Hayward (in the chair), and other magistrates, William Ernest idd, New Buildings, Thelwall-road, Thelwall, was charged and pleaded guilty to having atolen some time during the past twelve months from the works of Messrs Fletcher, Russell and Co, Ltd, a gas stove, a water heater, &c, and to having received from William Bodle three enamel plates, the property of Messrs Richmond, Ltd, well knowing the goods to have been stolen. .

_ .... Mr WH J Jenkins (for Messrs Henry Greepall and Co.) prosecuted and said that similar thefts had been exceedingly rife in the town during the past few years. Employers were making every endeavour to prevent the thefts, and he asked the Bench to render such assist- ance in that direction as in the discharge of their duties they thought fit. Not only had the prisoner stolen from his own employers, but he had been guilty of receiving from another prisoner, who would be dealt with in due course. An enormous amount of wickedness had been perpetrated at Messrs. Richmond's and he asked for such a penalty as would act as a deterrent. Prisoner: lam sorry it has happened. A gentleman in Court expressed the opinion that the Prisoner had got into trouble through other people, and hoped the Bench would deal as leniently as possible with him.

Kidd was fined .£5, including an advocate's fee, on each charge, with the option of a mouth's imprisonment in each case. At the Police Court on Monday four other men appeared before the Mayor (presiding), Alderman Bolton, Councillor Wilkinson, Messrs 6 Wright, W Furness, G Pilling and W Roberts. The prisoaers, William Bodle, New Buildings, The!wall-lane, Latchford Without ; Thomas Hackett, clerk, 7, Frederick-street; Hildebrand Rench, clerk, Longdin-street • and Peter Knight, jun., goods inspector, 15, Stanley-street, pleaded guilty to having stolen some time during the past twelve months a large quantity of goods, the property of Richmond's Ltd. Mr Jenkins (for Messrs Henry Greenall and Co.) prosecuted, and Mr Steel appeared for Repot' and Knight. . _ .

Mi. Jenkins said the four men were employed by Messrs Richmond. They had been guilty of a long series of serious thefts. The Bench would appreciate the serious nature of the charge when it was stated that the firm were obliged to trust their employees,and they were treated in the despicable way they had been. There was no defence to the thefts. The men had been .employed by the firm for periods raging from six to two years. The methods by which they had robbed the firm were by placing goods on lorries which came to the works, and giving orders for them to be sent to their homes. Bodle said he bad no statement to make to the Bench except that be would be doubly punished, because he would have to break up his borne. Hackett : lam guilty. I have never done anything like it before, and it will be a lesson to me as long as I live.

Mr Steel, addressing the Bench on behalf of Bench and Kniebt.said those two men bad taken small things thoughtlessly. In one case Bench had given goods to a lady whose daughter he was engaged to, and Knight had given goods to his sister who had just started a fish business in Warrington. Two of the principals of the firm were prepared to give the young men excellent characters, and they were also prepared afier the case had been dealt with, to take them back into their employ. Having regard to that attitude of the firm his taek "ad been considerably relieved and the responsi- bilities of the mogistrates had Leen relieved to a great extent. Both were engaged to he married* He there fore annealed to the Bench to deal with them under the Probation of Offenders Act, thereby giving them an . . . . .

opportunity of redeeming their characters. Mr Jenkins said that as the prosecuting solicitor it would 171 become him to close his eyes to the gravity of the offence. He, however, believed that the firm were willing to give the two young men another chance and he sincerely trusted thit that opportunity being given to them it would he a lesson to them. He cou'd not agree with Mr Steel that the offences had been committed thoughtlessly. The Bench.after considering their decision in private, fined each prisoner .23 including costs, with an alter- native of a month's imprisonment. Mr Jenkins acknowledged the careful and smart way in which the case had been handled by the police. Detectives Reddington and Summers and the other officers who acted with them were deserving of praiie for the creditable manner in which they dealt with the case. A sixth man was fined at the Borough Court on Tuesday, before Mr J Rymer Young (prodding), Colonel Edelsten and Mr C Jackson Holmes, for having robbed his employers. Matthew-street, was charged with having stolen during the past six months goods to the r.slne of 17s 6d, the property of Messrs Richmonds, Limited. ‘‘)att had been employed by the firm for seven years and was engaged in the racking department, where he we- constantly handling goods similar to those he had Stolen. "FINISH HER SHE IS NOT DEAD."—KECKWICK ASSAULT CASE.—Harry Platt, Preston Brook and Harry Higbfleld, of Keckwick, were summoned for an aggra- vated assault on Sarah Ann Evans, Broome's Cottages, Neckar ick, on October Bth. They were further charged with assaulting George Evans on the same date. High- field was also summoned for using threats towards Agnes Evans.—Mr Steel appeared for the complainants. and Mr Browne for the defendants.—Mr Steel said that evidently the assault committed on his clients was not only premeditated but had been arranged. On the day in question the complainants were having dinner and the front door which was locked was burst open and the two defendants rushed into the kitchen, followed by their wives. Mrs Highfield had a poker, and the moment the defendants and their wives got into the kitchen Mrs Platt picked up a poker belonging to Mrs Evans. Subsequently Mrs Evans got up from the table, and Platt Ftruck her in the mouth and knocked out a Dumber of teeth and cut her lips. She fell under the table, and while there the defendant Highfleld kicked her. The husband walked round the table, and as he was doing so Platt hit him in the face and knocked him to the floor. He got up and went towards the front door, and he was knocked down again by both defendants. Be afterwards managed to get out. Ason of the complainants had meanwhile gone out of the house to see if he could find the police. The defendants were going out, but returned, and Mrs Evans took out a pistol. He thought the Bench would say she was quite justified in doing so. Platt eosin struck her, and knocked her down sgain, and Highfleld kicked her on the head, and she lost consciousness. Mrs High- field. said "Go and finish her, she is not dead yet." The defendant Highfield used threats to Agnes Evans.—The complainants gave evidence in corroboration of the statement. Mr Broen argued that defendant had visited the house. to see Mrs Evans and her daughter in reference to suggestions made against Mrs Higifield and also about a statement that he had knocked Agnes Evins down with his bicycle the night before. It was Mrs Evans who picked up the poker. In the struggle for tne revolver Mrs Evans went down. He submitted that the defendants were perfectly justified in view of the serious accusations, in going to the house to make inquiries and in protecting themselves when Evans pulled out a revolver and threatened to shoot them. Defendants gave evidence and denied kicking or hitting , the complainants. The women were never in the house, ' and did not have a poker. Hightleld said Mrs Evans hit him on the head with a poker.—The Bench retired, and on their return the Chair.nan said the defendants , would ts,ch be fined £5 including costs. The case of threats would be dismissed.

Borough Police Court.

SATURDAY.—Before MeFars J W Hayward(in the chair), A J Jolley, IL T Pairclough and T Pemberton. WEIGHED . AND FOUND WANTING.—Harry . .

. . Burrows, butcher, Mersey-street, was summoned for having had in his possession a pair of unjust scales. Mr A T Holloway, deputy town clerk, who prosecuted, said two Corporation inspectors visited the defendant's shop, where they found a 141 b. balance on the counter. Below the scale they found a piece of fat, on the top Of which wasa piece of metal, making the scale half- an-ounce unjust to the purchaser. In the course of the year a great fraud would be committed on the public. It was not necessary to prove that the defendant was fraudulently using the scale; it only had to be proved that the balance was in his possession. That sort of thing existed to a great extent all over the town and the Markets Committee were determined to put a stop to it. Evidence having been given, defendant was fined 102. including costs.

DEATH OF MR. E. C. COOPER.

Another farcilar and respected figure has been removed by the death, which took place on Saturday morning, of Mr Edmund Curtis Cooper, of 296, Manchester-road, Warrington. The deceased gentle- man, who was 78 years of aue, had been in indifferent health for some time, but did not take to his bed until within two days of his death. He retained full po,ses- sion of his faculties and the end came quite peacefully. Mr Cooper had been associated with this distiict for the greater part of his life. He took the licence of the Oak Tree Inn, Newton-le-Willows,fifty years ago, and after keeping that well known hostelry for ten years, he removed to the Swan Inn, Winwick, and remained there about 25 years, during which time he bought the house and licence. Taking up public work, he became clerk to the Parish Council and assistant overseer. He also represented the district on the Warrington B ard of Guardians for several years, and at the time he resigned was vice-chairman of that authority He also served on the Assessment Committee. On retiring from business Mr Cooper came to live at Warrington, but still retained his official connection with the , Parish Council at Winwick and continued to act as assistant overseer. He performed these ditties up to , the time of his death, his last accounts being sub- ; witted to audit a fortnight ago. Mr Cooper was a ; staunch Churchman and every Sunday morning he was to be seen in his place at the Parish Church. At one time he held the office of sidesman.Mr Cooper was prominently identified with the Order of Freemasons. He was a member of tbe Lodge " Faith" (484), Ashton-in-Makerfield, and passed through all the offices. During this time he was appointed Provincial Grand Sword Bearer. In politics he was a strcng C nservative, and was officially identified for a con- siderable time with the Newton Division Conservative Association. Widely read and blessed with a cheer- ful temperament and a very retentive memory, Mr Cooper was a most interesting companion and he never tired of recounting items from his never-failing fund of reminiscences. He was greatly devoted to out-door life and to this fact his remarkable alert- ness and vitality of mind and body, notwithstanding his ripe old age, must have been in a great measure due.

The funeral took place quietly at the Warrington Cemetery on Tuesday afternoon, and was attended by members of the family and friends. The first part of the service was held in the Parish Church. The Rector (the Rev Canon Willis) and the l?ev A J Bratt, officiated. Among a number of beautiful floral tributes was a cross from the Parish Church officials. The funeral arrangements were carried out by Mr II F Greenhough.

===== 0002601_19121116_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

" The Mormon and His Wives. " As pictured by the mormon missionaries Utah is a land flowing with milk and honey and its elders are benevolent gentlemen, ever ready and willing to receive with paternal kindness and consideration any young and attractive girl believer. But what a great difference between this phase of Mormon life and that presented in Mr H F Housden's drama" The Mormon and his wives " which has been played so successfully at the Royal Court Theatre during the week. Here we are given to understand that the real Utah is a veritable hot-bed of iniquity and vice, and its elders, men who would not refrain from any base action to serve their own ends. Although the play deals with an unpalatable subject, it is presented in an acceptably guarded manner. The story is quite orthodox and deals with the abduction of a young mill girl, in love with an English minister, to Utah ; her tribulations there and eventually a happy re-union, and the defeat of the Mormons. Mr Oswald Cray himself enacts the part of the Rev Dudley Wade, the Lancashire minister and in quite an unobtrusive manner he gives startling statistics and disclosures of the Mormon and his work. His acting is of a finished character and his elocutionary powers are excellent. Miss Rosalie Notrelle as Nellie Weirdale, the victim of the Mormons, whose acting in the heavier parts of the play is beyond reproach, ought to remember that an air of nonchalance and indifference is not generally assumed by the typical Lancashire mill lass—the character she represents—especially at a time of festivity, on the occasion of her 21st birthday. Mr David Blair as Josiah Beamish, a. mormon missionary of the lowest type, is very skilful in his treatment of such a reprehensible character, lie has a just con- ception of the depths to which these missionaries will descend, and exercises it with all credit to himself, but to the chagrin and disgust of the audience who wax indignant at his craftiness. As his accomplice, Mr William N Burton, in the role of Silas Mellish, is highly successful, and creates much laughter by his repetition of the hypocritical phrase "it's all for the good of the cause, brother " and his mode of expres- sion. Miss Aimee Gratton Clyndes as Julia Beamish, the jealous Mormon's wife, is an able foil in the hands of yeamish, and her style of accomplishing the various villainies of her master ensures a due quota of hisses from the popular parts of the house. Mr Machintosh Clyde appears as Michael Maloney, and is successful in keeping the ball of humour running through the piece, and at the same time acting as an amateur detective. He is ably assisted by Miss Pattie Dane as Rosie May, a mill-hand. Mr Harold Stevens and Miss Helen Lowther present the affectionate guardians (Mr and Mrs Jasper Weirdale) in a realistic manner ; and Mr Ernest Duval is a typical Osprey, the Indian Chief. Mr Leslie Howard makes a capital Zachariah Papp, the fawning old Mormon elder, whilst the part of Jerry Ackroyd, the jovial Lancashire mill hand, is in the able bands of Mr H Reynolds. The staging of the play is highly creditable, and the orchestra, under the able leadership of Mr Boothroyd, plays choice music.

e-SOCIAL

endurance was no longer possible, and when that point ' was reached the system which had brought it to pass would assuredly meet with the disaster which was, in reality, the only fitting end for it. Whether that dirvister would come violently and involve actual suffering, he did not know; nor, indeed, did he greatly care. lie was quite sure, however, of two things : firstly, our present system carried within itself the seeds of its own ruin, and secondly, no suffering which might or might not accompany Its final overthrow could possibly be greater than the actual suffering entailed by its existence at this moment. The preacher finilly emphasised the need for wholesale moral and spiritual conversion, the coming of a point of view which actually despised material things in love of spiritual things, and hated existence not because it starved the body but because it cramped the soul—of mor a and spiritual conversion so vast that even to suggest it seemed almost the enterprise of a madman. Yet it would have to come. Let but once the inward revolution take place in a man and the approaching Day of the Lord, which should witness the downfall of existing society, the overthrow of our present civilization would be most gloriously welcome. Though it be darkness to many, yet to him it would be light, and even in ruin he would find great joy.

Manchester Presbytery. fhe Rev. William Reid's Ordination.

The Pr( s`qtery of Manchester met on Monday night in the Grosvenor Square Presbyterian Church with the Rev J G Glover as Moderator. The Presbytery Clerk (the Rev. Thomas Mackay) reported a memorial received from the congregation of St John's Church, Warrington, in favour of the ordination of Mr William Reid, an elder of the church, to the pastoral charge of the congregation. The Rev R M Gray raid that Mr Reid had come to Warrington three years before to a dispirited remnant of a church. The numbers of the congregation were few and it had sold its church build- ing. To-day the congregation was one of the most flourishing in the whole presbytery. It was decided, without a dissentient, to send the request of the con- gregation to the Synod.

Wycliffe P.S A. Society.

There was a fair attendance on Sunday, when the meeting had special reference to the question of temperance. Mr. H. Farrar (the President) presided, and spoke upon the 'evils of intemperance and the support given to temperance work by all parties in the I State and country. He had been n supporter of temperance for fifty years, and was a supporter of the late Sir Wilfred Lawson's Permissive Bill.—Mr. J. Himsworth moved. Mr. H. Osborne seconded, and Mr. Joseph Owen supported a resolution calling upon the Government to introduce and pass the Licensing Bill, including the power of Local Veto in the Parliamentary Session of 1913. Mr J Jarman spoke upon the danger of delay in dealing with the question, as the evil was a growing one. Prosperity meant the growth of the drink bill. The public-house had Its influence upon the slum dwellers Financially the trade drew together all sorts and condi- tions of men, who invested in the business. Christian men ought to stand aloof from a business, the results of which were so degrading and impoverishing to the homes of the people. Fathers were lowered in their manhood. Mothers were robbed of their right to joy, peace and comfort, and children were deprived of a father's and n.other's natural love and care. Intoxicat- ing drink brought its own, results—poverty, insanity, crime and disease, and it was a fruitful source of con- sumption. He concluded by asking all present to be careful about the influence they had upon others. The resolution was carried unanimously. Miss E Hewitt sang sweetly "Nearer my God to Tnee " Mr P,.yton presided at the organ. 'Mr Yearaley took part in the meeting.

" The Necessary Revolution."

Dr. Mellor and the Destruction of Existing Society.

Continuing his course of sermons on " Religion and Democracy" at Cairo-street Cuspid on Sunday evening, the Rev Dr Stanley Mellor took for his subject "The necessary revolution." lie said the final result of open and sincere criticism was to bring them face to face with a situation so appalling that the only way of escape fri,ru it was by completely destroying it. They had, in some way or other, to produce that intense dis- content, to create that point of view, to foster that, attitude of mind which should demand not merely reform in our social life but radical reconstruction and renewal. ro the ears of some peopleithe word revolution had a fatal and ill-omened sound; they shuddered at it. But what did it really mean ? bimply a complete turning round, a total overturning of what existed. It might be sudden and it might be violent, but the great point was that it, was complete. It was the pulling down A the house, in order to build on a better foundation. The Christian religion had sometimes been described as revolutionary VS hy ? Simply because it demanded—what, of course,it never accomplished—the total reversal of all the exist- ing social order and the complete tr msvaluation ”f all social values. The ideas which must sway the future were themselves 'evolutionary. What those ideas were was surely clear by now : one was the idea of religion as a genuine element in the personal life, the other was the idea of genuine democracy as the form of social existence. They could not have any real democracy without, at least, equality of inheritance and oppor- tunity, and to get those they had to order society on an economic basis entirely different from that which at preset held. Reform as much as they could, if they began with a capitalistic and competitive order, that was assuredly what they would end with. They might perhaps make the working of the system more humane; they might possibly decrease its actual hardships in some few individual cases, but by reform alone they would not change it, and here only radical and complete change could be in any way satisfactory. The sole end for which our present economic system existed and seemed designed was profit ; no reform which did not, in any way, directly attack that particular fact could be ultim- ately valuable. They might shorten hours of labour and increase wages : they might provide for sickness and unemployment, but they would not get democracy nor anything like it, till they had destroyed the fact and the principle of inheritance, and made it possible for every child of the community to start his career on actually equal terms with any and every other. The destruction of existing society was delayed because neither of the great ideas of religion and democracy was adequately realised : the men of the moment were neither really religious nor really democratic. If they were, they would see, without any possibility of doubt or questioning, firstly the utterly intolerable character of the existing order and secondly the impersonality of its continuance. Whither was society actually tending? 'Towards what was the positive working of our economic system, as a matter of fact, leading? The system had by now gathered into itself a certain power of movement and of expres- sion which paid but little regard to the wishes and 1 desires of individuals who had to live under it ; the system of industrialism, so called, motived only by the desire for gain and turned only to the accumulation of wealth, had become in these days little more than it vast and terrifying machine, going its own way regarciless of the cries and prayers of individual hurnaeity,a, machine whose course it was supremely difficulty, if not impossible, to stop. This capitalistic and competitive machine went, on grinding out wealth which passed into the hands of fewer and fewer individuals ; day by day it increased the number of those who were nothing but its slaves. The one utterly certain thing about its working was this : that as the rich became richer, the poor became poorer ; the gulf between the few who had and the multitude who had not grew wider at every moment and no amount of the , philanthropic effort could make that gulf leis. And all 1 the while,ideas went their way to and fro in the world ; hatred of the system increased ; the range of unsatis- fied demand and desire became ever greater and the opportunity for satisfaction ever less ; already violence was familiar to us and the mutterings of cramped, enslaved and angered men and women turned to open opposition. What was the seemingly inevitable denoue- ment ? It was difficult to find a thinker of any eminence who could look with equanimity upon the actual movement of things as they are ; nay, it was difficult to find one who did not foresee and foretell the rapidly approach- ing climax, the swift coming downfall about our heads of this whole fabric of so-called civilization. The whole tendency of our system was not towards an ever finer and freer life of developing individuality, but towards a deeper and deeper enslavement, a more and more complete lack of leisure, a more and more whole- sale abandonment of all that was beautiful and worthy. And the question inevitably arose : Would the mind and heart of womankind permit that ?He had hirnself I sufficient faith in human nature to answer that question in the negative. There must come a point beyond which I

St John's Presbyterian Guild.

The above society met on iclondsy evening with Mr Robinson occupying the chair. A d scu ssion on" What is the solution of the Sabbath Problein ? " was opened by Mr P H Brown, who coveted a great deal of ground and drew attention to its many aspects, religious, social and political. Foreign Missionary Anniversary at Wycliffe Church.

The Rev A Warren, B.A , of Calciata,preached morn leg and evening at Wyclifie Cengregiktional Church on Sunday, on the occasion of the toreign missionary anniversary when collections were taken on behalf of

the London Missionary Society. Mr Warren also addressed the children in the Sunday school in, the afternoon, giving them an idea of a Ilindoo Temple, of the deities worshipped and the sacrifices made. His addresses at both the morning and evening services had referenca to his work as a missionary in Calcutta. In his first address he outlined the Christian work that p.as being undertaken, both among the English residents and the native populatic 0. These two branches of work ran on parallel lines but never met. The two pitfalls against which young men going from this country to India needed to guard against were whisky and sweepstakes.

In the evening, Mr Warren referred more particularly to the Christian educational work amongst the Hindoos. Be pointed out that the British nation had a special responsibility for the Indian empire, and the present was a critical time in that empire's history if as were going to shape it aright. The Indian youth was beidg educated on Western lines and in the English language. Religious education was not a part of the college course and the duty was vist upon the Christian Church of sasing whether a nation of 300 millions should have no religion as the - basis of their morals or wehther -Christianity should he the basis. It was almost impossible to have the Eastern mind

bent to the science, philosophy and learning of the West and still retain its hold on so much of the superstition of the ancient religions. They had therefore growing up a race of young men, keen intellectually, with practically no religion to be the anchor of their moral nature. That had been realised as a danger not merely by Christian, but by Hindoo and Mohammedan alike. In order to give religious education the missionary societies had founded colleges linked up to the universities for the purpose of teaching Christianity. In their hostels the prejudices of caste were being broken down, but the preacher acknowledsed that there were great difficulties in getting the Hindoo youths to be baptised as Christians owing to the ostracism of their parents and friends. Although the number of avowed Christians was comparatively small, Christianity had had the effect of causing the Hindoos to reform their religion and set up a standard of morals and sacial service which did not exist hitherto.

New Patten Hall. Gift to Holy Trinity Church.

Through the kindness and generosity of the Hon Ellinor Wilson Patten and Miss Elizabeth Wilson Patten, daughters of the late Lord Winroarleigh, the parishioners of Holy Tinity have been provided with a new and imposing ball, for paroohial purposes in the vicinity of Hanover-street. The new building which was opened on Saturday and named the Patten Hall, is admirably adapted for the purposes for which it has been built. The interior consists of a large hall 73ft long, by 30ft wide, and 23ft high, with two classrooms, 20ft by 12ft and 12ft by 12ft, with lavatory and other accommodation. The work was commenced in April last, and has been very satisfactorily c,irried out by Mr Henry Collin, builder, Marsou-street, from designs by, and under the superintendence of Mr T Beesley, Bold-street. The opening ceremony was a very interesting one and was performed by the niece of the Misses Wilson-Patten, the Hon Mrs Lumley. The Rev E C E Carleton presided and others present included : The Hon Misses Wilson-Patten, Mrs Carleton, the Revs H R Wild and J Lynn Parry, Mrs T H Lyon, Colonel and Mrs Lumley, the Rev Canon and Mrs Willis, Mrs Arthur Willis, Mr J E Seeker, Mr and Mrs H B Melville, Alderman Bennett. Mr and Mrs E A White, Mr J H Smethurst, Mr John Hesketh (people's warden), Mr T Welsby, Mr James Harding, Mr A Walsh, Mr H Collin (representing the builders of the hall, It W Collin), Mr Oldfleld, Mr Jackson, Mr J J Firth, Mr Thomas Hunt, Mr Dyke and Mr George Gill.

The Hon. ELLINOR WILSON PATTEN briefly touched upon the good work which had been done in the Bank Quay district for forty years, and acknowledged the help which had been forthcoming from manufacturers and others. She also paid a tribute to the devoted efforts of Mr. and Mrs. Carleton and of Mrs- Hunt, who had acted as Mission woman for 24 years. Mrs. LUMLEY, in opening the hall, expressed the sincere hope that the meetings of the Bank Quay Parochial Mission, which was started in the year 1876. and of which Miss Elizabeth Wilson-Patten had been lady superintendent ever since, would be continued and prosper in the new building, with the kind help of Mrs Carleton and Mrs Bunt. (Hear, hear, and applause.) 11 She had much pleasure in declaring the hall open, and lin christening it the " Patten Hall." (Applause). Colonel Lumley said that people had of late been dis- cussing the decadence of this good old country morally, politically' physically, patriotically and socially, but he thought that now and then they were rather inclined to overlook the steady stream of good which was being done in their midst, and of which they had a good example that day by the opening of that hall. (Applause.) The primary object in view was to enable Mr and Mrs Carleton and their helpers to carry on the good work in which they had been engaged so ;long in Factory-yard—parochial, mission work, Sunday school classes and other meetings connected with the parish— hot he did not think that the Misses Wilson-Patten or Mr Carleton wished to adopt a dog in the manager policy, as the intention was that when the building WAS not required for the purposes he bed mentioned, now and then it could be let for entertainments of a lighter nature. (Hear, bear.) There was, however, one rather thorny question which aro.e, namely, the holding of political meetings. He had not inquired lately what the political views of the Misses Wilson-Patten were—(laughcer)—but he should not he surprised to find that they had strong feelings on one side or the other. (Renewed laughter.) Still he thought they were of opinion that it would be much better for politics to be kept outside the hall. (Hear, hear.) The best way they could show their thanks would be by co-operating together to ensure the hall being used for the best possible purposes, the well- being of the Inhabitants and of the town. (Hear, hear, and applause). _ _ _ _

The Rev E C E CARLETON, in the name of the parish, thanked the Hon Mrs Lumley for opening the ball and tor naming it, and the Misses Wilson-Patten for erect- ing it to be available, as Colonel Lumley bad said, for objects which were non-political, namely, for religious and educational purposes. The erection of this build- ing was only one of a long series of generous acts on the part of the late Lord Winmarleigh and the Patten family for the benefit of that and other parishes, in fact he did not think they were all known even to those who lived in the district and that because they had always been done quietly. (Hear, hear.) The HECTOR, proposing a vote of thanks to Mrs Lumley for her services, congratulated the Vicar and the parishioners of Holy Trinity upon having acquired such a beautiful and useful room for parochial pur- poses. Mr JonN ITESKETH seconded the resolution, which was heartily and unanimously carried. Mrs LUMLEY briefly responded.

THE MOTOR SCARE.

Warrington Police Campaign Against Excessive Speed.

-FSERAL PROSECUTIONS.

At the Warrington Police Court, on Thursday, before Messrs J Rymer Young (in the chair), W Roberts, J Upson, A J Jolley and Dr Naden, several motorists appeared in answer to summonses for driving at a speed dangerous to the public in the narrow portions of Knutsford-road and Sankey-street. The police ar<3 prosecuting in such cases chiefly with the intention of warning drivers, as any attempt at excessive speed in the streets named is exceedingly dangerous to the public. The first case was that in which Thomas Hewitt, jun., 4, Hewitt-street, was summoned for an (-Scene of dangerous driving in Sankey-street on October 80th. The Chief Constable said that the defendant was going at about 20 miles an hour but he did not wish to press the case as that was his axst offence. On being stopped the defendant had said he was only driving at six miles an hour. _ _

The case was dismissed on payment of costs and 53. into the poor box. Elliot Marcel Gorst, 42, Parkfield-road, Liverpool, pleaded guilty to having driven a motor car at a dangerous speed in Knutsford-road on October 29th. P.C. Skelton said the defendant would be travelling at 25 miles an hour from the direction of Warrington. It was in the narrow portion of the road and considering the numerous streets entering in Knutsford-road, and the people about at the time it was highly dangerous. Defendant said his father and mother were travelling In the car. His mother was nervous and did not like a great speed. He thought they were travelling about 20 miles per hour. The ,Chief Constable said he was satisfied that the defendmnt was one of the most careful drivers and he suggested that the case should be dealt with like the other,

The case was dismissed on payment of costs and ss. into .the poor box.

William' Douglas York, 170, Wargrave-road, Newton- le-Willows, was summoned for a similar offence in Knutsford-road on the 3rd November. P.C. Skelton said he saw the defendant riding a motor cycle at about 20 miles per hour past the corner of Sparling-street. Defendant : I don't recollect the case a bit ; I don't know where this place is. P.C. Tolley : It was about 200 yards down Knutsford-road.

Defendant who said he had no idea he had committed any offence until he received the summons was ordered to pay the coats of the case and 5s into the poor box. Thomas 51over, 77, Tyldesley-rcad, near Wigan, was dealt with in a similar manner for an offence in Knutaford-road.

_ Richard Coggins, 205, Winwick-road, was summoned for having driven a motor car at a speed dangerous to the public in Winwick-road on November 3rd.— Detective-Sergeant Woolacott stated that at 6-'25 he on the day in question he was in Buckley-street when he heard a motor car coming along Winwick-road at a furious He went to ..he bottom of the street and saw the motor dash past him in the direction of Central Station. The car would be travelling about 19 to 20 miles per hour. The road was very narrow and formed a bottle neck from Winwick-road to Winwick- street. There were many people about, and as there was a slight fog, the light was rather had. LECTURE ON GRAPPENHALL CHURCH.

The ancient church pf St. Wilfred, Grappenhall. one of the most interesting parish churches in Cheshire. Its history was told by the Rector (Rev H V Pigot) in a lecture before the members of the Warrington Literary and Philosiphical Society on Tuesday evening. He pointed out that the date of tbe font had been fixed by an authority as 1120. By referring to plans of two churches built about 1120 and which survived to our time—the Church of St. Michael, Newhaven, Sussex, and St. Peter's Church, Cat,sington, Oxfordshire—the rev gentleman con- veyed to his hearers an impression of the east end of Grappenhall Church as it must have appeared in the reign of Henry Ist. The next date of importance in the history of the church was 1277 (5 Edward I.), when William Boych3l died, as recorded in an' inquisitio post mottern," 111 which his son, John Boydell, aged 21 years, 7as declared to be his fathers' heir, the property consist- ing of four knights' fees and a half. That tvaa, according to Mr Barns, the knight whose effigy w,aa now found on the north side of the sanctuary. 't last crusade took place in 1270, and William- BoYdet might have taken part in that or an earlier one. His son John married Margaret Standoill and had a son, William, who founded the Boydell Chantry shortly before his death tit 1331. (8. Edward III). Ms wife, Nicolan, stlta the sister of the Rector of Grappenhall, (Richard for Robert) Fitz William de Doncaster (rector from 1311 to 1346), their father, William de Doncaster, being Mayor of Chester in 1316,1317 and 1318. A son of this William, also called William (junior), took a° oath in the Collegiate Church of St. john in Chester on the 19th January, 8, Edward 111, 1334, agreeing "to find an honest chaplain to celebrate for ever the divine mysteries for the souls of William Boydell,nl father, and Nicolan, my mother, and for the souls °L all the faithful departed, in the chapel of Grappenhale, built by the aforesaid William, my father." The Plan showed how the Norman church was altered by addition of this chapel, or chantry, in 1331. The Rector traced the gradual, developments in the structure of the church Oa roferred to the Registers which dated from the Year 1573, The old church chest, probably dating front the foundation of the Norman Church, was in the Warrington Museum. MAYOR'S SUIIDAY.

HOW CHURCH AND MUNICIPALITY CAN CO-OPERATE.

The time-honoured custom of inaugurating the civic year with attendance at divine worship was once more observed on Sunday morning when the newly- elected Mayor (Councillor Dr Joseph) and members of the Corporation paid an official visit to the Parish Church. Notwithstanding the depressing weather, there was a large and representative muster of prominent townsmen at the Town Hall where the pro- cession was formed at ten o'clock. A dash of military 1 colour was given to the scene by the presence of a detachment of Territorials, under the command of Lieut Col. B Fairclough. First came the regimental and bugle bands of the 4th Battalion South Lancashire Regiment and, in addition to a fair complement of officers and men of the main battalion, our citizen soldiers were further represented by the Army Service Corps, under Sergeant-Major Robinson. Next came two of the chief disciplinary organizations of the rising generation—the Church Lads' Brigade, in charge of Major Brooks, Captain and Adjutant Sankey and Lieutenant Bunch, and the Boy Scouts, led by Mr J B Starkey (chairman) and Scoutmasters Bennett, Morris, Clare and Shepherd. The procession also included the local corps of the St. John Ambulance Brigade, directed by Superintendent Baldry, the Borough Fire Brigade, with the veteran superin- tendent (Mr Frank Turner), and a poBBe of police com- manded by the Chief Constable (Mr Martin Nichols). Preceding the Mayor walked the sword bearer (Acting Sergeant Leonard) and the mace bearer (P.C. Plumb) Ili s Worship the Mayor hbd the Town Clerk on his right and Alderman Bolton on his left. The other members of the Corporation in tho procession were: Aldermen Bennett, Burton, John Evans, Smethurst and Tinnion ; Councillors Birtles, Chrimes, Craik, Cunnington, Dickinson, Gough, Halliwell, Hawthorn. Jenkins, Lashford, Lindley, Osborne, Peacock, Shaw, Skelland, Taylor and Wilson ; while the cfficials who attended wet e : Mr J Lyon Whittle (town elerk), Mr A T Hallaway (deputy town clerk), Mr T H Spinks (as sistant town clerk), Mr John Fairhurst (borough treasurer), Mr George F F White (clerk to the magis- trates), Mr E A White (deputy clerk), Dr G W N. Joseph (assistant medical officer), .Mr F U Ruddock (public analyst), Mr J M Murray (director of educa- tion), Mr W T Flood (chief inspector of nuisances), Mr Robert Young (cemetery registrar), Mr W S Haddeck (general manager of the gs department, Mr W S Howard (assistant secretary and cashier), F V L Mathias (electrical engineer), Mr G S Pendle- bury (school of art), and Mr J Telfer (tramways manager. The borough magistrates in attendar cc in addition to those who are members of the Couici 1. were Messrs C S Burgess, R 1' Fairclough, W Furness, A J Jolley, G Pilling, and S Wright. Other townsmen taking part in the procession included Mr George Walker (Education Committee), Mr J H J flampson(secretary of the Infirmary), Mr G Wallington, and Mr A C B:nks.

At the church a large congregation had asseinbled The proceinion was met at the west door by the clergy and choir who preceded it up the centre aisle. The clergy taking part in the service were the Rector (Rev Canon Willis) who is the Mayor's chaplain, and the Revs A J Brett, T Vickers and J M Buckmaster. As is usual at the Parish Church, the musical portion of the service was very impressively rendered by the choir and Included the anthem "Plead Thou my cause "—an English version of parts of Mozart's Twelfth Mass— which was given with beautiful effect. Mr F M Darby MEI at the organ. The Rev A J Brett preached from the words" For we are saved by hope "(Romans viii. 24) and "That ye may abound in hope" (Romans xv 13). He said Jesus Christ 'in His attitude towaids men and in His opinion of men was a radiant and invincible optimist. His optimism was not a blind and ignorant optimism, but an intelli- gent, reasoned and logical optimism. There was an optimism which was ignorant and shallow, which turned a blind eye towards all the bard,bitter and unpleasant facts of daily life,a lila prated merely about everything being as it should be in "the best possible of worlds." But the optimism of Jesus Chriot was not of that kind. It was because of His wondrous power and insight that lie could road men through and through. He hoped because His eye could glance into the secret recesses of man's heart. In the world's waste and outcast material Jesus Christ saw what nobody else saw. Beneath and behind all men's folly, sin and shame He saw glorious potentialities and possibilities. Deep down in the hamon heart, overlaid perhaps by wickedness and vice, He saw that which might be restored by grace. He hoped for every man. One of the greatest preachers had said i" When we go into a court or alley,or Into the elegant house in a favoured suburb, and find men and women sunk in animalism, trailing robes of human dignity in unnameable mire, how do we see them with the eyes of the soul? To the eye of sense they are all filthy, offensive, repellent. What are their faces like, and what sort of robes do they wear in the vision of the soul? And do we address the beast that is as the 'gentleman to be.' Are we dealing with the might be or only the thing that is •r" Continuing, the preacher said that whatever their work was, they must be optimists. They must dream dreams and see visions if their work was to be fruitful. They were co-operators and co-labourers in the matter. The Church of Jesus Christ aimed at the transformation of character by bringing to bear upon it those mystic influences which they called the grace of God, and those who had to govern their towns and cities could help them in the work of forming the character, by improving the conditions which were called environ- ment. They, the Church of Gad and Christianity, touched the man himself and those who governed the town touched the outward circumstances of his life, so that their work was one. They were co-labourers in uplifting the character of men and women. Much lad been done in this town. He had only been here a few years, but he knew that much bad been done amid great discouragement. Much remained to be done, and their progress would depend upon their vision. They must see and realize the value, dignity and beauty of human life. They must have a profound contempt and hatred for every- thing that made it difficult for men and women to rise to rise to the !possibilities of their manhood and womanhood, They must, in a word, enconrage that magnificent and glorious work of unloosening the angel in the man. He prayed that God might bless the Car- portion in the coming year in all those efforts which they put forth for the good of the town and especially in those dTrta put forth in the interests of those who were least able to help themselves. Might they abound in hope, might they have the vision,and might, by God's grace, that vision be realized. The service concluded with a verse of the National Anthem.

While the procession was returning to the Town Hall rain fell heavily and the occasion was thus shorn of much of its spectacular interest. A pleasing ceremony took place at the Town Hall when the Mayor presented Captain Waring, chief superintendent of the Warring- ton corps of the St John Ambulance Brigade, with a long service modal. Be referred to the captain's active work in the movement, extending over twenty years, and expressed the hope that his health would continue ipprove and that be might be spared for many years to enjoy the honours he so well deserved. I Captain Waring briefly returned thanks and ackuow- ledged the loyal support which he had received from all the members of the corps.

WARRINGTON NONCONFORMITY. ITS 250th 71AINIVERSARY.

When the Academy began its work in 1757

the students made their homes with the Tutors, and a sum of £l5, exclusive of certain luxuries, was fixed as the annual boarding fee. This arrangement held for ten years or so. Then in 1767, students quarters were built in Academy Place and a new era commenced.

The erection of this additional and roomy building must have seemed an ambitious venture to those who knew the slender financial resources, and the mortgage which the Trustees were obliged to create must have given them uneasy moments. But the buildings gave the Academy quite a new magnitude. The life of its students was now to be communal, and with the new conditions came the creation of the office of Rector Academm, a post which Mr. Seddon had shown himself well qualified to fill. The conduct of the students had given much concern. It was clearly the hope of the Trustees that under new conditions there might be amendment. The duties of Rector, if we may judge from the terms in which they are set forth, certainly suggest anxious dis- cussion and the consciousness that a grave difficulty is to be dealt with. They are thus defined: "To superintend the decipline and morals of the students, and in connection with the tutors to have full power to make such further reformations as might be judged expedient. An exact weekly register was ordered to be kept of the violation of the laws, which register was to be read over by

The Decline and Failure of the Academy. Student Life at Warrington.

It was a memorable morning in Warring ton when the keepers of the different_ inns.C.l MO _ _

out to gaze with confused and bewilder ed looks upon the sign boards that swing over their doors. There was reason for the amazement.

In the course of the night the "Royal Oak" had become the "Griffin," the" Nag's Head" had become the "Golden Horse Shoe " ; the- "Red Lion" was now the "Roebuck," the "Royal Oak" was the " Griffin " and the "George and Dragon" appeared as the "Eagle and Child." The students had had a busy night of it, and the town had a merry day.

A MIDNIGHT SHOCK.

Another story is told of a well-connected lady who was coming home from a ball. Her expected carriage awaited her at midnight, but in stepping into it sheibeheld a sight that froze her with terror. The footmen were devil's imps terribly adorned and carrying torches, the coachman grinned down with a a demon's face from the box ; and from the carriage stepped down a terrible figure to lend her his arm, a figure with horns, tail, and cloven feet.

===== 0002601_19121116_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
TIIE BANK-STREET GHOST.

the Rector every Saturday afternoon publicly, before all the students, and such reprimands and admonitions to be given by him and the other tutors as to them should seem necessary; if such admonitions did not prove effectual, the tutors were to proceed to appoint the deliquents a proper exercise, and onlno account to dispense with the perform- ance of it. A report from these weekly registers was to be drawn up and sent once a quarter to the Committee, who were to cause such reports to be forwarded to the parents or guardians of the student; and if this should not be effectual they were to proceed, in the next instance, to expulsion." This weekly roll call might have had terrors to boys young enough to dread the birch rod, but the Academy was dealing with those who had passed their first youth. It had no punishment save reprimand and expulsion, this last a process which might mean more loss to the Academy than the culprit. Nor could it inspire awe by its antiquity, its magnitude, or a staff of vener- able tutors. It was an institution struggling into usefulness, willing in its financial straits to receive pupils of any kind from any quarter and in the largest numbers.

Appalling rumours spread through the town of a nightly apparition in Bank-street. This quiet street was then the fashionable area of Warrington and the ghost showed every liking for good society in choosing it as the scene of his wanderings. Night after night came stories of horror. Accounts differed,but all told of a monster black as the

very night with fearful horns and fiery glow- ing.eyes. _

The inhabitants fled in terror and Bank- street must soon have been empty and ruined had not the ghost ceased his persistent atten- tions. It did not occur to the terrified ones that the back gardens of the students lodgings were within a few yards of Bank- street and that perhaps the ghost had strayed over the garden wall. It was a young man with a black ox skin and a healthy sense of mischief. Another got possession of a bear skin and appeared at night to an old woman, a seller of nuts, with distressing results. But much more tragic than the ghosts was the love-making. This had results that we still deplore.

THE WICKED MISS RIGBYS. The gentleman who held the office of "provider of the Commons" was a Mr. Rigby. He held also, more or less securely, two sparkling and handsome daughters known as Miss Lizzie and Miss Sallie who spread a very contagion' of gaiety and affection. It is very much to be suspected that they helped the contagion of mischief too. Here is one authentic fragment. _ Some students had been asked to supper.

It was a smart occasion and the table made a pretty show. There were hams, potted beef, trifles and decorated luxuries, and ladies who sat shy and demure. The students,as became gentlemen, must carve the tasty viands and serve the ladies. There were puzzled looks, explosive laughs, then uproar. The youth who served the potted beef was handing out potted sawdust, the carver of the ham was hacking at decorated wood, and the trifles were soap suds. The sober historian would probably omit such incidents as things which happened when the world was very young; but they were the ruin of the Warrington Academy. The Miss Rigbys made sad work among the students. The Trustees of the Academy were compelled to take note of them as a dis- .

turbing influence and insisted that they should be removed from the house if any student stayed there. For a while they were, but Mrs. Rigby's health failed and the young ladies came back again, to the delight of the students and the regret of the distressed and long-suffering tutors. THE LOVE EPIDEMIC.

Such students as had not lost their heads over Miss Sallie and Miss Lizzie were severely in love with Miss Anna Letitia Aikin, and for her sake (so tradition says) some of them sighed and were single all their days. Miss Aikin, who wrote many interesting and gossipy things for us about the Academy, 'says that Rousseau's " Heloise " when it appeared had dire results in Warrington. ' "Everybody instantly fell in love with everybody" is the description of what happened when Rousseau's too-strong senti- ment laid hold upon them. It was then that Mr. Barbauld made successful suit to Miss Anna Letitia and carried her away to a life of gloom. Miss Lizzie Rigby had already become Mrs. Burney and Miss Sallie went to Bath to be the wife of Dr. Parry. SERIOUS STUDENTS AND FAMOUS.

But not all the students came under the blight of frivolity. There were many who attained real eminence and some whose names are not yet forgotten nor yet likely to be. Dr. Percival, the first son of the Academy, is a Lancashire worthy • so also is Dr. Thomas Barnes; and Maithus as a political economist has a fame world-wide. Other students were Lord Ennismore, Lord Willoughby, Sir James Carnegie, Dr. Estlin, Dr. Rigby, Dr. Crompton and Justice Heywood. These names in themselves are Sufficient to awaken regard,and no critic, however hostile, with the list of its sons before him, could successfully disparage the Academy as an educational force. It had ,teachers vho could inspire great things as well as teach great things,and their character alone should have redeemed -any institution from failure. CAUSES OF DECLINE.

Failure, when it came, resulted from causes which had distressed the Academy from its ' very earliest years; insufficiency of money and insufficiency of control of its students. In 1781, after a quarter of a century of use- fulness the institution is making its last effort to gain release from debt, and can command sufficient sympathy to raise £2,500. It is the last *effort and it is unavailing. The same

year its two most enlightened servants are raising cries of alarm. Dr. Enfield, nothwith- standing financial help, feels that he is living within crumbling walls. }le puts his finger upon the real weakness in saying "I find myself confirmed in the opinion that it is impracticable in such a place as ours, where youths from 14 to 15 years of age are placed in College apartments, without any superior resident among them, and free from all domestic restraint. Irregularities have from time to time unavoidably arisen, which have at last, I am afraid, led the public to form a decided judgment against the Academy." In yet plainer language he writes •to the Trustees "An idle waste of time, a coarse and vulgar familiarity, a disposition towards riot and mischief, intemperance, and in some instances gaming, profaneness and licentious manners, have found their way into a seminary intended to train up youth in habits of sobriety and virtue." _ _

Dr Wakefield states the weakness with equal clearricss : "the youths are too young when they come, they aro.treated as men while yet but a set of wild and reckless boys they are thrown together in a large seques- tered house; the Academy is neither school nor college, it lacks the supervision given in the one and the influence and authority of the other. "

This was the real weakness and no remedy was forthcoming. . . _ _

At Midsummer 1786 after anxious and careful enquiry on the part of Trustees and Subscribers, the Academy closed its doors.

A. M.

(To be continued.)

===== 0002601_19121116_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
A DEPRESSING PICTURE.

It seems certain that the character of the students was the chief cause of its dissolution. William Turner, a student of the Academy, gives a picture of its inner life which fills one with dismay. Two sets of hot-blooded students were there, either of them sufficiently unruly to inflame all the rest. The worst were some yonng West Indians, sons of planters in Jamaica and Antigua. Almost equally unmanageable were some young and excitable Irishmen. Theirs was a brutal refractoriness that invaded the class-rooms and inflicted cruelty upon the tutors, and Mr Turner pictures the kindly Dr. Enfield openly insulted "by the dissipated and inflamed West Indian whose pastime it had been from his youth to sport with human sufferings; by the profligate outcast of our great public schools, who had learned all the evil without any of the good of those establishments, and was sent hither as a dernier ressort ; and by the pampered pet of fortune, who, from the treatment he had seen given, and been allowed himself to give, to his private tutor at horne,had learned to consider every tutor as a sort of upper servant."

===== 0002601_19121116_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
A PLACE OF RUSTICATION.

Coming into such an atmosphere the students did not take the institution seriously. Archibald Hamilton Rowan speaks of the Academy as the place where he "spent a year of rustication" from Cambridge, and it would appear that he was only one of many mho took the same light view of the Academy. Too young and ill educated when they came most of the students were affected by the prevailing lawlessness, and the Academy became for them a veritable play- ground. Mr. Seddon's strong presence was barely efficient; Dr. Enfield's voice was as one crying in the wilderness. The governors devised plan upon plan but all came to naught. Says Mr. Bright in his 'account of the Academy; "Did the students lodge, as at first in the tutor's houses—they fell in love with the young ladies, and studied any- thing rather than the divinity, and belles lettres and logic, and rhetoric, which were the supposed attractions of the place.", "Did they live all together, as afterwards they did live, in a range of college rooms they got into debt, they played mad pranks in the town, and cost Mr. Seddon and then Dr. Enfield, their Rectors, many a long hour of anxiety and wretchedness." The West Indians lamented the lost joys of their native islands, where, as one of them told the tutor, the first toy of a planters child w a ellyoungas nigger to kick." But they made u they could by imparting West Indian manners into Warrington. r as "w Political storms swept the sky in those days as they do now, and they were trying times for the tutors. Whigs and Dissenters as they were they had to endure fierce Tory enthusiasm inspired by the American war. One of the students who lodged with Dr. Enfield insisted upon illuminating his windows in honour of an American victory. The student claimed the right to light up his own windows; the doctor had to contest the point, and as master of the house give a forceful refusal.

===== 0002601_19121116_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMAN EXPORTS SINGE LAST TARIFF 1906.

Last week we compared the character of the exports and imports of manufactured goods of Germany and the United Kingdom, and showed that from 1903 to 1910 our increase in the exports of manufactured goods was greater than that of Germany by £26,000,000; while on the other hand our inorease of imports of manufactured goods was £16,000,000 less than Germany. This week by the help of Mr. Enever Todd's diagram we show that since the introduction of the New German Tariff in 1906, contrary to the expectation and design of the German Tariff Reformers, the tariff had apparently the effect of reducing the proportion of exports of manufactures and increasing the proportion of exports of raw materials and foodstuffs. This shows clearly how impotent a so-called "scientific tariff" is to change the character of exports from raw materials and foodstuffs to manufactured goods, and how as in this case it sometimes works the other way. It is significant that over the same period, without the questionable help of a tariff, our own proportion of .manufactured exports has been fully maintained. To Free Traders this is no mystery. To Tariff Reformers it must be a prize puzzle, and we would invite the Warrington Tariff Reformers to suggest some explanation.

iCHILDREN'S CORNER, Conducted by UNCLE CALEB.

THE DIFFERENCE A FLY'S LEG MAY MAKE.

I have just been reading a curious little story in an American newspaper. In the city of St. Paul a large grain company found when their books were examined that there was a shortage of cash. The amount was exactly a hundred dollars, and they tried to discover the error. Careful search did not show anything wrong in the books, but there was a hundred dollars short in money. The ledgers were sent to a firm of accountants, who went through them again and again. The firm did not like to suspect any one and spent much more than a hundred dollars in trying to find the error. Going over the figures yet once more the accountant's pencil happened to stop upon an item for 140 dollars. The pencil point rested upon the figure 1, when to the surprise of the accountant the figure broke in two and slipped down the page. This was curious, and careful examination was made. Then it was discovered that the figure 1 was a fly's leg which had become pasted in front of the forty dollar item, making it a hundred and forty. Someone had evidently killed the poor fly by shutting the book upon him. But Mr. Fly took a very costly revenge.

JUST A KIND WORD. Drop a word of cheer and kindness— Just a flash and it is gone, But there's half a hundred ripples Circling on and on and on, Bearing hope and joy and comfort On each splashing dashing wave, Till you wouldn't believe the volume Of the kind word you gave. D.op a word of cheer and kindness In a minute you forget; But there's gladm ss still a-swelling— ' And there's j y a-circling yet, And you've rolled a wave of comfort Whose sweet music can be heard Over miles and miles of wmer Ju-t by dropping a kind word. Gustavus Williams. THIS WEEK'S STORY.

At a Scotch dinner every one had con- tributed to the entertainment but Dr. MacDonald, "Come come, Doctor," said the chairman, but the doctor protested : "My voice is altogether unmusical, and resembles the sound caused by the act of rubbing a brick along the panels of a door." The com- pany attributed this to the doctor's modesty. " Very well," said he finally, "if you can stand it, lam willing." There was a pain- ful silence as the doctor sat down, broken at length by the voice of a braw Scot at one end of the table. "Mon," he exclaimed, "your singing's not up to much, but your veracity's just awful."

The Examiner, Saturday, November 16, 1912.

dangerous to the proper conduct of debate was the use of personalities. He knew that members frequently got heated—the Council Chamber was not always perfect from a sanitary point of view and that was some excuse. It was also perhaps natural that members should get heated on subjects which affected the interests of the people whom they represented. He made many allow- ances, but at the same time he hoped they would make a good resolution to avoid personalities as they would poison, because it could not possibly have a good effect and must tend to create ill-feeling and to impede the proper conduct of the business. It was highly probable i that Warrington would be honoured next summer by

A -VISIT FROM THEIR MAJESTIES the King and Queen, and if they were so fortunate he was sure he could promise, on behalf of the town, that a welcome seoond to none in the kingdom in its heartiness and loyalty would be given. (Applause.) Alluding to the administrative work entrusted to Town Councils, his Worship said their duties were very little less import- ant than those of members of Parliament. (Hear, hear and Alderman BOLTON: "Worse paid.") A member of Parliament, of course, received a salary from a grateful country and that was probably the main distinction between the legislator and the municipal administrator. They might not all agree with him, upon that point, but he could not help thinking that there was a great deal in it. The duties they had to perform were daily becoming more onerous and important. They looked after the health interests of every resident in the borough. They provided them with the two chief necessities of life—gas and water ; they conveyed them by trams to different parts of the borough ; they educated their children ; they provided them, as a Pensions committee, with old age per.sions, and at the end of their lives they found them a last resting-place. He, therefore,thought they were entitled to the gratitude of the inhabitants. The work of course, involved a large expenditure which in Warring- ton was almost invariably for the benefit of the com- munity. During the last forty-two years street improvements had cost £lOO,OOO. A great deal had been said about the errors, sins and omissions of their predecessors in the management of the business of the town.
