THE MAYORALTY. RE-ELECTION OF COUNCILLOR DR.

JOSEPH. —_ Warrington's Slum Problem.

A SCENE.

The Warrington Town Council, in common with municipal authorities throughout the country, inaugurated its year's work on Saturday with the election of Mayor. For the third year in succession Councillor Dr Joseph was unanimously chosen for the highest civic office and in returning thanks he made significat.t allusions to the E 1 um problem, repeating his arresting word picture of Bridge-street as " a veneer of splendour, covering a mass of squalor and poverty." There was a good attendance at the meet- ing which was held at noon. Pending the election of Mayor, Alderman Bolton was voted to the chair. The members of the Council present were :—The retiring Mayor (Councillor Dr Joseph), Aidermen A Bennett, John Evans, James Evans, D Tinnion, T Burton, J Smethurst, Councillors G Archer, T Dirties, J G Craik, J Chrimes, E Cunnington, S Gough, J Hawthorn, It fienshall, F Lindley, J Pemberton, P Peacock, H Roberts, J Shaw, J Skelland, S Taylor and W J Wilson.

In a brief hut eulogistic speech, Alderman BOLTON proposed the re-election of Councillor Dr. Joseph to the Mayoralty. He said that if Councillor Dr Joseph carried out his duties in the same diligent and conscientious spirit as in the past they could rest assured that they would have a very good Mayor. By previous exporience of the work he had come to understand all the failings of the members—(laughter)—and some of their good points He was closely in touch with the business of the Cor- poration and, while he accepted the position with considerable diffidence, there vas no doubt that having made up his mind to go through with it, his Worship would carry out the work to their satisfaction, (Applause.) Alderman TINNION, in seconding, said Councillor Dr Joseph had discharged all the duties of his important office to the satisfaction of the town generally. He had worked faithfully, conscientiously and assiduously. Ile (the speaker) sometimos thought that the plsition of Mayor was held too long by one individual. The honour should go round. Ile was not voicing that opinion from any personal motive, because he did not hesitate to say that there were several members of the Council who could have held the position had tley desired it, but personally he neither de..ired nor wanted it—at any rate for some time to come. Ile was simply enumerating what he thought was a very sound prin- ciple when he said that the honour should go round. But if there ever was a good and valid reason why the occ..ipant of the chair should be asked to accept office for a third year they had it on the present occasion in the earnest and conscientious way in which Councillor Dr. Joseph had discharged the many and varied duties. The coming year would be a memorable one. The most important event would be the King's visit—at any rate he hoped it would be an important event, and not a flying motor visit. Ile trusted the Mayor and those ass Iciated 'with him in bringing the matter forward would represent the

===== 0002601_19121116_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
G REA T DISAPPOINTMENT

that the people -olVitriing-Con-r;nd- -neighbourhood had suffered on the occasion of the late King's visit. They were entitled to some consideration on that account and the visit thould to so arranged that the people should have the opportunity of showing their heart- felt loyalty to his Majesty. (Applause.) The alderman next expressed the hope that Warrington Bridge would be completed during the Mayor's term of office. There was a great deal of scepticism and doubt as to the progress that was briog made, but he thought everybody would admit that considerable head- way had been toads with the first portion of the struc mire. Questions affecting the public health and town planning, in which the Mayor was very deeply interested, would also come tefore the Council. Ile felt sure every member would heartily cooperate with his worship in every possible direction for the pros- perity and improvement of the town which, although it was sometimes called hard names, was in his opinion one of the best business towns and one of the best managed municipalities in England. (Applause). Alderman SMETHURST supported the proposition. Referring to the demands upon tha Mayoral purse, he said only those who had occupied the pention knew what expense it entailed. It would have given great satisfaction to all the inhabitants if it bad been found possible to complete Warrington Brigde in time for the King's visit. No-one knew better than Lord Derby how disappointed the people of Warrington were that the late King, on the occasion of his visit, was not able to spend a little longer in the town. As to the completion of the bridge, he (the speaker) had had great faith—in fact he lost one hat on the strength of the first pert of the bridge being opened this year. (Laughter.) By working night and day and using double machinery, it might have been possible to build the bridge in time for it to to opened by the King, but the expense would have been enormous. lie (the speaker) did not feel any disposition to force on the work under such con- ditions, as it would have been a waste of money.

The MAYOR, in returning thanks to the Council, said he appreciated the honour of re-election very highly but he also realized the heavy responsibility of the office. One did not set more accustomed to that aspect of the work as the years glided by. He could claim frankly that, according to his own conscience, he had endeavoured to do his very best to fulfil the duties of the office. The Lonour of the position collectively belonged toi the Council who conferred it and, that being so, it WAS in their interests that they should con- tinue to help him as loyally as they had done in the past, to uphold the dignity of tho most important position in the borough. He wished to speak some- what plainly on one or two points affecting the conduct of the business of the chair. When they got upon topics which interested them particularly, they were apt to go in for rhetoric and wax eloquent upon them, but he had always allowed cousiderable latitude, as long as a fair amount of relevance to the subject was maintained. JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

MAYORAL SUNDAY REFLECTIONS.

On Sunday, after witnessing the Mayoral procession, I came across a printed scrap on which was the timely observation to the effect that one has only to turn to the costumes of bygone generations to see how, in the matter of male attire, all ranks of society outclassed us from an testhetic point of view. Surely many spectators of the annual Church parade of our " city fathers,"in their ugly polished "tiles" and habilaments generally, are profoundly thankful that the Mayor at least must on these occasions appear in his IE6BI regalia, and that there are certain other picturesque figures to relieve the situation with splashes of colour. A portrait in oils of his Worshiyi in robes and chain would obviously be more attractive than one of Dr. Joseph in ordinary, everyday, civilian dress. One does not need expert assistance to that conclusion. It has been truly remarked that while the "well-dressed" man of to-day considers himself a model of style, the aatist who, fur the sake of filthy lucre, is compelled to introduce him into his picture, racks his brain to know how he is to partly hide him out of sight and cover up his inelegancies. If it were not for military uniforms and the varied hues introduced by the ladies, what a depressing affair such an open-air function as that of Sunday last would be ! THE "DOMES."

Time was when it was the invariable rule for the newly elected mayors to lead the procession to their respective palish churches, but the gradual stiffeuing of Nonconformity has finally and irrevocably changed all that, and nowadays the November municipal parades are to the Anglican, the Methodist, or the Congregational Church. according to the religious connections or preference of the Miyor for the time being. HOW ITS DONE.

Last week's story in the " Children's Corner" brought to my mind an interview I or.ce had with a famous mycologist. He told me a good deal about fungi of which I was previously unaware, asserting among other things that many varieties habitually avoided as poisonous are edible. " But" pursued I, " how is one to identify these? Suppose I gather what to my untrained eye appear to be mushrooms, how am Ito be assured that they are not really dangerous toadstools ? " " Why" was the reply, " Thats's very simple, try them on your wife! " Is it necessary to add that the eminent botanist was him- self a confirmed bachelor? CONCERNING CHOIRS.

The choir strike in connection with St. Michael's Runcorn, somehow recalled for me a recent observa- tion by Sir J Compton-Rickett : "In practical life there are three things which need handling with extreme care. They are gunpowder, choirs and boys.' Let me just repeat a little refererce made by a lively New Church minister whom I happen to know. It is sometimes asserted that members of church choirs do not take an interest in the services except from the point of view of a musician. Indeed, on- friend once heard it said in praise of a New Church preacher, "He made even the choir listen to him." He declares he has himself always fcund choristers just as devout as the other worshippers. They may perhaps take a surreptitious glance at the church journals during the delivery of the sermon, but one has known deacons to be guilty of similar conduct. Yet his experience evidently differs from that of many other people. The following hints to choir members, taken from an Ametican Church Magazine, have evidently been pro- voked by the unseemly conduct of those who above all others should be devout and orderly in their behaviour in church : —Rehearsals may begin any time between eight and nine o'clock. On Sunday, when the time comes for the anthem, don't all rise as one person, but one by one ; this gives a sort of rag- time effect which is much more novel than the usual way. Come in late ; the later the better. If you are almost on time the congreg tion may wonder why you couldn't have been early. Look around ti ith a smile ; greet all your friends. Never mind if the service has begun ; it's only a church service you are attending. Ask if your hat is on straight ; the organist will be delighted to take down his glass for you to see for yourself. During the service do not listen to the sermon ; remember you are in the choir and don't need any advice.

TITE DAN OF TIANKRTJPTCY.

Having widened Bridge-street it was a great pity that they had left the adjoining property as it was. To use the phrase he had previously applied to it, Bridge- street was a veneer of splendour covering a mass of squalor and poverty. fbere were greater evils then narrow streets to be remedied and he had been extremely dislppointed with the lukewarm support he had received on certain questions affecting sanita- tion: Ile did not say it reproachfully but he mentioned it conscientiously from the point of view of duty. On visiting the different wards on November Ist he was pleased to note, from the mural literature, that both parties were taking a great interest in providing BETTER DWELLINGS FOR .THE POOR.

That consoled him a great deal and he looked forward to the members fulfilling those promises. His Worship touched upon the provision of municipal dwellings for workingmen by the Corporations of .Yeovil and Newcastle-on-Tyne. He advocated a similar scheme for Warrington two years ago, and be regretted that they had allowed Newcastle and Yeovil to forestall them and to steal their clothes. In Warrington they had no parks except %bat they had bought. In St. Helens they were pretty well off for parks, but they had been given to them, whereas Warrington people had had to pay for their., which made a great difference. The Council had therefore some uphill work in providing open spaces. Emphasising the !lied for rigid economy, his Worship specially alluded to education, lie regarded it as a waste of space and money to build a new school and empty others which were satisfactory from a structural and sanitary point of view. With reference to the Insur- ance Act, whatever opinions they might have as to the merits or de-merits of that measure, it was now a law of the realm and they had to administer it efficiently with a view to helping those whom it was intended to benefit. Finally, his Worship touched upon the Town Planning Act and the promise of co-operation from adjoining authorities, and said they had every possible evidence of good auguries for the future in carrying out the provisions of the Act for the benefit of War- rington and the surrounding district. (Applause). The Mayon then appointed Alderman Porshaw as his deputy and the Rev Canon A MN as his chaplain.

TIIE LATE MRS. FORSIIAW.

Before the Council rose, a resolution was passed recording the regret of the members at the death of Mrs Forsbaw, the ea-Mayorese, and condoling with the family. The MAYOR, in moving the resolution, said Mrs Forshaw's acts of kindness would be remembered and her memory respected for many years to come. Further tributes to Mrs Forshaw's goodness of heart and unfailing sympathy and tact were forthcoming from Aldermen Bennett, James Evans and Burton, Councillors flenshall, Pemberton and Roberts. Alderman BENNFNT said Mrs Forshaw's life was a reminder that goodness was better than greatness and was the one thing that really counted. Ihe resolution was passed in silence, the members standing.

sturdy Nonconformists is a matter for sincere rejoicing and the fearless independence of our dissenting Mayors is wholly commendable; but it is p-ssible to " protest too much " after this fashion. I once lived in a borough that straggled along three narrow valleys winding along through a chain of hills, and during my residence the Chief Magistracy was occupied for a term by a worthy member of a small Methodist community at one of the extremities— a couple of miles or so from the Town Hall. His Worship must needs have the Sunday morning pro- cession to his own little Bethel, and, although rain had fallen during the previous night and the roads were very muddy, quite a number of his fellow members and officials and others made a brave attempt to reach "the distant scene." The top hat, sacred to such occasions and indispensable, is an article with which wet weather is capable of playing such fan- tastic tricks before high heaven as are calculated ta make the very angels Weep; and tie wearers had not proceeded far on their contemplated long tramp when the rains again descended and the floods came, and drove them to shelter in consternation and dismay. For the Mayor, a cou2le of aged aldermen, and the Town Cleilt a cab was hurriedly requisitioned, and such of the general body as were unable to obtain vehicular-accommodation and ' yet resisted the temptation to return, struggled along in disgust, uttered or unexpressed, but manifest and I natural. The whole thing was a humiliating failure —and might so easily have been avoided, too, without violation of conscience or sacrifice of principle. Some- times we Nonconformists sorely lack saving sehse of humour. • ANOTHER POOR SPURT.

What time Viscount Wolmer was desporting himself in the House of Commons last night week, Mr B S Townroe was lamenting his Lordship's absenca from a meeting which he had arranged to address in that part of the Middleton Division in which the prospec- tive Tory candidate has been in residence for three months or so. lam told that the engagement had been entered into several weeks before, and that, elated by their supposed capture of a real live Viscount, the promoters not only covered the local hoardings with huge placards gleefully announcing their triumph in fat capitals, but actually enclosed in large' envelopes copies of the handbill advertising the meeting and bad them delivered to householders. Notwithstanding these tremendous efforts to excite a little ropular enthusiasm on behalf of their discredited cause when the opening hour struck there was but a beggarly array of empty benches. It was not far short of half-past eight when Mr. Townroe and his friends appeared upon the platform, and by that time the hall—certainly one of the most commodious in those parts—was not half filled. More- over, as previously intimated, there was no Lord Weimer alter all ! Later in the evening the gentleman who won the Oldham bye-election for the party, appeared in his Lordship's room and stead, and nearly bored several hearers to death. A reporter who was present says it was all very tedious and wearisome, save and except an occasional interval for indulgence in some thundering political taradiddle—and at that sort of thing more than one of the speakers seem to have been as ready as an acquaintance who this very week ' solemnly assured me he had been obliged to leave a certain house because he could hear the lady next door putting her stockings on ! THOSE INSURANCE MEN.

The companies' disclaimer with reference to Mr Peter Delaney and the Town Hall Ward election reminded me of an experience of my own which shows to what lengths insurance agents may go now they are organised and have their own union. In one town ' with whose affairs I was personally intimate a certain Company had a superintendent whose methods made him extremely uupopular and obn, xious to the men under him.Lle reported two of their number for what he was pleased to regard as" shady tricks" and the pair were dibmissed. The members of the Union there- upon set to work to accumulate evidence again him and produced several well-authenticated cases of gross misrepresentation and fraud on his part. Reports were sent to headquarters, hot the Union could not be , recognised, and for that reason the cffender continued unmolested for some time longer. Then the whole story was set out in four large pages of close print in language such as no newspaper dare have published, and copies were distlibuted from door to door in the district for which the superintendent was responsible. Of course this was done over the signature of the Trade Union secretary, an independent individual with plenty of money behind him with which to fight the agents cause. Still, it was a bold stroke, calcu- lated to turn olive green with envy a harrassed and handicapped journalist who has suffered many things because of Our fearful and wonderful law of libel as affecting newspapers.

WOMEN'S GHAT.

King George and Buckingham Palace.—Lord Esher makes an interesting reference to the King and Buckingham Palace in his introduction to " The Girl- hood of Queen Victoria," of which a charming picture is given in the two volumes which Lord Esher has edited. They ccntain a selection from the diaries of Queen Victoria from the year 1832 to 1810. The Queen, then Princess Victoria, was only thirteen when the diary commences, and her opinions and experiences, the romance of her girlhood, and the story of her life, are carried down to 1840, the 3ear of her marriage with Prince Albert. Lord Esher gives summaries of the various chapters, and in one of these introductions, he tells 1.13 that King George would like to pull down Buckingham Palace,round off St. James's and the Green Parks at Constitution Hill, and reconstruct Kensington Palace, as the town house of the Sovereign. The palace which has been temporarily transferred into a museum, is memorable as having been the scene of the death of one Queen, and the birth of another. For it was here that Qtieen Anne find in 1714, and Queen Victoria was born in 1819. Lord Esher refers to the project as " King George's dream," but it could easily be carried out without any espouse to the nation. The gardens of close upon fifty acres, attached to Bucking- ham Palace, would afford immensely valuable sites, and the sum which the whole property would realise, would be ample to build a palace worthy of the Sovereign of the world's greatest Empire. Growing Spendthrift Ilabits.—Notwithstanding the high cot of living, the spending power of the general public, as regards 'both the necessaries of life, and the' luxuries, is increasing. There is evidence on all sides to this effect, and the Government statistics are alone sufficient to prove it. The habit of spending more is especially marked among the rich and well-to-do classes in London, where home life is changing much more than it is in the provincial t..)wns. Lunching and dining out at restaurants, is an every-day habit with people who used to take their meals at home as a matter of course. Another extravagance is the taxi-cab habit, which has spread among people who seldom if ever thought of taking a hansom. Motoring, in fact, seems to have ushered in an age of luxury and extrava- gance, of which there is more or less evidence among all grades of society. The result is shown in the higher charges at theatres, music halls, hotels, restaurants, and the doubled and trebled scale of tips expected by waiters, and the higher cost of shopping generally. All this, it may he urged mainly affects the better-provided classes, but it has its counterpart in the larger purchas- ing power of the masses, which is the cause, rather than the effect of the higher coat of every-day household ' necessaries and commodities.

The Electric Cooking Bange.—The gas companies made war upon the kitchen range by offering to supply gas cookers to the public on the cheap hire system, and some of the more enterprising of the electric supply companies in London, propose to let out electric ranges on similar terms. Hitherto it has been necessary to purchase the range outright, which people are reluctant to do,especially as there is a general belief that cooking by electricity is very expensive. This it is said, is not the case where the price per unit of electricity is low or if, as is usual, it is supplied at a lower rate for cook- ing, than for lighting. The system, of course, has many advantages, and cleanliness alone,is sufficient to recom- mend It. There is no dirt, or imperfect combustion, as when coal, gas, or oil, is used, and there is but little escape of heat in the room. The oven has three heats, high, medium, and low, regulated by switches and it is uniformly distributed in the oven, so that ;here is no need for shifting or turning the baking dishes about. The great majority of people are slow to adopt up-to- d tte household arrangements, not only because of the trouble and initial expense, but also because they doubt whether the advantages are not outweighed by draw- -1 backs, which they only dissover by finding out for themselves. In this advertising age there are often grounds for such suspicions, but many housewives would rejoice if the supply companies could demon- strate the practicability of electric range cookery, at a cost which would not compare unfavourably with I existing methods. Revival of the Fur Cape.—Time was when the ambition of many a woman was gratified by the posses- sion of a fur shoulder cape. But it was a bulky affair, and by no means graceful. In the interval of the time that has elapsed since Vie fur cape was worn, by those ' who could afford to purchase it, and the present day, furriers have accomplished marvels with skins. The peltry of to-day is as pliable, snd lies in folds as soft as satin or lace, instead of being as stiff as the pro- ' varbial board. Thus the cape that has just made its reappearance is quite a different garment from that which gratified its proud possessors some twenty years ago. An ermine cape soft as velvet, and white as snow, when worn with a huge muff to match, is both rich. looking, and very becoming. It is also luxurious, and within the reach of only the very rich. For those of limited means, these are however quite attractive imitations, and there is little doubt that the cape of fur will be given a warm welcome. The Tailored Frock and Coat, —Every woman willingly acknowledges her indebtedness to the tailor whose inventive genius dowered hr with her tailor-made garments, without which life in this country would he difficult. Another gift almost as valuable, which, so far, has met with but scant appreciation, is the tailored frock and coat. Doubtless, like its predecessor, it will come into its own, as time goes on ;it certainly deserves to do so, for it is not only convenient but elegent, whilst its artistic possibilities are mending. Its general adoption would mean the disappearance of the blouse, which will die hard, since it admits—with the coat and skirt—of so many changes and varieties— soda love of both is inherent in human nature. The lack of enthusiasm shown regarding the tailored frock and coat may be thus accounted for. With a suit of this description, the corsage resembles a dainty blouse, for it is usually made of ninon or some similar fabric, whilst the material forms the trimming. For the woman of solid proportions this style offers undoubted advantages. Winter Dresses on French Lines—livery winter we depart more and more from tradition in the matter of winter dresses, and clothe ourselves after the manner of our neighbours across the Channel. This winter drosses are composed of light, dainty and summer materials, apparently most unsuitable to these northern latitudes. Not so very long ago,as winter approached,every woman considered it imperative to lay in a stoo'r of heavy cumbersome winter gowns of serge or some similarly thick, woollen material. These had sepsnte bodices, heavily trimmed with velveteen, voluminous skirts, and ample sleeves; and their weight was intolerable. Thanks to the advent of thellong coat of fur, or its imitations, d therys ears—unconsciousl Plush •or velvet coat, the dtiatiest, thinnest dr esses may be warn without discomfort on the coldeJt days. Hence it has come about that we have of y perhaps—adopted French styles in our choice of winter dresses. An Economical Arrangement.—Nor need the adoption end estsee of this idea lead to extravagince ; on the contrary, it makes for economy, for it obviates the necessity of putting away dainty gowns as soon as the first first has made its appearance, and enables the wearer to get the best out of them, whilst they are in fashion, thus saving the expense of remodelling when summer comes again. But there are many of us who are so constituted that to wear a thin dress on a cold day is positive misery. These are the shivery mortals, and for them Dame Fashion has this 3 ear provided the most delightful gowns of lovely velvet, in which they may look and feel deliciously comfortable sal warm. A favourite combination of materials for these dresses is velvet and taffetas, and as mivtures are de rionrr, the collars are frequently made of ration, which is embroidered in t.ilks to match the colours of the gown. In shape the collars are square and have slender points on the shoulders.

COUNTY COUNCIL LECTURES ON HORTICULTURE.— On Monday evening last Mr H Li James, of Holmes Chapel College, gave a very interesting lecture on the cultivation of the garden potato. This is the second of a series of lectures now being given in the Council School at Grappenhall k'y Mr James. In a very lucid and interesting mariner the lecturer dealt very ably with every aspect. The soil and its preparation, manur- ing, planting and fertilizing. He strongly advocated the use of che.oical manures with farmyard manure, and substantiated his arguments in a very convincing manner. Potato diseases and remedies 'were fully gone into. Much useful information was given on the ] different varieties of potatoes for certain purposes and according to different natures of the soil. Mr A Lister Kaye presided end thanked the lecturr r for the very I able manner in which be dealt with the subject. FATAL TRAP ACCIDENT.—On Thursday evening Broom Edge was the scene of a fatal trap accident. At about 9 o'clock Mr. Bert Whitlow, of Deans Green, came out of Mr C Gleave's provision stores, where he had been purchasing groceries, drove off in his trap in the direaion of his home. Some time later an Irishman found a man lying in the road in a pool of blood, with his head badly crushed. He ran back to Broom Edge and gave an alarm. When helpers ran to the spot where the accident bad occured about 100 yards away, they found the injured man was Mr Whitlow. They at once carried him to the surgery of Dr White who telephoned to Dr Thorpe. Having been attended, he was conveyed to his .own house. Unfortunately his injuries were so severe that he pissed away at 2 am, yesterday. The cause of the accident is at present unknown though several ..heories have been advanced. One of the most probable is that owing to "ne of the horses' shoes being loose, the animal swerved round, throwing Mr Whitlow out of the trap on to his bead The conveyance then dashed into Mr Percival's hedge where the shafts broke and the horse became free. One of the strange facts in the case is that the horse, on becoming free, ran back again down the road through Broom Edge and on to Heatley where it rushed into the yard of Mr W Whitlows brother. The deceased, who had been married only 18 months, had taken over his mother's fa rrn at Deans Green and was doing very well. He was highly respected and much sympathy is felt with the I widow in her bereavement.

A GRUESOME DISCOVERY.—WhiIst Mr Phillip Leather, one of the men employed by Mr Harry Fair- clougb, was excavating in the new road on the West side of the Lake on Thursday afternoon of last week, his spade brought to light a gruesome discovery in the form of a skull. The skull was very much decayed, only the front portion, ie. the forehead, cheek and jaw bones remaining, though some of the teeth were in an excellent state of preservation. Strange to say, no other bones were unearthed. The skull was found in sandy soil about three feet below the surface of the field, on the opposite to the temporary office,. about two hundred yards distance from the entrance to the read.

THE B.W.T.A.—A LYAIM BRANCH.—For some time there has been a wish that Lymm should possess a Branch of the National British Women's Temperance Association, and on Wedited,ay afternoon this desire found a practical form through the kindness of Mrs G L Welford, who entertained a number of ladies at her residence," Bleak House,' for the purpose of meeting Mrs Shilston-Watkins (President of the Liverpool Union) and Miss Cherry thou secretary of the Cheshire County Union). BOY SCOUTS' "RAID."—On Saturday afternoon and evening last, the Heatley Troop of the Baden Powell Boy Scouts under the command of Scoutmasters T Nash and James Webb Jim., entertained troops of scouts from High Legh (Scoutmaster C Surridge), Broadheath (Scoutmaster Wolstenholme), Brocklauds (Scoutmaster Turner), and Hale (Scoutmaster Stokes). The chief event was a raid OD the " Saracens Head " by about sixty scouts of the last three named troops who were opposed by a defending force of fifteen members tad) of the Heatley and High Legh troops, After a strenuous battle, which contained many thrilling moments, the attackers were driven off and overwhelmingly defeated as shown by the following official news Defenders lost five men but captured twenty-six opponents." The decisive battle over, the diplomats of both parties got to business, peace was soon declared, and toe combatant foregathered in the room adjoining the " Saracen's Head " where an enjoy- able tea was partaken of. This was afterwards followed by a bon-fire, fireworks and the attendant fun. A sing- song brought the happy outing to a close. • MAY QuEEN FESTIVAL MATTERS.—The annual general meeting of the Lymm May Queen Festival was held in Hurst's Pavilion on Tuesday night under the Presidency of Mr J D B Smith. The rules of the Festival Committee that bad been formed at the last meeting were adopted, as was the balance sleet, after a few questions and explanations had been given. In his brief report Mr Hudson, the treasurer, said that the efforts of the committee to reduce expenses had resulted in a saving of £3l over that of the last festival. It was the general belief of those present that had the coin- committee not lost the afternoon's gate through the inclement weather they would have had a substantial balance in hand, and were full of optimism for the coming year. Several methods of cleariog the present radverse balance were put forward and it was ultimately decided to hold one or more concerts during the present winter, a sub-committee being formed for that purpose. Votes of thanks to the retiring officers were passed and the following positions for the ensuing year were filed by :—President, Mr J B Smith ; vice-president and treasurer, Mr A II Hudson ; bon secretary, Mr J Webb, Jon; auditors, Messrs T S Brown, and W H Blount. DEATH OF Miss E. PEARSON.—After several years' painful illness Miss Edith Pearson, aged 26, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. W. For many years F. faithful member of the Wesleyan Church and a teacher in the Sunday School, Miss , Pearson had endeared herself to all who knew her ; especially so was this the case with those who were cognisant of the great affliction she bore with such exemplary patience, and her presence will be greatly missed. THE WATERWORKS UNDERTAKING.

Bill to be Promoted.

Although the proceedings at the special meeting of the Lymm Urban District Council on Wednesday even- ing were of a purely formal character,they were fraught with great possibilities to the future development of Lymm. The negotiations with Sir Wm Lever have resulted in the decision of the Council to acquire the waterworks and a large portion of the estate for public purposes, and in order to carry out the scheme a Bill is to be promoted in Parliament. Wednesday's meeting was called to " consider the propriety of resolving upon the promotion if a Bill in the next session of Parliamennt," but it was evident that the members of the Council had made lip their minds on the question and a few minutes sufficed to agree to the Parliamentary procedure suggested. Mr W G Groves presided and there was also present :—Messrs h.L. Davies, It H Gotobed, 8 W Gould, G L Welford, 0 G Smith, H Holr, F Lockyer, W Mullard (clerk) and T Ridg- way (solicitor). The Chairman said that the statutory meeting had been called to conform to the Borough Funds Act, before they presented a Bill to Parliameut for the purchase of the waterworks. He would ask Mr Ridgway to explain the meaning of the meeting. Mr Ridgway said the meeting was known as the statutory meeting and the object was to pass a resolu- tion authorising the promotion of a Bill for the following, among other purposes:— To authorize and empower the Council to purchase and acquire by agreement the undertaking, rights, powers and properties of the Lymm Waterworks Com- pany (hereinafter referred to as the Water Company), and to make provision for the trs.usfer to and vesting in the Council of the said undertaking, rights, powers and properties. To authorize and empower the Council to purchase and acquire by agreement from the Lymin Estate Development Company, Limited, certain lands, rights, and easements within the Urban Districts of Lymm, and to provide for the vesting of the same in the Po authorize the Council to carry on the undertaking of the Water Company, to maintain, improve, alter, extend and enlarge the existing Waterworks and to extend the existing limits of supply of the Water Com- pany, and to supply water within such extended limits, and to do and execute all works necessary for those purposes. To authorize the Council to acquire by agreement additional lands for the purposes of the said water undertaking or any easement, right or privilege over or upon such lands. To extend the Council's limits for the supply of gas and to make further provision in regard to the gas undertaking of the Council and the supply of gas within the Council's ex•sting and extended limits of supply. To confer on the Council powers and to make further provisions with respect to Streets, Buildings, Sewers, , Drains, Infectious Disease, Sanitary and other matters, and for the improvement of the district. To authorize the Council to make provision with I regard to Recreation and Pleasure grounds. To confer on the Council such further powers and to make such further provisions as may be necessary or expedient to give effect to the objects and purposes of the said Bill, and to empower the Council to borrow moneys for the purposes of the said Bill, and to apply their property, fends, rates and revenues for such purposes, and to amend all or any of the Acts or Orders relating to the said district Or the Water Company, or the matters aforesaid; and of visolving that the costs and expenses of and Incidental to the promotion of the said Bill shall be defrayed in the first place out of the General District Rate and ultimately out of moneys to be borrowed under the provision of the Act. Mr Welford moved the resolution authorising the promotion of the Bill. Mr Davies seconded and, on being put to the meeting, the resolution was unanimously agreed to.

STOCKTON HEART.

MOTOR DRIVER AND INJURIES TO A HORSE.—At the Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday before MeEars Hervey Talbot (in the chair), Roger C Parr, CI Dakin, NV Fletcher, 1' Baxter, H (.4 Roberts, C Jackson Holmes and J J Whitley, John Vernon, Dundonald- avenue, Stockton Heath, was summoned hr being the driver of a motor car and failing to stop when signalled to do so by a person in charge of a horse at Daresitury on September 16th. Defendant, who was represented by Mr Dean (Liverpool), pleaded not guilty.—Supt. linnion said the driver was in the employ of Messrs Greetiall, Whitley and Co., Ltd., and was in charge of a motor lurry, which was proceeding in the direction of Preston Brook. Mr Ferguson, a Warrington veterinary surgeon, was driving from Dares- bury to Warrington. He signalled to the defendant to stop his engine. When near to Mr Ferguson the defendant shut of the motive power stopping opposite the trap, but at that time Mr Ferguson's horse had got on the rails at the side of the road, and received severe injuries. —Mr Ferguson said he first saw the motor about forty yards away. When the pony got alongside the waggon the vehicle wae still moving. The horse swerved ' round and got on top of some rails.—Cross- examined: The horse was not restive before it got to the motor waggon. Ills complaint was that the motor did not draw up soon enough.—Mr Dean said the defendant saw the horse coming along, and he saw Mr Ferguson put up his whip. He drew up slowly in order not to make any noise and frighten the horse. —Defendant said he could have drawn up more quickly by putting on the compression brakes, but they made a noise, and be thought it would have made the horse wove. He drew op in ten or fifteen yards. His sole object in drawing up slowly to the side was to assist the driver. When he stopped the horse was eight or ten yards away, and was still going.—Richard Beck, who was with Vernon, corroborated.—The case was dismissed.

PAROCHIAL COMMITTEE. The monthly meeting of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee wee held in the Hall Cafe on Tuesday evening, Mr G Pilling presiding. There were also present :—Messrs J H Clarke, J MoDermid, F Pearson, J Whitley, G J Brown, F Warburton, C Hallows, J Hallows, J It Wood, W Mclntyre, G F Ashton (clerk)t S R Wright (surveyor), and J Farrington (inspector). THE HOARDINGS QUESTION DEFERRED, The Clerk read A letter from the Clerk to the County Council with reference to the resolution passed by the Committee at their last meeting in respect of the hoardiogs at the Twenty Steps Bridge, stating that he would submit the resolution to the Standing Orders Committee of the Council. He said, however, that the Committee, when recommending the Council to make the by-laces which !lad already been published did not overlook the question of hoardings in unsuitable places, hut they felt, having regard to the terms of the Act, that they were not in a position to make a by-law to deal with such hoardings.—Other letters were read from the Manchester Ship Canal Company acknowledging the receipt of a copy of the resolution, and further discussion on the matter was deferred.

_ STREETS WHERE THERE ARE NO HOUSES. An objection was made by Councillor. Pemberton on his own and other property owners' behalf in respect. to orders having been issued relative to the making of certain back passages. Mr Pemberton's objection ass that the orders had been issued before the houses were completed and be could not get tenants for them because they did not want to have the obligation of having to make the passages. It was a great hardship as be himself had expended about £175 on the passages he objected about. If they were going to enforce the making of streets before the houses were tenanted people would assuredly refrain from purchasing the property. The Clerk pointed out that notices which had been served on Mr Pemberton had been returned by Messrs Forshaw and Forshaw, saying the property dicl not belong to him.

THE NEW SEWAGE SCHEME.

Is the Scheme Too Costly P "I feel quite sure that the Local Government Board will tell you that it is not so much a question of expense as a question of public health and the neces- sities of the neighbourhood." This stgtement was made during a discussion on the proposed Padgate Sewerage Scheme by the Clerk (Mr J C Sutton) at a special meeting of the Warrington Rural District Council on Wednesday at ternoon. The meeting had been specialty convened in reference to the pmposed scheme and Mr Mather presided. There were also present the Rev A M Mitchell, Messrs A Brooks, J Backhouse, W Furness, J Ball, P Longton, J W Knowles, J C Sutton (clerk), B S Newcombe (surveyor), and 1-1 H Burnage (assistant surveyor). .

'I ho report ot the consulting engineer, Mr .3 Commin (Westminster, S.W.), dated November 411, which was circulated amongst all the members of the Council, stated that :—" As verbally reported at your committee meeting on Wednesday last, the scheme prepared by your Surveyor shoes the best possible arrangement of gradients for gravitating the sewage to one outfall. It unfortunately demonstrates the impracticability of dispensing with MOD,'6 the sewage if the Local Govern- ment Board are to be satisfied that the necessary Purification Worlis are beyond the reach of floods—a point on which they invariably insist ; also the limited margin of fall left at the outfall above the ordinary level of the river would entail the surface area of the works being increased in a manner which would greatly augment the cost. _Further, the site would require to be excavated for a depth of at least five feet, before reaching the surface level of the works. For thee reasons, more especially the first, it becomes necessary to absndon the idea of trusting to gravitation alone for the collection of the sewage. The scheme I have outlined combines the use of gravitation as far as practicable, with an arrangement of ejectors for lifting the sewage at various points. t he whole of the sewage of the district will be brought by the sewers provided to a point in Paddington-road opposite Field No. 257 on Ordnance plan. From this point it will be lifted by ejectors through a rising main to the Purification Works, which will be situate on the southern half of this field, and for the most part entirely above the present ground line. To provide the power for the ejectors, an engine-house with gas or oil engines air compressors and air storage cylinders, will be erected on the same site, and air mains carried there- from to the various ejectors. The engines and ejectors now provided to suffice for dealing Aith the flow of sewage from double the pre- sent population, to be of the most modern and efficient type, and all in duplicate so as to comply with the requirements of the Local Government Board in that respect, and provide reserves in Case of breakdown. The purification works to be designed on thoroughly efficient and up-to-date lines, and arranged to as to be installed in three sections as required by increasing populations. The following are my estimates of cost: (a) The complete scheme as described, to serve the district when fully developed (that is, four times the present population): :•-ewers, junctions, manholes, ventilating shafts, rising mains, etc., £4.471 ; ejector chambers and engine house, £441 ; air mains, values and fittings. £460 ; engines, ejectors, air compressors and storage cylinders £2,261 ; reinstating roads after laying sewers and air mains, fencing, lighting and watching, £284 ' • wayleaves £5O ; sewerage purification works, £5,060 ; tote £13,270 ; (b) The sections which it is desirable to instal forthwith sewers etc., omitting portions not pro- , vided for in Mr hewcombe's estimates and presumably not required at present. £3,329 ; ejector chambers and engine house, £304 ; air mains, valves, fittings r&c., £257 ; engines, ejectors, air com- pressors and storage csclinders, £1,451 ; reinstating roads, fencing, lighting, watching, &c., £lBl ; way- leaves, £25; sewage purification works for 33 1-3 per cent, more than present population, £1,35-0 ; total, £7,397.—T0 either of the foregoing 10 per cent, for contingencies, and the cost of the site of, say, four acres, should be added, to arrive at totsl cost of scheme exclusive of engineering and legal expenses, viz., £1,539. The working expenses I estimate as follows :—(a) For the complete scheme serving four times the present population, £6 lOs per week ; (b) for the scheme to be installed at present, £3 2s 6d per week.

TOO COSTLY FOR THE PARISH. At the meeting on Wednesday mention was made of the expense of the scheme to the parish and the opinion was exprrssed that it was too costly for the parish to stand Mr Bro ,ks said they were doing it at the behest of the Local Government Board. Would it not be as well for them to send to the Local Government Board a few of the figures and ask them if in view of the fact that a penny rate only produced £22, whether they were still anxious that they should go on with the scheme. The Clerk : You will be told that you most do it under the law. Mr Mather : That is exactly what they want. When the inspector comes down we shall have to ask him if be is prepared to grant us 60 or 80 years in which to repay the loan. Mr Furness said they should try to get out of the difficulty by extending the loan. The Clerk : I feel quite sure that the Local Govern- ment Board will tell you that it is not FO much a ques- tion of expense as a question of publics health and the necessities of the neighbourhood. Mr Brooks ; Our hands are forced then. Mr Backhouse : There is no doubt something must be done in Padgate. Thirty years will be no use to us. Mr Mather : Not a bit ; sixty might do. It was decided that the figures as to the cost of the scheme be sent to the Local Government Board.

rALL RIGHTS RESEIT/ED.I BEST POLICY.

By W. PETT RIDGE,

Author of "A Son of the State," "Secretary to Baynes, M.P.," "A Clever Wife," "An Important Man," "Mord Daly," &c.

This story that James told me the other afternoon down at Edmonton—James has cost the Strand Board of Guardians, boy and man, a pretty penny —1 have taken the trouble to verify, and 1 am rather astonished to find that it has the virtue of comparative accuracy—so easy it is in this world to waver from a line of conduct.

"Honesty the best policy," read out James from an advertisement on the back of my newspaper. He took off his cap, rubbed his cropped white hair, and gave a short laugh. "Oh, yes. Twice ! Ought to be wrote up in letters of gold! Ought to be inscribed on every lamp-post! Honesty the best policy, indeed! For other people, perhaps." I guessed from what he did when he had removed his pipe from his mouth that he spoke ironically. "Loogere!" said James, pointing the stem of his pipe at me. "A ounce of fact's worth a ton of tvgument. I'll give you a case in point."

James having pulled off what he called a "koop" determined that the money thus acquired should take him into a society brighter and loftier than that which Ilaggerston affords. Wherefore, instead of going to a shop that advertised its misfits as though it were a matter for pride to have had so many efforts rejected, he went to a tailor who measured him, who arranged an impressive fall in the back for the frock coat ; selected with the touch of an artist the true, the one, the indisputable place for the two back buttons ; invented a double- breasted waistcoat that somehow made James appear quite slim, and furnished two quite reticent pairs of trousers. These and a silk hat at fifteen and six enabled James to stroll in at the Criterion bar withoht encountering frowns, to ask the bar- maids whether his friend, Sir William Something, had been in, to express perturbation on receiving a nqative reply; it also enabled him to sit languidly on the handle of his stick quite close to the steps of Pall Mall clubs. It was in this way that he met M. llenri Fasquelle, temporarily of Gerrard- street, and, indeed, never permanently at any one place of his own accord, and when each had dis- covered that the other was looking out for some- thing to turn up, they became good friends. Such good friends, indeed, that one Sunday night at a club in Old Compton-street (since, alas! raided and no more) M. Pasquelle made a proposal that as neither wanted to work for a living they should put thoughts together and thus secure a competency. "Explain yourself," said James. M. Fasquelle, though hampered by a foreign tongue and the near presence of other members, contrived to do so.

"Can I trust you ?" asked James. By the memory of his mother, M. Fasquelle said (and said it with tears), he could declare that in matters of this kind he was the very soul of honour. "Then 1 tell you what," said James, holding out his hand, "we'll have a dash at it." James tells me that to find the ordinary every- day mug in London is the easiest matter; to find a Iktog of a peculiar pattern is a task. Nevertheless, being something of a connoisseur in the article, he did a week later find one bearing the assumed name of Borster, a man possessing visiting-cards and a fox-head pin in his necktie, a meerschaum cigarette- holder, and other evidences of moving in a good circle. Borster had been in the City, but the City had found fault with him and had sent him to Holloway and subsequently to Wormwood Scrubbs, and he was ready•now to do almost anything "on the cross " as opposed to the correct line of conduct described as "on the straight." To him the scheme was explained under a bond of secrecy that nothing but an earthquake could break. Some- where in the West Central district (street not to be mentioned even to such a dear old chum as Borster) Russian bank-notes were being imitated with such artistic preciseness that only the specialist could detect the masquerade. They could be passed in Antwerp, in Berlin, in Paris—in Paris especially, because of the Franco-Russian understanding. For five hundred pounds Borster should have ten thousand pounds' worth. On Borster asking of James and M. Fasquelle the reason why they themselves did not take advantage of this astonishing opportunity for making a fortune, the two had to confess that they were so well known and so unfavourably known to the police that every act of theirs was being watched with unkind suspicion. Whilst Berster, on the contrarv----"

"Why, compared with us," said James, in a t:onfidential whisper, "compared with uu you're whiter than the drivelling snow !" Borster, with all the conceit of the amateur, was inclined at first to take this as a deliberate insult, but the great advantages of his intermediate position were strenuously pointed out in a foreign language by M. Fasciaelle and:in very had, forcible language by James, and after two hours of hard, continuous debate, Borster was compelled to admit that the occasion and all the circumstances were unique. "Now, I'm a strictly business man," said Borster, accepting a cigarette from M. Fasquelle, "and I should like this little affair to be run on strictly business lines. Where and when can I touch_these so-called notes " -

"Bruxelles," said M. Fasquella. "A week to-day," added James. "Why not in London?" "How many more times," demanded James, heatedly, "am Ito tell you that we are being watched by men who've got eyes like 'awks ?" "In London," said M. Pasquale, with a regret- ful wave of his cigarette, "it is impossible. In many respects a charming town "—here he kissed his hand to Old Compton-street—"it is in this regard im-poss-ible I " "Whereabouts in Brussels ?" asked Borster, after a pause. M. Fasquelle borrowed a pencil from James, wrote the address on the marble table, and rubbed it out immediately. "Rue-- ! " began Borster. The other two begged him in a whisper not to talk FO loudly. If any of these confounded detectives (who could not always be confounded) were to bear so much as a syllable, then would the game be up indeed. Easy to find the small tavern in question, said M. Fasquelle; it was but the throw of a stone from the Place ,Royale, and Brussels was just one of those quiet places where the police would be very unlikely to interfere. This was the great point, for should the police get wind of it then there was no other prospect but a dreary five years for one and all. Borster admitted this and promised that be, for his part, would seal his lips in regard to the matter. It was thereupon agreed that each should travel separately, one via Flushing, one via Ostend, and the other rid Calais, and the appointment, three o'clock in the afternoon of the following Wednesday, was agreed upon and ratified. James tells me that this was his only experience of Continental travel; that anybody can go across Channel who likes to do so, but for his part—never again I Looking back upon it all, he thinks he can see that there was low cunning somewhere in the arrangement that he should go by way of Flushing. James had once been to Clacton by steamer, but that had been mere Serpentine boating compared with this Tuesday night voyage. It will be under- stood, without the knowledge of details, that James eventually arrived at Brussels a limp, white-faced, forlorn person, a stranger in a strange land, with no further knowledge of the tongue than that con- tained in the words, "an revoir," which, taken alone and used on arriving in a foreign town, could not be considered as a sufficient outfit. He had, however, the name of the tavern written on a card, and a white-hatted coachman drove him there, and, encouraged by the thought that he was to return that evening with two hundred and fifty pounds in his pocket—for M. Fasquelle had insisted that the profits should be exactly halved and was quite obstinate on this point when James had suggested that something troiA and enn o thingextran should be f allowed to Fasauelle. i

GREAT SANKEY.

ALLEGED AROON.-At Widnes, on Thursday, James Gilbert Whitley,fa farm labourer, of t o fixed abode,was charged with setting fire to a haystack belonging to Thomas Owen, farmer, of Vickers Green Farm, Sankey, and doing damage to the amount of £lOO. Superintendent Foster said prisoner was seen' running away from the farm on Wednesday night by a bricklayer's apprentice named Stretch, who tried to stop him, but failed. The lad suspected that something was wrong, and on looking from whence prisoner bad run he noticed the stack on fire. Sergeant Smith stopped Whitley as he was making his way to Warrington. Prisoner was then out of breath and excited. At first be denied all knowledge of the fire, but on being charged with the offence he replied, " I am guilty of it." A remand was ordered, as prisoner will be brought up at Warrington, Sankey being out- side the Widnes district.

NO CONSUMPTION 20 YEARS HENCE.

"By the operation of the Insurance Act consumption will be stamped out in 15 or 20 years," said Mr. John Burns at Bradford on Monday night. During the last four months, he added, his department had approved of over 200 sanatoria.

DEMAND FOR MECHANICS IN ONTARlo.—information has been received from the Employers' Association of Waterloo County at Berlin, Ontario (Cnada), that there are excellent opportunities in that town for mechanics and tradesmen. Berlin, Ontario (Canada) has a great variety of manufacturing industries, such as rubber, furniture, shoe, shirt and collar, buttons, ;tanneries, felt boot factories, etc. The mechanics particularly required are cabinet makers, upholsternrs, rubber shoe ra.kers, tanners, shoe makers, and a number of girls, experienced or otherwise, for shirt, eollar and rubber irtories. Wages for men run from 8s to 108 per day ; girls, if they are experienced, VAR earn from 28s to £2 per week. Learners receive about 14s per week.

"Ah, my brother " cried M. Fasquelle. "At ast,_ you are arrived!"

"What there is left of me," growled James. "One moiety of you," declared Fasquelle wifh enthusiasm, "is worth the two of other men. good comrade always!" "Yes, well, don't go slapping me on the thoulder like that. I only feel like about sixpence- halfpenny in the pound." "Some wine?" "Wine be hanged! " said Jarnes,'l r I wan' something to drink."

“At throa hcrArs,” remarked Irarquelle, Wong James's arm and conducting him through the passage, "at three hours Borster will come to arrive."

James remembers the big square room where the meeting took place with its old-fashioned English sporting prints, its French billiard-taLle, its comic papers, and a view through the windows of a wall which would have been blank only that some ingenious artist had painted thereon some astonish- ing scenes of red-coated huntsmen taking rifle shots at foxes, of sportsmen preparing to aim at birds that were already falling to the ground. Fasquelle seemed on good terms with the proprietor*so fart as James could guess, and at any rate the proprietor furnished James with some excellent _Bass and crusty, filling ham sandwiches. At three o'clock to the minute Borster walked in. Borster also had made some change in his appearance and looked the kind of man who might have about him a halfpenny stamp, but was not likely to have a penny one. In point of fact, he had the English notes inside a letter-case in his hip pocket, and he produced them at cnce: he shewed them to James and to Fasquelle. "Now," said Borster, seriously, "it's understood .hat we mean business."

"Strict business," agreed James. Fasquelle tried to express his cordial acceptance of this suggestion, but could do it only by pantomime. "And we needn't waste time over it." "The sooner it's over," said James, humorously, "the quicker." "Then produce the bits of Russian paper," said Borster.

"My dear friend is up on top," explained Fasquelle, pointing upwards. "Second stage. Him you must not see. Give me your dear, charming Bank of England notes and in the space of two minutes I return with—"

"You go with him, James." To explain what followed James has to ask my assistance. I have to pretend to be James, James Flays the character of Fasquelle. I take James by the arm for three or four steps, going, as it were, out of the large room into a dim passage. ("That seat over there," says James, "is the entrance from the street.") James orders me to bold Fasquelle'a arm very securely, and this I do. Upstairs in this way to a room where Fasquelle, still held .rfectionately, gives three quick raps, and, after a pause, a fourth. Door opened half-way. A large bundle tied with blue tape handed out to Fasquelle. rue Bank of England notes handed in. A return Jewnstairs, I as James still holding the arm of Tanlea .as Fasquelle. . . . .

"Grip tight," whispers the stage manager. I hold him, as I think, securely, but not se 9 ecurely when he gives a sudden melodramatic start as to.prevent him from releasing himself. Pannell°, with a cry of "Heavens I The police!" throws the bundle out of the window, darts into the large room, and gives swift, hurried, generous warning to Borster. In another moment the three are escaping in various directions ; Borster wildly, without knowing where he is going, out into the. oorridor where the sporting subjects are painted, Samos butting the innffensive arrival out of the way and making a mad dash out into the street and away down the hill of the Montagne de la Cour, Fasciaelle apparently vanishing into space. James, linding his way by accidw t more than design, runs tip against the Gare tin Nord, and hiding himself in the waiting-room takes the _ train back to, London. • ....... .

"And what," I ask of James when he has- mopped the inside of his soft black hat and has regained something like calm after the excitement f this description, "what was the end of the. -Hair ? "

War Victims Fund.

Sir,—The Society of Friends have opened a fund for the help of the non-combatants on ti.th sides in the Balkan War. A woman friend resident in Constantinople will help in the distribution ; also two friends are on their way out (at our expense) to help among the Bulgarian villages. The idea of this fund is to help those who are not fighting, but have been made homeless and destitute through the war. Either of the undersigned will be glad to receive and acknowledge sums sent for this Fund. E. MADELINE BROWN, Penketh School. HELEN N. RANSOME, Shirley, Hill Chile.

Town Hall Ward Election.

Sir,—l am not desirous of entering into any con- troversy which would in the slightest degree create cleavage or produce bi:terness of feeling, but as Chairman of Town Hall Ward Liberal Committee I feel bound to reply to the remarks of your corres- pondent a " Clubman" in your issue of November 9th. Had he really been familiar with all that had taken place prior to the decision of Mr. Isaac to contest the Ward in the Liberal interest he would have known, that all the claims which he makes for Mr. Delaney and probably more, had been conceded and recognized by the Town Hall Ward Liberal Committee. This I think would be endorsed by Mr. Delaney himself. All other assumptions ar d Tresumptions he, "Clubman," makes therefore fall to the ground. Had Mr Isaac fought to a finish the whole of the Liberal Committee would have given him ungraging and loyal support no matter what their opinions may have been as to the result, but he decided differently and we respect his judgment. I have no quarrel with the prospective intentions of insurance men. What I know of them satisfies me that they are the equals of any other body of men, but I would suggest, if they ccntemplate attacking a position held by any man of the mental calibre, and personal respect with which Mr Jenkins is held, they bad better not be so dilatory in the future as they were in the past.—l am yours very respectfully.

===== 0002601_19121116_art0148.txt | included=true | included =====
PATRICK O'BRIEN.

123, Wilderspool-road, Warricgton November 12, 1912.

The Rights of Animals. Sir,— A s the honorary secretary of the local branch of the Research Defence Society, allow me to make a few comments on your report of the meeting of the Anti-vivisection Society to which you devoted con- siderable space in your last issue. This is especially necessary as the meeting was held under the presid- ency of a medical Mayor. As reported by you the only relevant remark made by Dr. Joseph referred to the rights of animals. I suppoe he meant domesti- cated animals, the great mass of whom are called to life by the will of man for special purposes as for work, food, sport, and experiment. Do Dr. Joseph and his anti-vivisection friends seriously contend that the horse may be bred for work, the cow for milk and the pheasant for sport, while the guinea-pig must not be bred to test milk for the tubercle bacillus? Looking at the question in this way it seems to me that the humble guinea-pig performs a higher function than any in helping to alleviate human misery—and 11,1 only human misery but animal misery as well, for the prevention of tuberculosis in animals is only second in importance to its prevention in man. A little reflection will show that any advance in our knowledge of disease or of the working processes of normal life benefits our d, mesticated animals no less than man.lt is their right as well as Ours that this know ledge should advance further. The Research Defence Society was founded four years ago to defend the experimental method in medical and biological science against the attacks and misrepresentations of the anti- vivisectionists. It has a further duty in educating the general public in the meth( ds and results of these experiments and so justifying their continuance. As there is considerable ignoi ance on the part of the public with regard to animal experimentation let me state that no such experiments are carried out without a license from the Home Office, and with some excep- tions these must be carried out at a registered place where they can be inspected. In 1901 there were only 423 licensees in England and Scotland, and 96 5 per cent of the experiments were simple inoculations chiefly performed on behalf c f public bodies as tests for tuberculosis, sera, and vaccine or for the testing and standardisation of drugs. No cutting operation more severe than the lancing of a vein is ever performed without an anmsthetic. The public will thus see that the work is carried on by comparatively few persons and under great restrictions. -

If the anti-vivisectionists have their way this work will be further hampered and possibly abolished. The medical sciences would then have to fall back on clinical observations and post-mortem findings—a merely superficial view of nature's grim experiments. In the interests of scientific progress with which is bound the welfare of man and the domesticated animals, I think the general public should be thankful that few medical men support the anti-vivisectionist cause.

If any of your readers are interested in the work of the Research Defence Scciety I shall be pleased to supply them with literature bearing on the subject.— Yours faithfully,

DIVORCE REFORM.

REPORT OF THE ROYAL COMMISSION.

EQUAL LAWS FOR MEN AND WOMEN.

Important alterations in the law affecting divorce in England and Wales are recommended by the long awaited report of the Royai Commission, which was published on Monday nigl,t as a Blue Book. The following are recommended as new grounds for dissolving marriage :

Adultery. Wilful desertion for three years and upwards. Cruelty. Incurable insanity after five years' confinement. Habitual drunkenness, found incurable after three years from first order. Imprisonment under commuted death sentence. Majority and mfnority reports are presented. The majority report was signed on November 2 by Lord Gorell (chairman), Lady Frances Balfour, Mr Thomas Burt, Lord Guthrie, Sir Frederick Treves, Br H Tindal Atkinson, Mrs May Tennant, Mr Edgar Brierley (stipendiary magistrate at Manchester), and Mr J A Spender. The signatures of Mr Tindal Atkinson, Mrs Tennant and Mr Spender were subject to various notes. _ _ .

The minority report is signed by the Archbishop of York, Sir W R Anson and Sir L T Dibdio.

In the majority report the Commis,inners summarise their recommendations under the following heads : _

1. The decentralisation of Fittings for the hearirg of divorce and matrimonial cases to an extent sufficient to enable persons of limited means to have their cases heard by the High Court locally. 2. The abolition of the powers of Courts of Summary Jurisdiction to make orders for the permanent separation of married persons and the introduction of amendments with regard to their powers. prccedure ad practice. 3. The placing of men and women on an equal footing with regard to grounds for divorce. 4. The addition of five grounds for divorce which are generally recognised as, in fact, putting an end to married life.

5. The addition of grounds for obtaining decrees of nullity of marriage in certain cases of unfitness for marriage. 6. The introduction of other amendments of the present law, procedure, and practice in a number of details.

7. The making of certain provisions with regard to the publication of reports of divorce and matri- monial cases.

S. The extension of the protectinl clauses in the Act of 1857 with regard to the position of the clergy of the Church of England if the further grounds for divorce above recommended be added.

LOCAL OPINIONS.

THE RECTOR. "MARRIAGE IS INDISSOLUBLE."

Sir,— According to the Law of Christ marriage is indissoluble. Death alone dissolves the marriage bond. Separation is possibly permissible for one cause, and of e only,—unfaitbfulness, but this does not justify re-marriage.—Yours truly, .

FREDERICK W. WILLIS. The Rectory, Warrinpton, November 14th, 1912. REV. DR. MELLOR

"MARRIAGE A CIVIL CONTRACT."

Sir,—With refererce to the report cf the Divorce Commission, I have little to say excepting that I heartily approve the recommendations contained in the majority report, and have little sympathy with the objections of the minority. Marriage is a civil contract, a social arrangement ; its religious significance and sanctions are purely individual matters. Untold harm has been wrought by the idea that Almighty God is concerned in the binding together Of two people who are utterly unhappy in the union. A marriage from which mutual respect and affection have departed is just a mockery, and the sooner ended, the better. Especially glad am I to, see the iniquitous system of "separation orders," not giving dissolution of marriage, so strongly condemned. It is something also that women should be put on an equality with man in this matter, and that divorce should be no longer a luxury of the rich. I welcome the Majority Report. STANLEY A. MELLO R,

===== 0002601_19121116_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
Cairo-street Chapel, Warrington. FATHER WILSON.

THE STATE'S INTRUSION INTO RELIGIOUS SANCTUARY.

Sir,—As a Catholic I hold of course that Christian marriage is absolutely indissoluble ; and that it is so not by any human legislation that may be varied or abrogated, but by the Divine unalterable institutim of Christ Himself. The very idea of divorce is abhorrent to me. In my opinion it can lead only to evils worse than those it is intended to remedy. The Divorce Act of 1875,80 vehemently opposed by Mr. Gladstone, was a most deplorable step by which the State intruded into that religions sanctury where alone Christian marriage had hitherto been dealt with and controlled. The further natural, and I fear inevitable, results of "that step are foreshadowed in the Divorce Report. I am convinced that ncthing but evil can come of the State's further tampering with the integrity and indissolubility cf Christian Marriage, which is indeed the only firm foundation of a Christian State. I most earnestly hope that the suggestions to extend or multiply the grounds for divorce will never be adopted by our legislature The results of easy divorce in the United States should surely be a warning to us. Whatever cases of hardship there may be that now stir the unthinking emotions of the compassionate, cases of worse misery and of greater wrong will be indefinitely multiplied by extended facilities of divorce. • Unquestionably the chief reason why matrimonial misery is so frequent is this :—That marriage is so little looked upon as a matter of religion and so much withdrawn from its influence, that consequently it is entered into with such levity, such want of prudence in choke, with such little appreciation of its responsibilities, so !ight a consideration of its diffi- culties, so small a regard for its indissoluble character. This evil will be greatly aggravated by the knowledge that divorce may be obtained more easily and for many reasons. When persons realise that marriage is a union to be ended only with life, then only will they enter into it with due carefulness and prudence. Then only will they set themselves to exercise that self-restraint and mutual forbearance which will minimise and overcome those uprisings of disturbing elements which from time to time will inevitably occur. These on the other hand, if the way of escape is seen to be open, will be allowed to grow in frequency and violence till they become intolerable. The Report and any subsequent legislation arising from it may cause the greatest apprehension and regret to Catholics for their country's sake ; but it cannot cause them any perplexity nor change in their convictions, nor departure from their present practice. Here I might end, but it may ba p-rmissible to add that if there is a large portion of the people who think easy divorce is wise and salutary and desire it, and unfortunately insist on the State's interfering and legislating in accordance with their wishes, then it seems to me that in consistency and in equity there must be afforded the same grounds of divorce and facilities that are equal for women and fur men, for poor and for rich. NEVILLE WILSON. St Mary's Church, Buttermarket-street.

FOUR GENERATIONS.

FOUR GENERATIONS OF A WARRINGTON FAMILY WHICEI WAS ASSOCIATED WITH THE EARLY DAYS OF THE RYLANDS' IN WARRINGTON.

The above photograph represents four generations of an old Warrington family. In the centre is seated Mrs Wilkinson,of Morris Court,who last week completed her eightieth year, and Who is probably the oldest resident in the Church-street neighbourhood. The group includes a daughter of Mrs. Wilkinson—Mrs Cottam, 10, Elaine-street, a granddaughter, Mrs Parr, Orford-lane, and a great grand- daughter, Doris Parr. Mrs. Wilkinson was born within a stone's throw of the Parish Church and all her life has been spent in Church-street or the immediate vicinity. For fifty-six years she lived in a house at the top of Morris Court. Her father, the late Richard Boscow, was apprenticed to Messrs. Rylands when that great concern was in its infancy. A representative of the "Examiner " had the,. privilege on Thursday of inspecting his indentures which were dated October 6th, 1825. at,d were signed by John Rylands and Nathaniel Greening. They set forth that the apprentice was to be instructed in "the art,. trade and business of a wire worker" The late Mr. John .Rylands was the grandfather of Mr. W. Peter Rylands and the relations existing between the heads of the modest enterprise of those days and the little band of employees must have been very intimate, for Mr. Boscow was wont to recall with some pride that he had on occasion carried the late Mr. Peter Rylands (then a promising school boy and in later years M.P. for Burnley) on his back to the Grammar School. Notwithstanding the weight of years, Mrs. Wilkinson is still fairly active. About two years ago she was knocked down by a motor car, while crossing Church-street, and was for some weeks a patient in the Infirmary. At her age such an accident was naturally a severe shock, but her constitution was strong enough to withstand it. Mrs. Wilkinson is now in enjoyment of the old age pension. Her surviving descendants are one son and four daughters, 23 grandchildren and 14 great-grandchildren.
