MR. P. M. OLIVER. A TILT AT THE £1,03D DUAL DINERS.

TARIFF REFORM "ARITHMETIC."

Mr. P. M. Oliver, the prospective Liberril candidate for the Kuutaford Division, Hddi eased a meeting miner the auspices of the Stockton Heath Liberal Association in the Hall Cafe, on Nlonday evening. A striking feature of his speech was his reference to those stalwarts of tariff reform who while, objetting to con- tribute under the National Insurance Act, are willing to spend £l,OOO per.head ft tr the privilege of dining with the Duke of Westminster. Mr Oliver's gifts of lucid and convincing expositton are now recognized througlmut the division and the Liberal Association has been fortunate in securing a candidate who unites demccratic convictions with the ability to present the progressive case in a manner which cannot fail to enlist the interest (if thoughtful politicians. The meeting on Monday was dis- tinctly encouraging, the hall being crowded with an enthusiastic audience. Mr J Longland presided and, in addition to Mr Oliver, there mete on the platform Mr W G Fallon, ex candidate for Mid Cork, and Mr George Wallington. A letter was read from Councillor H Roberts, who was announced to speak, regretting that he had been uttexnectedly called to London on business connected with the administra- tion of the Insurance Act. It would have been a pleasure to him to join with fellow-Liberals in support- ing the Government in their determination to confer upon the Irish people that great measure of justice towards the accomplishment of which Mr Gladstone devoted the last years of his great life. The CHAIRMAN said Home ttule was bound to come. (Applause.) He had no doubt that, either with or with- out alterations of small details %blob did not affect the principle one atom, the Home Rule Bill would Ise passed The same remark applied to the Welsh Church Bill. He for one would he prepared to be extremely

GENEROUS ON THE QUESTION OE DISE-NDOWMENT but the question of Disestablishment was bound to be settled in accordance with the wishes of the Welsh people. The Franchise Bill would make a considerable difference to all constituencies and do away with very much of the injustice which had hitherto confronted the man who wanted to obtpda a vote. (Applause.) In many cases electors had been aisfranchised for about two years and such a grievance ought to be remedied, (A pp lause.) Mr FALLON then delivered a pow etful and eloquent address on Home Rule. Referring to the Government defeat in a "snap " division, he pointed out that it was simply the

OUTCOME OF A LITTLE SCHEME which had been hatched for some days, to his certain knowledge, by the members of the Opposition If ttell Unionists, after having spent such an uninteresting time for many months and with a similar prospect for some years to come, derived much joy troru such a triumph, it would be very selfish of Liberals to wish to deprive them of it. (Laughter.) Mr Fallon emphasised the democratic character of the Home Hale movement and said its great object was to give the working people of Ireland a chance of living in their own country. He showed how the land had been devastated and depopulated through mis-rule, and said the wonder was not that there was little money in Ireland, but that there was any money or prosperity at all. Under native auspices Ireland was capable of developing into an exceedingly rich and prosperous country, for it was a land of rich delds, fair valleys and vast mineral resources which, however, had been left undeveloped because there was no sympathetic Govern- ment to help the people to carry on industries. It was on the financial aspect that the "snap " division had taken place. Anti-Home Rulers would tell them that Mr. Asquith and his wicked Government were conferring upon Ireland A NEW CDT OF .£2,00,00 A YEAR. It was 112 years since the Act of Union was passed and for 109 years the Irish people not only paid their own way, so far as the cost of Government in Ireland was concerned, but also contributed their share to the Imperial funds. In addition, a Royal Commission which reported in 1904 discovered that Ireland had been over- taxed for over 100 years to the extent of between £2,750,000 and £3,500,000 per annum. As Lord Macdonald had pointed out, England at the present moment actually owed Ireland £325,000,000. He (the speaker) did uot suppose they would ever reaeke a cheque for that amount. (Laughter.) Mr Fallon showed that of late years Ireland had been unable to keep pace with the strain and England had this year to pay some- thing to the upkeep of that country. If Unionists insisted on having their wpy the debt would increase as years went on. The only solutlim was to allow Ireland to make a fresh start in life. If the pr.sent impossible position were persisted in British finance would become annually more entangled. Mr Fallon warmly controverted the argument that Ulster was the prosperous and wealthy province of Ireland, and made special allusion to the position of Belfast. The income tax yield per head in Dublin was £l9 14s. whereas in Belfast it was only .£lO 12s. Lord Londonderry and the Earl of Erne, about whom nobody ever heard anything, the Duke of Abereorn—that great, democratic leader—(laughter)—Sir Edward Carson and Captain Craig were always speaking of the Prosperity of Belfast. What did they mean by prosperity? was Belfast a prosperous city when the average wage of the linen worker was only 12s. 30. per head? Was Belfast, in the opinion of Lord Londonderry, a prosperous city when girls had to work 300 dots into a pocket handker- chief, at 6d. a day or Es. per week and keep body and soul together? WAS BELFAST PROSPEROUS when women had to make a dozen men's shirts for Is. 10d when the rate of infant mortality was worse than in any other city in the British Isles, when women had to work the four corners of a sheet for lid. a dozen and linen pillow covers at a dozen and maintain life on that Pittance? If that was Lord Londonderry's, the Duke of Abercorn% or Sir Edo ard Carson's notion of a prosperous city, he, as an Irish democrat, begged to differ from thorn on the meaning of prosperity. (Applause.) Belfast was not a prosperous city and Ireland was not a prosperous country, but they would be when Ireland bad a native sympathetic Parliament of her own. (Applause).

continued Mr. Oliver, had undoubtedly shown the power and the rejuvenation which could be given to a race by realising its nationality. In 1877 Bulgaria was one of the poorest countries on the face of the globe. For over 400 years she had suffered tyranny, and had been crushed down by the Turks ever since they came into Europe. Now, in a generation's time, we saw the Bulgarian arms sweeping like a sword of God across the fields of Thrace, and we must realize that that had been done largely by allowing Bulgaria to manage her 0,4 n affairs. her people, having been given freedom and liberty, bad made themselves men, and had forced themselves into the very forefront of the nations of tha world. TWO YEARS WE SHALL BE ABLE TO SMASH

PROTECTION

once and for all. I have got my work planned out. After that I am going to do some other work, because believe my work, so far as defending Free Trade is concerned, will be finished within two years' time." (Applause.) The Conservative party had for a long time been bewailing the death of British prosperity, They had ordered the hearse and the coffin and had bought their black kid gloves. (Laughter.) But British prozperity, for some reason or other, did not seem to die. (Laughter.) They wanted a burial but they bad not got a corpse—a very important item. (Laughter.) He agreed that the prosperity of a country was not to be measured entirely by exports and imports. They had to go deeper and see how the people lived, examine their weekly budgets and look at the price of food. Nevertheless, in a country like England, it was impor- tant to look at our foreign trade. At present, as far as he could find, peof le had got just about as much work as they wanted and our foreign trade had never been approaching the prosperity that we found to-day. (Applause.) In the course of a speech at Oldham on Friday Mr 13, nar Law said : Now what is the position? If they (Free Traders) showed to us that our trade is increasing more rapidly under our system than is the trade of other countries under their systems, then I would say that is a strong argument. But suppose the reverse is true. Suppose that the extension of trade is smaller with us than it is in other countries with a different fiscal system. Do they still say it is better for us? In eight months of the present year our trade has increased to the extent of 99 millions. But in the same period that of Germany has increased to the extent of 41 millions, and in the United States to the extent of 76 millions. It is a funny argument that our fiscal system must be the best because our trade has increased by 29 millions while that of other nations has increased in one case nearly twice, and in the other case nearly three times as much as it has with us. (Hear, hear). He (Mr Oliver) wanted to treat Mr Boner Law with all respect but he thought it was necessary to verify his figures and so he turned to the official statistics issued by the Board of Trade. Mr 13,nar Law said the trade of the United States had increased by 76 millions. As a matter of fact it had only increased by 71 millions—but 5 millions was not much to those who could pay £,,0t0 for a dinner. (Laughter.) Still it showed what Mr Asquith bad called the " incurable sloppiness " of tariff reformers. In regard to German trade, Mr Boner Law had made a clear mistake, because he bad put it worse than it really was. As a matter of fact, It had increased to the extent of 50 millions. He (Mr Oliver) was not sorry when the trade of German or any other country increased • it would react upon us and help us sooner or (Applause.) English trade, according to Mr Boner Law, had increased by only 29 millions. One would expect that Mr Sonar Law, being the leader of the great patriotic party, would be specially anxious to put our figures as high as possible. The official statistics, however, showed that the increase of our trade was not 29 millions but 48 millions. (Applause.) If such mistakes were made by Mr Boner Law in a carefully prepared speech, one wondered what statements must be made at street corners by irresponsible tariff reform tlecturers. Still, s. on according to the official figures, Mr Boner Law's argu- ment would appear to hold good by the skin of its months of he me year hold wteheitehh, pbutlsy he ei havochad ftp apparently r trade overlookedearly the i sar. coalye tritte the other hand the railway strike would no doubt injure our rd texrs'ratys tinhajtulya duringnd the egnursstt onfinlaesmt which included the railway strike and the coal strike, our trade had increased by 68 millions as compared with last year and he would lay anything Mr Boner Law liked that by the end of the year we would have beaten Germany absolutely hollow in our trade returns. TAKEN TO EATING BANQUETS.

(Laughter.) They were very fond of banquets. No doubt many of those present would remember the banquet at the Hotel Cecil five years ago when, Mr Boner Law said they only wanted two bad winters and tariff reform would come. (Laughter.) At the piesent time,if they paid down £l,OOO they could have dinner any day they liked with the Duke of Westminster (Laughter.) He gave that as a little bit of advice and no doubt one or two of those pre.ent would be ready and anxious to accept it. (Laughter.) At the last dinner there were 200 guests which, at £l,OOO each, should have meant £200,000. But there again tariff reform arithmetic seemed to be a bit wrong—(laughter) —and they only bagged £60,000. By holding numerous other dinners it was hoped to raise £1,C00,000 and he (the speaker) wished to emphasise that Free Traders would have to face as a big. solid fact, that huge sum which would be spent,somettow or other,before the next election. He dare say a large number of those who put down £l.OOO to dine with the Duke of Westminster .xere the same people who had been protesting against the insurance tax." He would like to remind them that £l,OOO would have paid the contributions of their scullery maid for 153 years. (Laughter and applause ) That was a significant thing. He did not believe those people paid their £l,OOO for the love of tariff reform. They paid it in order to sit with a Duke—that was the snobbery at the bottom of the Conservative party. If it was worth £l,OOO to dine with a Duke for one night, surely the health and security of their scullery maid was worth thr,epence a week. (Applause.) They were told that all the money raised at the banquets would go into an Imperial fund. " I have never been ashamed to declare." continued Mr (Liver," that, according to my lights, I am

===== 0002601_19121116_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
AN IMPERIALIST.

Of course, there are all sorts of Imperialism. One Imperialism is mere affectation—an extravagant love for Australians and Canadians and even ybody who lives a long waf off, coupled with an extrayagant hatred of Welsh, Irishmen and everybody who lives near to. Another rotten kind of Imperialism is the met- love of the big. Just as Barnum will show you the fattest woman in the world and your Welch stationmaster will show you the longest word in the world, so you can point to the biggest Empire. There is a better and liner sort of Imperialism, which is founded upon our colonising instinct, our love of adventure and our natural will to live. We believe our institutions are just and proper and we wish our traditions to be carried out. That sort of Imperialism will never be helped by putting a tax on food." (Applause.) fouching upon the Insurance Act, 'Mr. Oliver said it was now working smoothly. In some cases there had been a certain amount of vindictiveness and spite, but they had only sho 7in up the more brightly the loyalty of the vast majority of employers. (Applause). The Conservative party was pledged drastically to amend the Act—although nohony knew in what direction. If Monday's " snap " Division had been a real division and the Government had suffered a real defeat, he shuddered to think what might have happened to the Insurance Act. The only drastic, amendments proposed so far were those of the Insurance Tax Resistance League, which would wipe out the contributions of the employer and divide the burden betwesn the workman and the State. That Leagne had placed the Conservative party under an obligation by helping them to win by-elections and it would be a piece of shame and folly if, by any mischance, the Liberal Government were defeated. lie did no': believo it would be defaated ye'.,—not for two years. (Applause). They had ft great deal of work to get through and when the election came it would be fought not upon the issues which the Conservative party chose t) raise but upon the ground chosen by the Govern- ment. CONCERT AT THE PARR HALL,

The indebtedness of the music-loving public of Warring'on to Mr. J. P. Johnson, of Kendal, was further increased on Thursday evening by the privilege of hearing one of the most interesting of the many concerts promoted by him. It not infrequently happens that the strictly classical concert is too testricted in its appeal, but on this occasion, the programme, while uuquestionably high class through out, was notable for the variety imparted to the choice of items. Although the Parr Hall was by no means full, the attendance was a considerable improvement on the meagre support which has so frequently acted as a discouragement to ambitious musics! projects—a sign, let us hope, that public apathy in matters artistic is at last about to be dis- pelleo. There was one great disappointment ia store for the audience, their indulgence being claimed, on the ground of indisposition, on behalf of Miss Alys Bateman, who found it impossible to submit the whole of the, songs allotted to her on the programme. However, her interpretation of the great soprano aria from the opera " Louise " was sufficient to disclose the possession of a voice of fine range and flexibility. Miss Ratemen also took part in the " Miserere " scene from "Il Trovatore " but, u,ider the circumstances, failed to invest it with that dramatic intensity which it deatands. In this item the tenor part was sustained by Mr. Harold Wilde whose artistic singing both here and in a most enjoyable series of solos was one of the most delightful features of the evening. Purity of tone, excellent enunciation and moving expressiveness marked his rendering of several pleasing songs, but he was heard to the best advantage in that gem of Coleridge Taylor's " Onaway, awake ! " Mr. Stuart Edwards, besides being endowed with a robust, baritone voice, brings- individuality to bear on all his work and his songs were well chosen to afford interesting contrasts in mood and style. It seemed a far cry from the sombreness of "In the great unknown " to the rollicking, infections rhythm of "The floral dance" and Mr Edwards, while doing 'justice to both, abandoned himself so completely to the spirit of the latter composbion as quite to carry the audience with him. Tim reputation of Mr Johannes Wolff as one of the most gifted violinists of the day was fully maintained in 80103 which demon- strated his exceptional executant abilities and the temperament of the true artiste. We were particu- larly impressed with his playing in " Berceuse " (Wienawski) and " Morceau de Concert" (A. Duteil d'Ozanne), characterised as it was by exquisite delicacy and neatness of detail. To the list of notable pianists who have visited Warrington must now be added Mr Parlovitz whose brilliant technical equip- ment, allied with the emotional qualities nest summed up in the word soulfulness, make him one of the foremost interpreters of Chopin. His magical ' touch was most strikingly evidenced in passages of gossamer-like lightness, and in none of his pieces was there the slightest tendency to showiness or exaggerat tion. Miss Gertrude Wood displayed high elocutionary attainments in three delightful recitations " The thousandth man," "The bachelors' sale" and "The singing leaves" in which alternated the notes of inspiration, humour and tender sympathy. Miss Nellie Ganthony's sketches at the piano provided a distinct and much appreciated novelty. As a raconteur and mimic, this lath must have few equals, and her, description of "That garden party " and "The 'bus ride " abounding in genuine fun, were so originally conceived and cleverly given, that the attention of the audience never flagged for a moment and the hall continually rang with merriment while she occupied the platform. The pianoforte accompaniments were perfectly aud unobtrusively played by Mr Farlovitz.

THE SALE OF A BUSINESS.

Warrington Action at the Assizes.

At the Liverpool Assizes, on Wednesday, before Mr Justice Rowlatt, Edith Heesom, Rylands-street, War- rington, sued Robert Lee, Manchester-road, Warring- ton, for 190, being the amount alleged by the plaintiff to be due to her from the proceeds of the sale of goods and a lease under a partnership. Mr Lindon Riley (inNtructed by Mr Pennington Riley) appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr Kennedy and Mr Chappell (instructed by Messrs Robert Davies and Co.) represented the defendant! Mr Riley mentioned that the defendant was the brother-in-law of the plaintiff. Mrs Heesom and her sister, from ISS7 to 1900, -kept a Berlin wool shop in Sankey-street, Warrington, and on the dissolution of the partnership the stock and lease were sold and the money handed over in trust to Mr Lee. The plaintiff had not yet received her httlf of the proceeds from the defendant. She had been paid a few inconsiderable sums which brought the action outside the Statute of Limitation.

The plaintiff gave evidence, and in cross-examina- tion said that a coolness sprang up between herself and her sister because the latter objected to witneas marrying a mau without money. Her sister died in 1906, and when witness went to the defendant for the money she claimed,he said that she had had her share. On the conclusion of evidence for the plaintiff His Lordship intimated that on the evidence he could not find that the defendant had received the money upon trust, and he had therefore no caso to answer.

Judgment was entered for the defendant with costs.

STRANGE DISCOVERY AT BOURNEMOUTH. When the police entered a house in Fenton-road, vilest Southhourne, Bournemouth, they discovered the only occupant, Mrs Jessie Scott, dead. She was sitting at a table, her head on her arms, and before her a copy of a local daily paper for October 25. •

St. Helens are engaged at Rochdale in 'the semi-fi,,al for the Lancashire Cup, and their match with Warrington has been re-arranged for Thursday ,next at St. Helens at 2.45. -- Another postponed match is the one with Hull at 'the Boulevard, down for Saturday next. As Hull will 'be playing the Y.,rkshire Cup Final on that date, Warrington have arranged to travel to Hull on April :sth—unless they are in the semi-final for the Cup! Pure Indian Tea commands the markets of the world. India exports in a year 249,000,000 lbs. of well manufactured tea of which the United Kingdom consumes 169,000,000 lbs. So keen is the desire for the delicious teas from India that the de- mand has out-run the supply, and the dealer is tempted to keep down the price of his blends by substituting inferior teas from foreign countries for the genuine Indian product. These substitutes have neither the richness nor the purity of Indian tea. It is therefore desirable for the purchaser to obtain a guarantee that the tea sold her is genuine Indian. Considerations of economy, of health, and of comfort, unite in recommend- ing this course.
